Prefer a new field rather than changing its type; the proto3 guide permits some wire-compatible type changes, but their value ranges and application semantics still need review.","reason":"Official proto3 allows some compatible type changes; conservative rule is advice"}
- {"file":"api-grpc-streaming-selection.md","before":"Netflix, Shopify and GitHub all expose GraphQL to UIs; Google, Square and many others run gRPC internally; nearly everyone offers REST to third parties because every language and tool already speaks it.","after":"These styles can coexist without requiring every consumer to use the same interface. The decision depends on the documented client contract and measured workload.","reason":"Remove unsupported aggregate vendor adoption claim"}
- {"file":"api-grpc-streaming-selection.questions.json","question":"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q5","before":{"id":"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q5","level":"advanced","type":"single","prompt":"You migrate internal service calls from REST/JSON to gRPC. Behind an L4 (TCP) load balancer with 10 backends, you notice one backend at 90% CPU and the others nearly idle. What's the most likely cause?","options":[{"text":"Protobuf is slower to parse than JSON","explanation":"Parsing performance depends on workload and implementation. It would not by itself explain why an L4 balancer pins a high-volume connection to one backend."},{"text":"gRPC multiplexes many calls over one long-lived HTTP/2 connection, and an L4 balancer only balances connections, so all of a client's requests stick to one backend","explanation":"Correct. An L4 balancer picks a backend once per TCP connection. HTTP/1.1 clients open many short connections, which spread naturally; a gRPC client may open one connection and send thousands of requests per second down it. Use an L7 (request-aware) proxy such as Envoy, or client-side load balancing."},{"text":"gRPC deadlines are concentrating retries on one server","explanation":"Deadlines cause calls to fail fast when time runs out. They don't steer traffic to a particular backend."},{"text":"The backend is running a different protobuf version","explanation":"A version mismatch could cause decoding issues, but it wouldn't attract a disproportionate share of traffic to one host."}],"answer":1,"workedExample":"1. L4 balancing decides at **TCP connect** time.\n2. With HTTP/1.1, clients typically open a pool of connections and recycle them, so load spreads.\n3. gRPC/HTTP/2 multiplexes: one connection carries hundreds of concurrent streams and lives for hours.\n4. If a few large callers each hold one connection, those connections land on one or two backends, and stay there.\n\nFixes:\n- L7 proxy (Envoy, Linkerd, gRPC-aware cloud LBs) that balances per **request**.\n- Client-side load balancing: the client resolves all backend addresses and round-robins calls.\n- Set a max connection age so connections are periodically re-established.","link":{"title":"gRPC: Performance best practices","url":"https://grpc.io/docs/guides/performance/"}},"after":{"id":"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q5","level":"advanced","type":"single","prompt":"You migrate internal service calls from REST/JSON to gRPC. Behind an L4 (TCP) load balancer with 10 backends, you notice one backend at 90% CPU and the others nearly idle. What's the most likely cause?","options":[{"text":"Protobuf is slower to parse than JSON","explanation":"Parsing performance depends on workload and implementation. It would not by itself explain why an L4 balancer pins a high-volume connection to one backend."},{"text":"gRPC multiplexes many calls over one long-lived HTTP/2 connection, and an L4 balancer only balances connections, so all of a client's requests stick to one backend","explanation":"Correct under the stated connection-level balancer. Each TCP connection is assigned to a backend; a long-lived gRPC connection can carry many calls to that backend. HTTP/1.1 also supports persistent pooled connections, so it does not guarantee an even distribution. L7 or client-side balancing can distribute calls."},{"text":"gRPC deadlines are concentrating retries on one server","explanation":"Deadlines cause calls to fail fast when time runs out. They don't steer traffic to a particular backend."},{"text":"The backend is running a different protobuf version","explanation":"A version mismatch could cause decoding issues, but it wouldn't attract a disproportionate share of traffic to one host."}],"answer":1,"workedExample":"1. L4 balancing decides at **TCP connect** time.\n2. With HTTP/1.1, clients typically open a pool of connections and recycle them, so load spreads.\n3. gRPC/HTTP/2 multiplexes: one connection carries hundreds of concurrent streams and lives for hours.\n4. If a few large callers each hold one connection, those connections land on one or two backends, and stay there.\n\nFixes:\n- L7 proxy (Envoy, Linkerd, gRPC-aware cloud LBs) that balances per **request**.\n- Client-side load balancing: the client resolves all backend addresses and round-robins calls.\n- Set a max connection age so connections are periodically re-established.","link":{"title":"gRPC: Performance best practices","url":"https://grpc.io/docs/guides/performance/"}},"reason":"HTTP/1.1 does not inherently spread traffic through short connections"}
- {"file":"api-grpc-streaming-selection.questions.json","question":"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q7","before":{"id":"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q7","level":"advanced","type":"single","prompt":"A ride-hailing team explicitly wants (a) resource-oriented HTTP endpoints callable with ordinary tools for partners, (b) UI-specific response composition, and (c) generated protobuf RPC stubs and streaming internally. Which combination most directly fits these chosen constraints?","options":[{"text":"gRPC for all three, for consistency","explanation":"gRPC is poor for (a): partners would need protobuf tooling and couldn't use plain HTTP clients. It's also awkward for the mobile web parts of (b) without gRPC-Web proxies."},{"text":"(a) REST, (b) GraphQL or a BFF, (c) gRPC","explanation":"Correct. Partners want something any language can call with documented, cacheable endpoints (REST + OpenAPI). The app benefits from fetching exactly the shape each screen needs (GraphQL or a BFF). High-volume internal calls benefit from protobuf's small payloads, generated stubs, deadlines and HTTP/2 multiplexing (gRPC)."},{"text":"(a) GraphQL, (b) REST, (c) REST","explanation":"Public GraphQL is possible but raises query-cost and caching challenges for third parties, while internal REST/JSON at 50k calls/s leaves performance on the table. This reverses the natural fit."},{"text":"REST for all three, because it's the most widely understood","explanation":"REST works everywhere, but the mobile app would suffer from chatty, over-fetching calls, and internal traffic would pay JSON parsing overhead and lack deadlines and streaming."}],"answer":1,"workedExample":"Match each consumer to the style's strengths:\n\n| Consumer | Needs | Fit |\n|---|---|---|\n| Partners | Easy, stable, any language | REST |\n| Rider app | Few round trips, varied shapes | GraphQL/BFF |\n| Internal | Low latency, typed, high QPS | gRPC |\n\nThe combination follows the stated interface requirements. Actual payload savings, CPU cost and latency need workload-specific measurement; the earlier 60% byte saving is not universal.","link":{"title":"gRPC: Core concepts","url":"https://grpc.io/docs/what-is-grpc/core-concepts/"}},"after":{"id":"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q7","level":"advanced","type":"single","prompt":"A ride-hailing team explicitly wants (a) resource-oriented HTTP endpoints callable with ordinary tools for partners, (b) UI-specific response composition, and (c) generated protobuf RPC stubs and streaming internally. Which combination most directly fits these chosen constraints?","options":[{"text":"gRPC for all three, for consistency","explanation":"gRPC is poor for (a): partners would need protobuf tooling and couldn't use plain HTTP clients. It's also awkward for the mobile web parts of (b) without gRPC-Web proxies."},{"text":"(a) REST, (b) GraphQL or a BFF, (c) gRPC","explanation":"Correct. Partners want something any language can call with documented, cacheable endpoints (REST + OpenAPI). The app benefits from fetching exactly the shape each screen needs (GraphQL or a BFF). High-volume internal calls benefit from protobuf's small payloads, generated stubs, deadlines and HTTP/2 multiplexing (gRPC)."},{"text":"(a) GraphQL, (b) REST, (c) REST","explanation":"Public GraphQL and internal REST are valid designs, but this option does not provide the explicitly requested internal protobuf RPC stubs and gRPC streaming contract. No universal CPU or latency comparison follows from a request rate alone."},{"text":"REST for all three, because it's the most widely understood","explanation":"REST can use compound responses, streaming HTTP and application deadlines. This option still does not meet the chosen requirement for generated protobuf RPC stubs and gRPC streaming internally."}],"answer":1,"workedExample":"Match each consumer to the style's strengths:\n\n| Consumer | Needs | Fit |\n|---|---|---|\n| Partners | Easy, stable, any language | REST |\n| Rider app | Few round trips, varied shapes | GraphQL/BFF |\n| Internal | Low latency, typed, high QPS | gRPC |\n\nThe combination follows the stated interface requirements. Actual payload savings, CPU cost and latency need workload-specific measurement; the earlier 60% byte saving is not universal.","link":{"title":"gRPC: Core concepts","url":"https://grpc.io/docs/what-is-grpc/core-concepts/"}},"reason":"REST can stream and use deadlines; no unsupported 50k/s performance claim"}
- {"file":"api-idempotency-atomic-recovery.md","before":"Recovery needs either a timeout plus a check with the downstream system (\"did charge X happen?\"), or making step 2 itself idempotent by passing the same key downstream.","after":"Recovery needs either authoritative downstream reconciliation that accounts for still-pending work, or making step 2 itself idempotent under the downstream's key scope and retention contract. A timeout or an eventually consistent not-found result alone does not authorise another charge.","reason":"Close ambiguous remote status/crash window"}
- {"file":"api-idempotency-atomic-recovery.questions.json","question":"api-idempotency-q9","before":{"id":"api-idempotency-q9","level":"advanced","type":"multi","prompt":"Your server claims key K (`status='started'`), charges the card, then crashes **before** recording the result. The row sits in `started` for ever and retries get 409. Which recovery strategies are sound? Select all that apply.","options":[{"text":"After a timeout, ask the card network whether a charge for K exists, then mark the row done (with that result) or let the retry proceed","explanation":"Sound only when the downstream offers authoritative status for the operation key and its contract rules out a still-pending operation later succeeding. A transient not-found response from an eventually consistent lookup is not permission to charge again."},{"text":"Forward K (or a key derived from it) to the card network, so re-running the charge step is itself idempotent","explanation":"Correct. If the downstream call deduplicates on the same key, the retry can simply resume and redo the step without a second charge. This is the atomic-phases approach."},{"text":"Delete stuck `started` rows after 30 seconds so the next retry starts fresh","explanation":"Unsafe. The card may already have been charged; starting fresh without any downstream deduplication charges it again."},{"text":"Mark stuck rows `done` with a `201` after a timeout","explanation":"Unsafe. You'd be telling the client 'paid' without knowing whether the charge happened. If it didn't, you've shipped goods for free."},{"text":"Use durable local recovery points together with downstream idempotency or authoritative reconciliation for the external charge step","explanation":"Correct with the added external-step protection. Local recovery points cannot by themselves close the crash window between a remote charge and recording its result; downstream deduplication or reconciliation is still necessary."}],"answer":[0,1,4],"workedExample":"1. A crash after the remote charge but before a local result write creates an unknown outcome.\n2. Retry the remote step with the same key only within a downstream idempotency contract, or reconcile using authoritative operation status that accounts for pending work.\n3. Local recovery points let the workflow resume, but do not atomically commit a remote charge and local database record. Combine them with step 2.\n4. Deleting the claim or inventing success after a timeout guesses the result and is unsafe.","link":{"title":"Brandur Leach: Implementing Stripe-like idempotency keys in Postgres","url":"https://brandur.org/idempotency-keys"}},"after":{"id":"api-idempotency-q9","level":"advanced","type":"multi","prompt":"Your server claims key K (`status='started'`), charges the card, then crashes **before** recording the result. The row sits in `started` for ever and retries get 409. Which recovery strategies are sound? Select all that apply.","options":[{"text":"After a timeout, reconcile K through an authoritative downstream status contract that rules out a pending charge later succeeding before a retry is allowed","explanation":"Sound only when the downstream offers authoritative status for the operation key and its contract rules out a still-pending operation later succeeding. A transient not-found response from an eventually consistent lookup is not permission to charge again."},{"text":"Forward K (or a key derived from it) to the card network, so re-running the charge step is itself idempotent","explanation":"Correct. If the downstream call deduplicates on the same key, the retry can simply resume and redo the step without a second charge. This is the atomic-phases approach."},{"text":"Delete stuck `started` rows after 30 seconds so the next retry starts fresh","explanation":"Unsafe. The card may already have been charged; starting fresh without any downstream deduplication charges it again."},{"text":"Mark stuck rows `done` with a `201` after a timeout","explanation":"Unsafe. You'd be telling the client 'paid' without knowing whether the charge happened. If it didn't, you've shipped goods for free."},{"text":"Use durable local recovery points together with downstream idempotency or authoritative reconciliation for the external charge step","explanation":"Correct with the added external-step protection. Local recovery points cannot by themselves close the crash window between a remote charge and recording its result; downstream deduplication or reconciliation is still necessary."}],"answer":[0,1,4],"workedExample":"1. A crash after the remote charge but before a local result write creates an unknown outcome.\n2. Retry the remote step with the same key only within a downstream idempotency contract, or reconcile using authoritative operation status that accounts for pending work.\n3. Local recovery points let the workflow resume, but do not atomically commit a remote charge and local database record. Combine them with step 2.\n4. Deleting the claim or inventing success after a timeout guesses the result and is unsafe.","link":{"title":"Brandur Leach: Implementing Stripe-like idempotency keys in Postgres","url":"https://brandur.org/idempotency-keys"}},"reason":"Make correct option contain its necessary safety assumption"}
- {"file":"api-idempotency-atomic-recovery.questions.json","question":"api-idempotency-q12","before":{"id":"api-idempotency-q12","level":"intermediate","type":"single","prompt":"A worker reads an SQS message 'credit account 7 with £20', applies the credit, then crashes before deleting the message. SQS redelivers it after the visibility timeout. How do you get an effectively-once credit?","options":[{"text":"Increase the visibility timeout so redelivery never happens","explanation":"A longer timeout delays redelivery but doesn't prevent it. After a crash, the message is redelivered whenever the timeout expires, however long it is."},{"text":"Delete the message *before* applying the credit","explanation":"That swaps duplicates for losses: if the worker crashes after deleting but before crediting, the £20 is never applied. That's at-most-once."},{"text":"Record the message id in a `processed` table in the **same database transaction** as the credit, and skip messages whose id is already there","explanation":"Correct. The credit and the 'seen it' marker commit together or not at all, so a redelivered message is recognised and skipped. At-least-once delivery plus an idempotent receiver."},{"text":"Nothing: SQS standard queues guarantee exactly-once delivery","explanation":"Standard queues are at-least-once and can deliver duplicates. FIFO queues deduplicate within a 5-minute window, but that doesn't help if your *consumer* crashes after applying the effect."}],"answer":2,"workedExample":"1. Delivery can be at most once (delete first) or at least once (delete after). Neither alone is exactly once.\n2. Choose at-least-once, then make the receiver idempotent:\n\n```sql\n-- Marker and credit commit together.\nBEGIN;\nINSERT INTO processed(msg_id)\n  -- Duplicate message ids abort the\n  -- credit.\n  VALUES ('m-81');  -- unique\nUPDATE accounts\n  SET balance = balance + 20\n  WHERE id = 7;\nCOMMIT;\n```\n\n3. On redelivery, the insert hits the unique constraint, the transaction rolls back, and the worker just deletes the message.\n4. The non-idempotent `balance + 20` is now safe, because it's guarded by the dedup record in the same transaction.","link":{"title":"AWS Builders' Library: Making retries safe with idempotent APIs","url":"https://aws.amazon.com/builders-library/making-retries-safe-with-idempotent-APIs/"}},"after":{"id":"api-idempotency-q12","level":"intermediate","type":"single","prompt":"A worker reads an SQS message 'credit account 7 with £20', applies the credit, then crashes before deleting the message. SQS redelivers it after the visibility timeout. How do you get an effectively-once credit?","options":[{"text":"Increase the visibility timeout so redelivery never happens","explanation":"A longer timeout delays redelivery but doesn't prevent it. After a crash, the message is redelivered whenever the timeout expires, however long it is."},{"text":"Delete the message *before* applying the credit","explanation":"That swaps duplicates for losses: if the worker crashes after deleting but before crediting, the £20 is never applied. That's at-most-once."},{"text":"Record the message id in a `processed` table in the **same database transaction** as the credit, and skip messages whose id is already there","explanation":"Correct. The credit and the 'seen it' marker commit together or not at all, so a redelivered message is recognised and skipped. At-least-once delivery plus an idempotent receiver."},{"text":"Nothing: SQS standard queues guarantee exactly-once delivery","explanation":"Standard queues are at-least-once and can deliver duplicates. FIFO queues deduplicate within a 5-minute window, but that doesn't help if your *consumer* crashes after applying the effect."}],"answer":2,"workedExample":"1. Delivery can be at most once (delete first) or at least once (delete after). Neither alone is exactly once.\n2. Choose at-least-once, then make the receiver idempotent:\n\n```sql\n-- Marker and credit commit together.\nBEGIN;\nINSERT INTO processed(msg_id)\n  -- Duplicate message ids abort the\n  -- credit.\n  VALUES ('m-81');  -- unique\nUPDATE accounts\n  SET balance = balance + 20\n  WHERE id = 7;\nCOMMIT;\n```\n\n3. On redelivery, the insert hits the unique constraint, the worker handles the unique-constraint error, rolls back the transaction, and deletes the already-processed message.\n4. The non-idempotent `balance + 20` is now safe, because it's guarded by the dedup record in the same transaction.","link":{"title":"AWS Builders' Library: Making retries safe with idempotent APIs","url":"https://aws.amazon.com/builders-library/making-retries-safe-with-idempotent-APIs/"}},"reason":"Rollback handling must be explicit across SQL implementations"}
- {"file":"api-backoff-jitter-budgets.questions.json","question":"api-idempotency-q6","before":{"id":"api-idempotency-q6","level":"advanced","type":"single","prompt":"A client uses exponential backoff with base 100 ms, a cap of 10 s, and **full jitter**:\n\n```formula\nT_max = min(cap, base × 2^a)\nT_sleep = random(0, T_max)\nwhere\nT_max: the longest the client may wait before the next attempt\ncap: the largest wait ever allowed, here 10 s\nbase: the starting wait, here 100 ms\na: the number of failures so far\nmin: the smaller of the two values\nrandom: a value picked uniformly at random between the two limits\nT_sleep: the wait actually used\nIn words: double the base once per failure, never exceed the cap, then wait a random time up to that.\n```\n\nWhat's the maximum possible wait before attempt 5 (i.e. after the 4th failure, a = 4), and what's the expected wait?","options":[{"text":"Max 1.6 s, expected 0.8 s","explanation":"Correct. 100 ms × 2⁴ = 100 ms × 16 = 1,600 ms, below the 10 s cap. Full jitter samples uniformly from 0 to 1.6 s, so the mean is 0.8 s."},{"text":"Max 1.6 s, expected 1.6 s","explanation":"That's plain exponential backoff without jitter. Full jitter picks a random value in [0, 1.6 s], so the expected wait is half the maximum."},{"text":"Max 10 s, expected 5 s","explanation":"The cap first applies at attempt index 7: a 100 ms base doubled seven times gives 12.8 s. Attempt index 4 gives 1.6 s, below the cap. The prompt defines attempt indexing from zero."},{"text":"Max 0.8 s, expected 0.4 s","explanation":"This uses 2³ rather than 2⁴. After 4 failures the multiplier is 2⁴ = 16, not 8."}],"answer":0,"workedExample":"```formula\nT_max = min(cap, base × 2^a)\nT_max = min(10,000 ms, 100 ms × 2^4)\nT_max = min(10,000 ms, 1,600 ms)\nT_max = 1,600 ms\nE[T_sleep] = T_max / 2 = 800 ms\nwhere\nT_max: the longest possible wait after a failures\ncap: the cap, 10 s = 10,000 ms\nbase: the base wait, 100 ms\na: the number of failures so far, here 4\nmin: the smaller of the two values\nE[T_sleep]: the expected (average) wait; for a value spread evenly between 0 and T_max it is half of T_max\nms: milliseconds, thousandths of a second\nIn words: the doubled wait (1.6 s) is still below the cap, so the wait is random between 0 and **1,600 ms**, averaging **800 ms**.\n```\n\nWhy jitter? If 10,000 clients failed together, without jitter they'd all retry exactly 1.6 s after the failure. With full jitter they spread evenly over 1.6 s: about 6,250 per second instead of 10,000 in a single instant.","link":{"title":"AWS Builders' Library: Timeouts, retries and backoff with jitter","url":"https://aws.amazon.com/builders-library/timeouts-retries-and-backoff-with-jitter/"}},"after":{"id":"api-idempotency-q6","level":"advanced","type":"single","prompt":"A client uses exponential backoff with base 100 ms, a cap of 10 s, and **full jitter**:\n\n```formula\nT_max = min(cap, base × 2^a)\nT_sleep = random(0, T_max)\nwhere\nT_max: the longest the client may wait before the next attempt\ncap: the largest wait ever allowed, here 10 s\nbase: the starting wait, here 100 ms\na: the number of failures so far\nmin: the smaller of the two values\nrandom: a value picked uniformly at random between the two limits\nT_sleep: the wait actually used\nIn words: double the base once per failure, never exceed the cap, then wait a random time up to that.\n```\n\nWhat's the maximum possible wait before attempt 5 (i.e. after the 4th failure, a = 4), and what's the expected wait?","options":[{"text":"Max 1.6 s, expected 0.8 s","explanation":"Correct. 100 ms × 2⁴ = 100 ms × 16 = 1,600 ms, below the 10 s cap. Full jitter samples uniformly from 0 to 1.6 s, so the mean is 0.8 s."},{"text":"Max 1.6 s, expected 1.6 s","explanation":"That's plain exponential backoff without jitter. Full jitter picks a random value in [0, 1.6 s], so the expected wait is half the maximum."},{"text":"Max 10 s, expected 5 s","explanation":"The cap first applies after seven failures: a 100 ms base doubled seven times gives 12.8 s. After four failures it gives 1.6 s, below the cap. Here the exponent is the failure count explicitly defined in the prompt."},{"text":"Max 0.8 s, expected 0.4 s","explanation":"This uses 2³ rather than 2⁴. After 4 failures the multiplier is 2⁴ = 16, not 8."}],"answer":0,"workedExample":"```formula\nT_max = min(cap, base × 2^a)\nT_max = min(10,000 ms, 100 ms × 2^4)\nT_max = min(10,000 ms, 1,600 ms)\nT_max = 1,600 ms\nE[T_sleep] = T_max / 2 = 800 ms\nwhere\nT_max: the longest possible wait after a failures\ncap: the cap, 10 s = 10,000 ms\nbase: the base wait, 100 ms\na: the number of failures so far, here 4\nmin: the smaller of the two values\nE[T_sleep]: the expected (average) wait; for a value spread evenly between 0 and T_max it is half of T_max\nms: milliseconds, thousandths of a second\nIn words: the doubled wait (1.6 s) is still below the cap, so the wait is random between 0 and **1,600 ms**, averaging **800 ms**.\n```\n\nWhy jitter? If 10,000 clients failed together, without jitter they'd all retry exactly 1.6 s after the failure. With full jitter they spread evenly over 1.6 s: about 6,250 per second instead of 10,000 in a single instant.","link":{"title":"AWS Builders' Library: Timeouts, retries and backoff with jitter","url":"https://aws.amazon.com/builders-library/timeouts-retries-and-backoff-with-jitter/"}},"reason":"Correct failure-count indexing terminology"}
- {"file":"api-quota-response-policy.md","before":"`503` means \"the service is unhealthy\", which triggers different alerts and different client behaviour. (The reverse is fine: shedding load because the *whole fleet* is overloaded is a legitimate `503`.)","after":"`503` describes temporary inability to serve this request, which clients and monitors may handle differently. Load shedding or maintenance can legitimately use `503` even if other endpoints or clients are still served.","reason":"503 does not prove whole-fleet outage or dictate monitor behavior"}
- {"file":"api-quota-response-policy.questions.json","question":"api-rate-limiting-q1","before":{"id":"api-rate-limiting-q1","level":"basic","type":"single","prompt":"A client exceeds its per-user quota. Which response best follows HTTP conventions?","options":[{"text":"`503 Service Unavailable` with no headers","explanation":"503 means the *service* is unhealthy or overloaded for requests the service cannot currently handle. It triggers outage alerts and generic retry behaviour, and it hides the fact that this particular client is the problem."},{"text":"`403 Forbidden`","explanation":"403 means the caller isn't allowed to do this at all. Clients won't retry, even though the request would succeed after the window resets."},{"text":"`429 Too Many Requests` with a `Retry-After` header","explanation":"Correct. RFC 6585 defines 429 for exactly this case, and `Retry-After` tells the client how many seconds to wait, so well-behaved clients back off precisely instead of guessing."},{"text":"`200 OK` with an empty body","explanation":"Pretending to succeed hides the problem: the client thinks its request worked, and your monitoring sees no errors."}],"answer":2,"workedExample":"Match the semantics:\n\n- Is the request malformed? No → not 400.\n- Is the caller never allowed? No → not 403.\n- Is the whole service down? No → not 503.\n- Has *this* caller sent too many requests *for now*? Yes → **429**.\n\nThen add guidance:\n\n```\nHTTP/1.1 429 Too Many Requests\nRetry-After: 30\nRateLimit: \"min\";r=0;t=30\n```","link":{"title":"MDN: 429 Too Many Requests","url":"https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/HTTP/Status/429"}},"after":{"id":"api-rate-limiting-q1","level":"basic","type":"single","prompt":"A client exceeds its per-user quota. Which response best follows HTTP conventions?","options":[{"text":"`503 Service Unavailable` with no headers","explanation":"503 means the *service* is unhealthy or overloaded for requests the service cannot currently handle. It triggers outage alerts and generic retry behaviour, and it hides the fact that this particular client is the problem."},{"text":"`403 Forbidden`","explanation":"403 means the server refuses fulfilment; it is not the specific HTTP quota signal. Different credentials or permissions may sometimes resolve a 403, so it does not universally mean permanent denial."},{"text":"`429 Too Many Requests` with a `Retry-After` header","explanation":"Correct. RFC 6585 defines 429 for exactly this case, and `Retry-After` tells the client how many seconds to wait, so well-behaved clients back off precisely instead of guessing."},{"text":"`200 OK` with an empty body","explanation":"Pretending to succeed hides the problem: the client thinks its request worked, and your monitoring sees no errors."}],"answer":2,"workedExample":"Match the semantics:\n\n- Is the request malformed? No → not 400.\n- Is the caller never allowed? No → not 403.\n- Is the whole service down? No → not 503.\n- Has *this* caller sent too many requests *for now*? Yes → **429**.\n\nThen add guidance:\n\n```\nHTTP/1.1 429 Too Many Requests\nRetry-After: 30\nRateLimit: \"min\";r=0;t=30\n```","link":{"title":"MDN: 429 Too Many Requests","url":"https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/HTTP/Status/429"}},"reason":"403 is not necessarily permanent"}
- {"file":"api-sliding-window-accuracy.md","before":"n: the number of requests that client made inside the window","after":"n: the number of accepted requests retained for that client inside the window","reason":"Snippet only records accepted requests"}
- {"file":"api-sliding-window-accuracy.md","before":"The weight 1 − t/W is the fraction of the previous window that still lies inside the sliding window","after":"The weight in the formula is the fraction of the previous window that still lies inside the sliding window","reason":"Remove repeated inline symbolic expression"}
- {"file":"api-session-token-revocation.md","before":"**Revocation is instant**","after":"**Session revocation applies at validation**","reason":"Avoid instantaneous revocation overstatement"}
- {"file":"api-session-token-revocation.md","before":"any service with the public key can verify it","after":"with asymmetric signing, a service with the trusted public key can verify its signature and then validate its required claims","reason":"Signature alone is not full token validation; symmetric forms differ"}
- {"file":"api-session-token-revocation.md","before":"| Cross-service | Needs shared store | Just a public key |","after":"| Cross-service | Shared authoritative state | Trusted verification key and profile checks |","reason":"Verification requires claims/issuer/audience policy"}
- {"file":"api-session-token-revocation.questions.json","question":"api-auth-q6","before":{"id":"api-auth-q6","level":"advanced","type":"single","prompt":"An account is disabled at 09:00. Its stateless JWT access token was issued at 08:30 and expires eight hours later. The API validates signature and claims, including exp, but does no revocation/account lookup. What exposure remains, and what reduces it without a per-request database lookup?","options":[{"text":"No exposure; disabling the account invalidates the JWT","explanation":"A stateless JWT is verified purely by its signature and claims. The API never consults the account table, so disabling the account changes nothing until expiry."},{"text":"Until 16:30. Shorten access tokens to ~10 minutes and use refresh tokens that are checked against the account on each refresh","explanation":"Correct. Exposure drops from 7.5 hours to at most ~10 minutes, and the only stateful check happens once per refresh, not per request. For instant revocation you can add a small `jti` deny-list."},{"text":"Until 16:30. Switch from RS256 to HS256","explanation":"Changing the signing algorithm doesn't affect lifetime or revocation. It would also make every verifier capable of forging tokens."},{"text":"Until 16:30. Rotate the signing key","explanation":"Issuing a new signing key alone does not invalidate tokens signed by a still-trusted old key. Removing the old verification key from every verifier can invalidate all tokens using it, with propagation/cache delays and broad disruption."}],"answer":1,"workedExample":"```formula\nT_exp = T_iss + L\nT_exp = 08:30 + 8 h = 16:30\nE = T_exp − T_dis\nE = 16:30 − 09:00 = 7.5 h\nwhere\nT_exp: the token's expiry time, its exp claim\nT_iss: the time the token was issued, 08:30\nL: the token's lifetime, 8 hours\nE: the exposure window: how long the token still works after the account is disabled\nT_dis: the time the account was disabled, 09:00\nh: hours\nIn words: the token outlives the account by **7.5 hours**, because nothing checks the account until **16:30**.\n```\n\nWith 10-minute access tokens, the worst case is a token issued just before 09:00, so the exposure is at most the lifetime: ≤ **10 minutes**. At 09:10 the client tries to refresh; the auth server sees the account is disabled and refuses.\n\nCost: one auth-server check every 10 minutes per client, instead of one DB lookup per API request. If you need sub-minute revocation (a compromised admin), push the `jti` to a deny-list cached on every gateway.","link":{"title":"RFC 8725: JWT Best Current Practices","url":"https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8725"}},"after":{"id":"api-auth-q6","level":"advanced","type":"single","prompt":"An account is disabled at 09:00. Its stateless JWT access token was issued at 08:30 and expires eight hours later. The API validates signature and claims, including exp, but does no revocation/account lookup. What exposure remains, and what reduces it without a per-request database lookup?","options":[{"text":"No exposure; disabling the account invalidates the JWT","explanation":"A stateless JWT is verified purely by its signature and claims. The API never consults the account table, so disabling the account changes nothing until expiry."},{"text":"Until 16:30. Shorten access tokens to ~10 minutes and use refresh tokens that are checked against the account on each refresh","explanation":"Correct under the stated validation policy. Short access-token lifetimes reduce exposure to at most about ten minutes if refreshes check the current account state. A jti deny-list can enforce earlier token revocation, subject to propagation and cache delays."},{"text":"Until 16:30. Switch from RS256 to HS256","explanation":"Changing the signing algorithm doesn't affect lifetime or revocation. It would also make every verifier capable of forging tokens."},{"text":"Until 16:30. Rotate the signing key","explanation":"Issuing a new signing key alone does not invalidate tokens signed by a still-trusted old key. Removing the old verification key from every verifier can invalidate all tokens using it, with propagation/cache delays and broad disruption."}],"answer":1,"workedExample":"```formula\nT_exp = T_iss + L\nT_exp = 08:30 + 8 h = 16:30\nE = T_exp − T_dis\nE = 16:30 − 09:00 = 7.5 h\nwhere\nT_exp: the token's expiry time, its exp claim\nT_iss: the time the token was issued, 08:30\nL: the token's lifetime, 8 hours\nE: the exposure window: how long the token still works after the account is disabled\nT_dis: the time the account was disabled, 09:00\nh: hours\nIn words: the token outlives the account by **7.5 hours**, because nothing checks the account until **16:30**.\n```\n\nWith 10-minute access tokens, the worst case is a token issued just before 09:00, so the exposure is at most the lifetime: ≤ **10 minutes**. At 09:10 the client tries to refresh; the auth server sees the account is disabled and refuses.\n\nCost: one auth-server check every 10 minutes per client, instead of one DB lookup per API request. If you need sub-minute revocation (a compromised admin), push the `jti` to a deny-list cached on every gateway.","link":{"title":"RFC 8725: JWT Best Current Practices","url":"https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8725"}},"reason":"Deny-list does not guarantee instant propagation"}
- {"file":"api-webhook-durable-delivery.md","before":"Providers time out (GitHub asks for a 2xx within 10 seconds) and treat slow responses as failures, which causes retries and duplicate work.","after":"Providers time out (GitHub asks for a 2xx within 10 seconds) and treat slow responses as failures. Providers with automatic retries can then send duplicates; GitHub instead supports explicit redelivery.","reason":"GitHub has no automatic failed-delivery retry"}
- {"file":"api-webhook-durable-delivery.questions.json","question":"api-webhooks-gateways-q4","before":{"id":"api-webhooks-gateways-q4","level":"intermediate","type":"multi","prompt":"You're consuming Stripe-style payment webhooks with bounded retries and possible duplicates. Which practices should you adopt? Select all that apply.","options":[{"text":"Verify the signature, persist the event to a queue, return 200 immediately, then process asynchronously","explanation":"Correct. Fast acknowledgement avoids provider timeouts (and the duplicate retries they cause), and your downstream outages don't turn into webhook failures."},{"text":"Deduplicate on the authenticated event id, in the same transaction as the local database side effect","explanation":"Correct. Retries can produce duplicates even though bounded retries cannot guarantee delivery. Doing the dedupe insert and the business update atomically prevents a crash between them leaving things inconsistent."},{"text":"Assume events arrive in the order they were created","explanation":"Wrong. Providers such as Stripe explicitly don't guarantee order. Retries and parallel delivery mean `updated` can arrive before `created`."},{"text":"Periodically reconcile against the provider's events or objects API","explanation":"Correct. If your endpoint was down beyond the retry window (Stripe retries for up to 3 days), reconciliation catches missed events. Webhooks are a notification, not your only source of truth."},{"text":"Return 500 for event types you don't handle so the provider stops sending them","explanation":"Wrong. A 5xx tells the provider delivery *failed*, so it retries for days and may eventually disable your endpoint. Return 2xx for events you ignore, and unsubscribe from them in the provider's settings."}],"answer":[0,1,3],"workedExample":"Check each against the delivery model (at-least-once, unordered, retried on non-2xx):\n\n1. Ack fast + async: avoids timeouts. ✔\n2. Dedupe atomically: handles at-least-once. ✔\n3. Assume order: contradicts 'unordered'. ✘\n4. Reconcile: covers deliveries lost beyond the retry window. ✔\n5. 500 for unhandled: causes days of retries. ✘","link":{"title":"Stripe docs: Receive Stripe events in your webhook endpoint","url":"https://docs.stripe.com/webhooks"}},"after":{"id":"api-webhooks-gateways-q4","level":"intermediate","type":"multi","prompt":"You're consuming Stripe-style payment webhooks with bounded retries and possible duplicates. Which practices should you adopt? Select all that apply.","options":[{"text":"Verify the signature, persist the event to a queue, return 200 immediately, then process asynchronously","explanation":"Correct. Fast acknowledgement avoids provider timeouts (and the duplicate retries they cause), and your downstream outages don't turn into webhook failures."},{"text":"Deduplicate on the authenticated event id, in the same transaction as the local database side effect","explanation":"Correct. Retries can produce duplicates even though bounded retries cannot guarantee delivery. Doing the dedupe insert and the business update atomically prevents a crash between them leaving things inconsistent."},{"text":"Assume events arrive in the order they were created","explanation":"Wrong. Providers such as Stripe explicitly don't guarantee order. Retries and parallel delivery mean `updated` can arrive before `created`."},{"text":"Periodically reconcile against the provider's events or objects API","explanation":"Correct. If your endpoint was down beyond the retry window (Stripe retries for up to 3 days), reconciliation catches missed events. Webhooks are a notification, not your only source of truth."},{"text":"Return 500 for event types you don't handle so the provider stops sending them","explanation":"Wrong. A 5xx tells the provider delivery *failed*, so it retries for days and may eventually disable your endpoint. Return 2xx for events you ignore, and unsubscribe from them in the provider's settings."}],"answer":[0,1,3],"workedExample":"Check each against the delivery model (duplicate-capable, unordered, bounded retries on non-2xx; eventual delivery is not guaranteed):\n\n1. Ack fast + async: avoids timeouts. ✔\n2. Dedupe atomically: handles duplicate delivery. ✔\n3. Assume order: contradicts 'unordered'. ✘\n4. Reconcile: covers deliveries lost beyond the retry window. ✔\n5. 500 for unhandled: causes days of retries. ✘","link":{"title":"Stripe docs: Receive Stripe events in your webhook endpoint","url":"https://docs.stripe.com/webhooks"}},"reason":"Bounded retries are not an unconditional at-least-once guarantee"}

- {"file":"api-webhook-signatures-replay.md","before":"The freshness check, applied after the signature has verified:","after":"The following one-sided age check rejects old deliveries after signature verification. Follow the provider SDK's clock-skew policy as well; this inequality alone does not reject future timestamps.","reason":"Age bound differs from complete clock-skew freshness policy"}
- {"file":"api-graphql-resolvers-costs.md","reason":"Resolve dictionary words to their intended local meaning","before":"---\nid: api-graphql-resolvers-costs\ntitle: GraphQL resolvers, batching and query costs\nlevel: intermediate\nminutes: 4\nsummary: Use DataLoader batching while controlling nested-query work and HTTP caching.\nsourceLesson: api-rest-graphql-grpc\n---\n\nOne client request can still trigger many database queries. Follow the work inside the resolver tree, as well as the shape of the HTTP response.\n\n## GraphQL: the client asks for exactly what it needs\n\nGraphQL, open-sourced by Facebook in 2015, exposes a typed schema. Clients send a query that mirrors the shape of the data they want, usually as `POST /graphql`.\n\n```\nquery {\n  feed(first: 20) {\n    id\n    title\n    author { name avatarUrl }\n    comments(first: 3) { text }\n  }\n}\n```\n\nOne HTTP request selects the requested fields. Successful data follows that selection; errors and nullability can alter what is returned. The GraphQL schema supports introspection, so tools like GraphiQL give autocomplete and type-checking for free.\n\n## The N+1 problem\n\nGraphQL moves the fan-out from the client to the server. Each field is backed by a *resolver* function. Naïve resolvers make the problem worse, not better:\n\n```\nfeed resolver:   1 query  -> 20 posts\nauthor resolver: called 20 times\n  SELECT * FROM users WHERE id = ?\n                 -> 20 queries\nTotal: 21 queries for these 20 posts\n```\n\nThe general count, for a list of N parents where each nested field gets its own resolver call per parent:\n\n```formula\nQ_naive = 1 + L × N\nQ_naive = 1 + 1 × 20 = 21\nQ_naive = 1 + 2 × 20 = 41\nwhere\nQ_naive: the number of database queries one GraphQL request makes with naïve resolvers\nN: the number of parent items the list query returns, here 20 posts\nL: the number of nested fields resolved separately for each parent: 1 for author only, 2 for author and comments\nIn words: one query fetches the list, then every nested field adds one query per item, so the total grows in step with the list length.\n```\n\nSo adding comments takes it from 21 to 41 queries. Nest one more level and it multiplies, because each of those children has its own children.\n\nThe standard fix is **batching with a DataLoader**. Within one tick of the event loop, the loader collects every `author` id requested, then issues a single query:\n\n```sql\n-- Illustrative ids; bind real parameters.\nSELECT * FROM users\nWHERE id IN (a, b, c, ..., t)\n```\n\nWith one database batch per nested field, the count no longer depends on N:\n\n```formula\nQ_batched = 1 + L\nQ_batched = 1 + 2 = 3\nwhere\nQ_batched: the number of database queries with batching\nL: the number of nested fields batched, here 2 (author and comments)\nIn words: one query for the list plus one batched query per nested field, however many posts the list returns.\n```\n\nSo the example drops from 41 queries to 3. Parameter limits, batch-size caps and per-parent pagination can require additional work as N grows. DataLoader also caches per request, so the same author appearing on five posts is fetched once.\n\n> [!warning] GraphQL's operational costs\n> - **Caching**: if operations use `POST /graphql`, ordinary URL-based CDN caching cannot distinguish request bodies. GraphQL queries may also use GET; mutation operations must not execute through GET. Teams use *persisted queries* (send a hash; allow `GET`) to recover some of this.\n> - **Abuse**: a client can write a deeply nested query that fans out to millions of rows. You need query depth limits, complexity scoring and timeouts. GitHub's GraphQL API, for example, charges each query \"points\" from the connections and backing requests needed.\n> - **Errors**: responses are often `200 OK` with an `errors` array, which breaks status-code-based monitoring.\n\nFor a static potential-node budget, count each nesting level by multiplying the number of parents by the requested children per parent, then add the counts across levels. Pagination bounds each branch before any resolver runs; actual database work also depends on resolver implementation.\n\n## Key takeaways\n- GraphQL gives clients a typed response shape and moves fan-out to resolvers.\n- Request-scoped DataLoader batches supported loads and caches repeated identities.\n- Persisted read queries over GET can support ordinary URL caching; mutations must not execute through GET.\n- Budget depth, potential node count and execution time; inspect GraphQL errors as well as HTTP status.\n\n## Further reading\n- [GraphQL: Introduction to GraphQL](https://graphql.org/learn/)\n- [GraphQL: Performance (N+1 and DataLoader)](https://graphql.org/learn/performance/)\n- [DataLoader on GitHub](https://github.com/graphql/dataloader)\n- [GitHub: GraphQL query limits](https://docs.github.com/en/graphql/overview/rate-limits-and-query-limits-for-the-graphql-api)\n","after":"---\nid: api-graphql-resolvers-costs\ntitle: GraphQL resolvers, batching and query costs\nlevel: intermediate\nminutes: 4\nsummary: Use DataLoader batching while controlling nested-query work and HTTP caching.\nsourceLesson: api-rest-graphql-grpc\n---\n\nOne client request can still trigger many database queries. Follow the work inside the resolver tree, as well as the shape of the HTTP response.\n\n## GraphQL: the client asks for exactly what it needs\n\nGraphQL, open-sourced by Facebook in 2015, exposes a GraphQL schema. Clients send a query that mirrors the shape of the data they want, usually as `POST /graphql`.\n\n```\nquery {\n  feed(first: 20) {\n    id\n    title\n    author { name avatarUrl }\n    comments(first: 3) { text }\n  }\n}\n```\n\nOne HTTP request selects the requested fields. Successful data follows that selection; errors and nullability can alter what is returned. The GraphQL schema supports introspection, so tools like GraphiQL give autocomplete and type-checking for free.\n\n## The N+1 problem\n\nGraphQL moves the fan-out from the client to the server. Each field is backed by a *resolver* function. Naïve resolvers make the problem worse, not better:\n\n```\nfeed resolver:   1 query  -> 20 posts\nauthor resolver: called 20 times\n  SELECT * FROM users WHERE id = ?\n                 -> 20 queries\nTotal: 21 queries for these 20 posts\n```\n\nThe general count, for a list of N parents where each nested field gets its own resolver call per parent:\n\n```formula\nQ_naive = 1 + L × N\nQ_naive = 1 + 1 × 20 = 21\nQ_naive = 1 + 2 × 20 = 41\nwhere\nQ_naive: the number of database queries one GraphQL request makes with naïve resolvers\nN: the number of parent items the list query returns, here 20 posts\nL: the number of nested fields resolved separately for each parent: 1 for author only, 2 for author and comments\nIn words: one query fetches the list, then every nested field adds one query per item, so the total grows in step with the list length.\n```\n\nSo adding comments takes it from 21 to 41 queries. Nest one more level and it multiplies, because each of those children has its own children.\n\nThe standard fix is **batching with a DataLoader**. Within one tick of the event loop, the loader collects every `author` id requested, then issues a single query:\n\n```sql\n-- Illustrative ids; bind real parameters.\nSELECT * FROM users\nWHERE id IN (a, b, c, ..., t)\n```\n\nWith one database batch per nested field, the count no longer depends on N:\n\n```formula\nQ_batched = 1 + L\nQ_batched = 1 + 2 = 3\nwhere\nQ_batched: the number of database queries with batching\nL: the number of nested fields batched, here 2 (author and comments)\nIn words: one query for the list plus one batched query per nested field, however many posts the list returns.\n```\n\nSo the example drops from 41 queries to 3. Parameter limits, batch-size caps and per-parent pagination can require additional work as N grows. DataLoader also caches per request, so the same author appearing on five posts is fetched once.\n\n> [!warning] GraphQL's operational costs\n> - **Caching**: if operations use `POST /graphql`, ordinary URL-based CDN caching cannot distinguish request bodies. GraphQL queries may also use GET; mutation operations must not execute through GET. Teams use *persisted queries* (send a hash; allow `GET`) to recover some of this.\n> - **Abuse**: a client can write a deeply nested query that fans out to millions of rows. You need query depth limits, complexity scoring and timeouts. GitHub's GraphQL API, for example, charges each query \"points\" from the connections and backing requests needed.\n> - **Errors**: responses are often `200 OK` with an `errors` array, which breaks status-code-based monitoring.\n\nFor a static potential-node budget, count each nesting level by multiplying the number of parents by the requested children per parent, then add the counts across levels. Pagination bounds each branch before any resolver runs; actual database work also depends on resolver implementation.\n\n## Key takeaways\n- GraphQL gives clients a typed response shape and moves fan-out to resolvers.\n- Request-scoped DataLoader batches supported loads and caches repeated identities.\n- Persisted read queries over GET can support ordinary URL caching; mutations must not execute through GET.\n- Budget depth, potential node count and execution time; inspect GraphQL errors as well as HTTP status.\n\n## Further reading\n- [GraphQL: Introduction to GraphQL](https://graphql.org/learn/)\n- [GraphQL: Performance (N+1 and DataLoader)](https://graphql.org/learn/performance/)\n- [DataLoader on GitHub](https://github.com/graphql/dataloader)\n- [GitHub: GraphQL query limits](https://docs.github.com/en/graphql/overview/rate-limits-and-query-limits-for-the-graphql-api)\n"}
- {"file":"api-graphql-resolvers-costs.questions.json","reason":"Resolve dictionary words to their intended local meaning","before":"[\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q2\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"A GraphQL query fetches 50 posts, each with author and tags. Naïve resolvers issue one query per parent for each nested field. Assume all author loads form one supported DB batch and all tag loads another. How many queries run before and after batching?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"101 naïvely; 3 with batching\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. Naïve: 1 (posts) + 50 (one author query per post) + 50 (one tags query per post) = 101. Batched: 1 for posts, 1 `WHERE id IN (...)` for all authors, 1 for all tags = 3.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"51 naïvely; 2 with batching\",\n        \"explanation\": \"This counts only one of the two nested fields. Both `author` and `tags` fan out per post, so each adds 50 queries naïvely and 1 when batched.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"3 in both cases, because GraphQL batches automatically\",\n        \"explanation\": \"GraphQL execution doesn't batch by itself. Each resolver runs independently for each parent; batching requires a tool such as DataLoader.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"2,500 naïvely; 50 with batching\",\n        \"explanation\": \"This multiplies 50 × 50 as if tags were nested under authors. They're siblings under each post, so the counts add rather than multiply.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 0,\n    \"workedExample\": \"**Naïve**\\n\\n1. `posts` resolver: 1 query → 50 rows.\\n2. `author` resolver runs once per post: 50 queries.\\n3. `tags` resolver runs once per post: 50 queries.\\n4. One list query plus 50 author and 50 tag queries gives **101** in total.\\n\\n**With DataLoader**\\n\\n1. `posts`: 1 query.\\n2. All 50 `author` loads are queued in the same tick, then sent as one `SELECT ... WHERE id IN (...)`: 1 query.\\n3. Same for tags: 1 query.\\n4. The total is **3**, under the stated batch assumptions; large batches can hit parameter or query-planning limits.\\n\\nThe same counts as formulas:\\n\\n```formula\\nQ_naive = 1 + L × N\\nQ_naive = 1 + 2 × 50 = 101\\nQ_batch = 1 + L\\nQ_batch = 1 + 2 = 3\\nwhere\\nQ_naive: database queries with one resolver call per post per nested field\\nQ_batch: database queries when each nested field is loaded in one batch\\nN: the number of posts the list returns, here 50\\nL: the number of nested fields resolved per post, here 2 (author and tags)\\nIn words: naïvely each nested field costs one query per post; batched, each costs one query in total.\\n```\\n\\nIf every query hypothetically costs 2 ms, summed query durations are 202 ms versus 6 ms. Batches may cost more per query and queries can overlap, so this is not a measured end-to-end latency improvement.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"GraphQL: Performance\",\n      \"url\": \"https://graphql.org/learn/performance/\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q4\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"multi\",\n    \"prompt\": \"Which of these are genuine drawbacks of exposing a **public** GraphQL API compared with REST? Select all that apply.\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Responses can't easily be cached by CDNs because most queries go to `POST /graphql`\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct for ordinary URL-based caching of POST queries: request bodies are not normally part of the cache key. Explicit POST caching can be defined, and persisted read queries over GET make ordinary URL caching practical.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Clients can craft deeply nested or wide queries that are very expensive, so you need depth limits and cost analysis\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. In REST the server decides each endpoint's cost. In GraphQL the client composes the query, so the server must defend itself. GitHub's API uses a points-based cost model for this reason.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"GraphQL can't return nested data\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Wrong. Returning nested data in one round trip is GraphQL's main selling point.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Errors often come back as `200 OK` with an `errors` array, which breaks tooling that relies on HTTP status codes\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. Partial success is a GraphQL feature, but it means load balancers, dashboards and retry logic that look at status codes see 'success'.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"GraphQL has no schema, so clients can't discover fields\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Wrong. GraphQL *requires* a typed schema and supports introspection; discoverability is one of its strengths.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": [\n      0,\n      1,\n      3\n    ],\n    \"workedExample\": \"Evaluate each claim against how GraphQL actually works:\\n\\n1. **Caching**: single endpoint, usually POST → CDN can't distinguish queries. Real drawback.\\n2. **Query cost**: client controls shape → potential for `friends { friends { friends ... } }` explosions. Real drawback.\\n3. **Nesting**: false; it's the core feature.\\n4. **Errors in 200s**: real operational drawback.\\n5. **No schema**: false; schema is mandatory.\\n\\nSo the drawbacks are 1, 2 and 4.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"GraphQL: Performance\",\n      \"url\": \"https://graphql.org/learn/performance/\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q8\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"A mobile app uses 40 known read-only GraphQL query shapes returning public, non-personalized cacheable data. It currently sends POST requests and the CDN does not cache them. Which approach enables ordinary URL-based caching?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Switch the CDN to cache POST responses by request body\",\n        \"explanation\": \"CDNs generally don't cache POST, and keying on large, variably formatted bodies is fragile. It also does nothing to stop arbitrary queries hitting your origin.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Persisted queries: register the 40 queries ahead of time, have clients send `GET /graphql?id=<hash>&variables=...`, and let the CDN cache by URL\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. A short hash stands in for the query text, which makes requests small, GET-able and cacheable by URL. If the server accepts *only* registered hashes, it also blocks arbitrary expensive queries.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Add DataLoader to the resolvers\",\n        \"explanation\": \"DataLoader batches and caches database lookups *within one request* on the server. It reduces N+1 queries but doesn't make responses cacheable at the CDN.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Return `Cache-Control: max-age=60` on the POST responses\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Explicit POST caching is permitted by HTTP, but max-age alone does not make this CDN distinguish and cache different query bodies. The stated ordinary URL cache is best served by persisted read queries over GET.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 1,\n    \"workedExample\": \"1. CDNs cache by method plus URL. `POST /graphql` with the query in the body gives them nothing to key on.\\n2. Because the app's queries are known at build time, you can hash each one (e.g. SHA-256) and register the hash with the server.\\n3. The client then sends:\\n\\n```\\nGET /graphql?id=9f2c...&vars={\\\"id\\\":7}\\n```\\n\\n4. That's a normal cacheable URL. Set `Cache-Control` on the response as you would in REST.\\n\\nBonus: an allow-list of persisted queries is a strong defence against query-cost abuse, because clients can no longer invent new queries.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"GraphQL: Performance\",\n      \"url\": \"https://graphql.org/learn/performance/\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q12\",\n    \"level\": \"advanced\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"Your public GraphQL API rejects any query whose *potential* node count exceeds 50,000. A client sends:\\n\\n```\\nusers(first: 100) {\\n  followers(first: 100) {\\n    repos(first: 50) { name }\\n  }\\n}\\n```\\n\\nWhat's the worst-case node count, and is it allowed?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"250, allowed\",\n        \"explanation\": \"That adds the page sizes (100 + 100 + 50). Nested connections multiply: each of the 100 users can have 100 followers.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"510,100, rejected\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. Count users, followers and repositories at every level: 100 users, 10,000 followers and 500,000 repositories give 510,100 nodes, about ten times the limit. The worked example shows the symbolic calculation.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"500,000, rejected\",\n        \"explanation\": \"That's only the innermost level. The users and followers are nodes too, though the answer (rejected) happens to be the same.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"It can't be known until the query runs\",\n        \"explanation\": \"The *actual* count can't be known in advance, but the worst case can, from the `first:` arguments. That's exactly why complexity scoring is done statically before execution.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 1,\n    \"workedExample\": \"Multiply down each nesting level and add the levels:\\n\\n```formula\\nT = U + U × F + U × F × P\\nT = 100 + 100 × 100 + 100 × 100 × 50\\nT = 100 + 10,000 + 500,000\\nT = 510,100\\nwhere\\nT: the worst-case number of nodes the query could return\\nU: users requested, from users(first: 100)\\nF: followers requested per user, from followers(first: 100)\\nP: repos requested per follower, from repos(first: 50)\\nIn words: every user can bring 100 followers and every follower 50 repos, so the levels multiply, and the total is the sum of all three levels.\\n```\\n\\n510,100 > 50,000, so the server rejects the query *before* running any resolvers. GitHub's GraphQL API uses this kind of static calculation, which is why every connection must specify `first` or `last`.\\n\\nThe client's fix is to page: fetch fewer users per query, or fetch repos for one follower at a time.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"GitHub Docs: GraphQL rate and query limits\",\n      \"url\": \"https://docs.github.com/en/graphql/overview/rate-limits-and-query-limits-for-the-graphql-api\"\n    }\n  }\n]\n","after":"[\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q2\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"A GraphQL query fetches 50 posts, each with author and tags. Naïve resolvers issue one query per parent for each nested field. Assume all author loads form one supported DB batch and all tag loads another. How many queries run before and after batching?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"101 naïvely; 3 with batching\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. Naïve: 1 (posts) + 50 (one author query per post) + 50 (one tags query per post) = 101. Batched: 1 for posts, 1 `WHERE id IN (...)` for all authors, 1 for all tags = 3.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"51 naïvely; 2 with batching\",\n        \"explanation\": \"This counts only one of the two nested fields. Both `author` and `tags` fan out per post, so each adds 50 queries naïvely and 1 when batched.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"3 in both cases, because GraphQL batches automatically\",\n        \"explanation\": \"GraphQL execution doesn't batch by itself. Each resolver runs independently for each parent; batching requires a tool such as DataLoader.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"2,500 naïvely; 50 with batching\",\n        \"explanation\": \"This multiplies 50 × 50 as if tags were nested under authors. They're siblings under each post, so the counts add rather than multiply.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 0,\n    \"workedExample\": \"**Naïve**\\n\\n1. `posts` resolver: 1 query → 50 rows.\\n2. `author` resolver runs once per post: 50 queries.\\n3. `tags` resolver runs once per post: 50 queries.\\n4. One list query plus 50 author and 50 tag queries gives **101** in total.\\n\\n**With DataLoader**\\n\\n1. `posts`: 1 query.\\n2. All 50 `author` loads are queued in the same tick, then sent as one `SELECT ... WHERE id IN (...)`: 1 query.\\n3. Same for tags: 1 query.\\n4. The total is **3**, under the stated batch assumptions; large batches can hit parameter or query-planning limits.\\n\\nThe same counts as formulas:\\n\\n```formula\\nQ_naive = 1 + L × N\\nQ_naive = 1 + 2 × 50 = 101\\nQ_batch = 1 + L\\nQ_batch = 1 + 2 = 3\\nwhere\\nQ_naive: database queries with one resolver call per post per nested field\\nQ_batch: database queries when each nested field is loaded in one batch\\nN: the number of posts the list returns, here 50\\nL: the number of nested fields resolved per post, here 2 (author and tags)\\nIn words: naïvely each nested field costs one query per post; batched, each costs one query in total.\\n```\\n\\nIf every query hypothetically costs 2 ms, summed query durations are 202 ms versus 6 ms. Batches may cost more per query and queries can overlap, so this is not a measured end-to-end latency improvement.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"GraphQL: Performance\",\n      \"url\": \"https://graphql.org/learn/performance/\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q4\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"multi\",\n    \"prompt\": \"Which of these are genuine drawbacks of exposing a **public** GraphQL API compared with REST? Select all that apply.\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Responses can't easily be cached by CDNs because most queries go to `POST /graphql`\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct for ordinary URL-based caching of POST queries: request bodies are not normally part of the cache key. Explicit POST caching can be defined, and persisted read queries over GET make ordinary URL caching practical.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Clients can craft deeply nested or wide queries that are very expensive, so you need depth limits and cost analysis\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. In REST the server decides each endpoint's cost. In GraphQL the client composes the query, so the server must defend itself. GitHub's API uses a points-based cost model for this reason.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"GraphQL can't return nested data\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Wrong. Returning nested data in one round trip is GraphQL's main selling point.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Errors often come back as `200 OK` with an `errors` array, which breaks tooling that relies on HTTP status codes\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. Partial success is a GraphQL feature, but it means load balancers, dashboards and retry logic that look at status codes see 'success'.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"GraphQL has no GraphQL schema, so clients can't discover fields\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Wrong. GraphQL *requires* a GraphQL schema and supports introspection; discoverability is one of its strengths.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": [\n      0,\n      1,\n      3\n    ],\n    \"workedExample\": \"Evaluate each claim against how GraphQL actually works:\\n\\n1. **Caching**: single endpoint, usually POST → CDN can't distinguish queries. Real drawback.\\n2. **Query cost**: client controls shape → potential for `friends { friends { friends ... } }` explosions. Real drawback.\\n3. **Nesting**: false; it's the core feature.\\n4. **Errors in 200s**: real operational drawback.\\n5. **No GraphQL schema**: false; GraphQL schema is mandatory.\\n\\nSo the drawbacks are 1, 2 and 4.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"GraphQL: Performance\",\n      \"url\": \"https://graphql.org/learn/performance/\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q8\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"A mobile app uses 40 known read-only GraphQL query shapes returning public, non-personalized cacheable data. It currently sends POST requests and the CDN does not cache them. Which approach enables ordinary URL-based caching?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Switch the CDN to cache POST responses by request body\",\n        \"explanation\": \"CDNs generally don't cache POST, and keying on large, variably formatted bodies is fragile. It also does nothing to stop arbitrary queries hitting your origin.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Persisted queries: register the 40 queries ahead of time, have clients send `GET /graphql?id=<hash>&variables=...`, and let the CDN cache by URL\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. A short hash stands in for the query text, which makes requests small, GET-able and cacheable by URL. If the server accepts *only* registered hashes, it also blocks arbitrary expensive queries.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Add DataLoader to the resolvers\",\n        \"explanation\": \"DataLoader batches and caches database lookups *within one request* on the server. It reduces N+1 queries but doesn't make responses cacheable at the CDN.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Return `Cache-Control: max-age=60` on the POST responses\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Explicit POST caching is permitted by HTTP, but max-age alone does not make this CDN distinguish and cache different query bodies. The stated ordinary URL cache is best served by persisted read queries over GET.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 1,\n    \"workedExample\": \"1. CDNs cache by method plus URL. `POST /graphql` with the query in the body gives them nothing to key on.\\n2. Because the app's queries are known at build time, you can hash each one (e.g. SHA-256) and register the hash with the server.\\n3. The client then sends:\\n\\n```\\nGET /graphql?id=9f2c...&vars={\\\"id\\\":7}\\n```\\n\\n4. That's a normal cacheable URL. Set `Cache-Control` on the response as you would in REST.\\n\\nBonus: an allow-list of persisted queries is a strong defence against query-cost abuse, because clients can no longer invent new queries.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"GraphQL: Performance\",\n      \"url\": \"https://graphql.org/learn/performance/\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q12\",\n    \"level\": \"advanced\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"Your public GraphQL API rejects any query whose *potential* node count exceeds 50,000. A client sends:\\n\\n```\\nusers(first: 100) {\\n  followers(first: 100) {\\n    repos(first: 50) { name }\\n  }\\n}\\n```\\n\\nWhat's the worst-case node count, and is it allowed?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"250, allowed\",\n        \"explanation\": \"That adds the page sizes (100 + 100 + 50). Nested connections multiply: each of the 100 users can have 100 followers.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"510,100, rejected\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. Count users, followers and repositories at every level: 100 users, 10,000 followers and 500,000 repositories give 510,100 nodes, about ten times the limit. The worked example shows the symbolic calculation.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"500,000, rejected\",\n        \"explanation\": \"That's only the innermost level. The users and followers are nodes too, though the answer (rejected) happens to be the same.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"It can't be known until the query runs\",\n        \"explanation\": \"The *actual* count can't be known in advance, but the worst case can, from the `first:` arguments. That's exactly why complexity scoring is done statically before execution.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 1,\n    \"workedExample\": \"Multiply down each nesting level and add the levels:\\n\\n```formula\\nT = U + U × F + U × F × P\\nT = 100 + 100 × 100 + 100 × 100 × 50\\nT = 100 + 10,000 + 500,000\\nT = 510,100\\nwhere\\nT: the worst-case number of nodes the query could return\\nU: users requested, from users(first: 100)\\nF: followers requested per user, from followers(first: 100)\\nP: repos requested per follower, from repos(first: 50)\\nIn words: every user can bring 100 followers and every follower 50 repos, so the levels multiply, and the total is the sum of all three levels.\\n```\\n\\n510,100 > 50,000, so the server rejects the query *before* running any resolvers. GitHub's GraphQL API uses this kind of static calculation, which is why every connection must specify `first` or `last`.\\n\\nThe client's fix is to page: fetch fewer users per query, or fetch repos for one follower at a time.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"GitHub Docs: GraphQL rate and query limits\",\n      \"url\": \"https://docs.github.com/en/graphql/overview/rate-limits-and-query-limits-for-the-graphql-api\"\n    }\n  }\n]\n"}
- {"file":"api-protobuf-schema-evolution.questions.json","reason":"Resolve dictionary words to their intended local meaning","before":"[\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q3\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"A proto message has `string email = 3;`. You delete the field and later add `int64 loyalty_points = 3;`. Some old clients are still deployed. What happens when an old client reads a new message?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"The old client gets a clear decoding error\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Protobuf parsers are deliberately lenient. They may not raise an error, which is why this is dangerous: the corruption can be silent.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Nothing; protobuf matches fields by name\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Protobuf matches by **tag number**, not by name. Names aren't on the wire at all, so renaming is safe but reusing a tag is not.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"A conforming reader sees a wire-type mismatch and normally treats the new value as an unknown field, leaving email unset; reusing tags is still forbidden\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. The field number matches but wire type 0 does not match the old string field’s type 2. Typical generated parsers preserve or skip it as unknown. Reusing the same number with a compatible wire type can instead silently assign the wrong meaning. Reserve removed numbers.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Old clients automatically download the new schema\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Protobuf has no runtime schema exchange. Clients use whatever generated code they were compiled with.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 2,\n    \"workedExample\": \"1. On the wire, each field is a key followed by the value. No names:\\n\\n```formula\\nK = (n << 3) | w\\nK = n × 8 + w\\nK = 3 × 8 + 0 = 24\\nwhere\\nK: the field key written before each value\\nn: the field's tag number from the .proto file\\nw: the wire type, a small code for how the value is encoded: 0 for varints (integers), 2 for length-delimited data (strings, bytes, nested messages)\\n<<: shift left: move the bits of n three places left, which is the same as multiplying by 8\\n|: bitwise OR: combine the bits; here it just puts w into the three low bits, the same as adding it\\nIn words: the key packs the tag number and the wire type into one number; for the new field (tag 3, a varint) it is 24.\\n```\\n\\n2. New message: tag 3, wire type 0 (varint), value e.g. 1200.\\n3. Old client's schema: tag 3 = `string` (wire type 2, length-delimited).\\n4. Wire type mismatch → a normal generated reader treats it as unknown, and `email` remains unset/default.\\n\\nCorrect evolution:\\n\\n```proto\\nmessage User {\\n  // Removed identity stays unavailable.\\n  reserved 3;\\n  reserved \\\"email\\\";\\n  int64 loyalty_points = 7;\\n}\\n```\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"Protocol Buffers: Language guide (proto3)\",\n      \"url\": \"https://protobuf.dev/programming-guides/proto3/\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q9\",\n    \"level\": \"advanced\",\n    \"type\": \"multi\",\n    \"prompt\": \"Old and new services exchange binary proto3 messages using implicit scalar presence. Considering wire compatibility only (not source API or business-required fields), which changes are safe? Select all that apply.\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Add `string nickname = 9;` (tag 9 never used before)\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Safe. Old readers skip the unknown tag 9; new readers see the default (empty string) when old writers omit it.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Rename `string sku = 1;` to `string product_code = 1;`\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Safe for binary protobuf, because only the tag goes on the wire. (It would break JSON-mapped traffic, which uses names, but that isn't in play here.)\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Change `int32 qty = 2;` to `string qty = 2;`\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Unsafe. Changing type on the same tag changes the wire type, so old readers will drop or misparse the field. Add a new field with a new tag instead.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Delete `string email = 3;` and add `reserved 3; reserved \\\"email\\\";`\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Safe. Old readers just see `email` as missing (empty), and `reserved` stops anyone accidentally reusing the tag or name later.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Delete `email = 3` and reuse tag 3 for `string phone = 3;`\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Unsafe, and the worst kind. Same wire type, so old readers will silently show phone numbers as email addresses.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": [\n      0,\n      1,\n      3\n    ],\n    \"workedExample\": \"Remember what's on the wire: a key, then the value. No names.\\n\\n```formula\\nK = (n << 3) | w\\nK = n × 8 + w\\nwhere\\nK: the field key written before each value\\nn: the field's tag number from the .proto file\\nw: the wire type, a small code for how the value is encoded: 0 for varints (integers), 2 for length-delimited data (strings, bytes, nested messages)\\n<<: shift left: move the bits of n three places left, which is the same as multiplying by 8\\n|: bitwise OR: combine the bits; here it just puts w into the three low bits, the same as adding it\\nIn words: only the tag number and the wire type identify a field on the wire, so a change is safe if both still mean the same thing to old readers.\\n```\\n\\n| Change | Tag | Wire type | Safe? |\\n|---|---|---|---|\\n| Add tag 9 | new | - | Yes |\\n| Rename | same | same | Yes |\\n| int32→string | same | 0→2 | No |\\n| Delete + reserve | gone | - | Yes |\\n| Reuse 3 | reused | same | No |\\n\\nThe reuse case is nastier than the type change: with matching wire types nothing fails at all, and the data is simply wrong.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"Protocol Buffers: Language guide (proto3)\",\n      \"url\": \"https://protobuf.dev/programming-guides/proto3/\"\n    }\n  }\n]\n","after":"[\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q3\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"A proto message has `string email = 3;`. You delete the field and later add `int64 loyalty_points = 3;`. Some old clients are still deployed. What happens when an old client reads a new message?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"The old client gets a clear decoding error\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Protobuf parsers are deliberately lenient. They may not raise an error, which is why this is dangerous: the corruption can be silent.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Nothing; protobuf matches fields by name\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Protobuf matches by **tag number**, not by name. Names aren't on the wire at all, so renaming is safe but reusing a tag is not.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"A conforming reader sees a wire-type mismatch and normally treats the new value as an unknown field, leaving email unset; reusing tags is still forbidden\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. The field number matches but wire type 0 does not match the old string field’s type 2. Typical generated parsers preserve or skip it as unknown. Reusing the same number with a compatible wire type can instead silently assign the wrong meaning.
