Reserve removed numbers.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Old clients automatically download the new protobuf schema\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Protobuf has no runtime protobuf schema exchange. Clients use whatever generated code they were compiled with.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 2,\n    \"workedExample\": \"1. On the wire, each field is a key followed by the value. No names:\\n\\n```formula\\nK = (n << 3) | w\\nK = n × 8 + w\\nK = 3 × 8 + 0 = 24\\nwhere\\nK: the field key written before each value\\nn: the field's tag number from the .proto file\\nw: the wire type, a small code for how the value is encoded: 0 for varints (integers), 2 for length-delimited data (strings, bytes, nested messages)\\n<<: shift left: move the bits of n three places left, which is the same as multiplying by 8\\n|: bitwise OR: combine the bits; here it just puts w into the three low bits, the same as adding it\\nIn words: the key packs the tag number and the wire type into one number; for the new field (tag 3, a varint) it is 24.\\n```\\n\\n2. New message: tag 3, wire type 0 (varint), value e.g. 1200.\\n3. Old client's protobuf schema: tag 3 = `string` (wire type 2, length-delimited).\\n4. Wire type mismatch → a normal generated reader treats it as unknown, and `email` remains unset/default.\\n\\nCorrect evolution:\\n\\n```proto\\nmessage User {\\n  // Removed identity stays unavailable.\\n  reserved 3;\\n  reserved \\\"email\\\";\\n  int64 loyalty_points = 7;\\n}\\n```\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"Protocol Buffers: Language guide (proto3)\",\n      \"url\": \"https://protobuf.dev/programming-guides/proto3/\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q9\",\n    \"level\": \"advanced\",\n    \"type\": \"multi\",\n    \"prompt\": \"Old and new services exchange binary proto3 messages using implicit scalar presence. Considering wire compatibility only (not source API or business-required fields), which changes are safe? Select all that apply.\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Add `string nickname = 9;` (tag 9 never used before)\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Safe. Old readers skip the unknown tag 9; new readers see the default (empty string) when old writers omit it.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Rename `string sku = 1;` to `string product_code = 1;`\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Safe for binary protobuf, because only the tag goes on the wire. (It would break JSON-mapped traffic, which uses names, but that isn't in play here.)\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Change `int32 qty = 2;` to `string qty = 2;`\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Unsafe. Changing type on the same tag changes the wire type, so old readers will drop or misparse the field. Add a new field with a new tag instead.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Delete `string email = 3;` and add `reserved 3; reserved \\\"email\\\";`\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Safe. Old readers just see `email` as missing (empty), and `reserved` stops anyone accidentally reusing the tag or name later.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Delete `email = 3` and reuse tag 3 for `string phone = 3;`\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Unsafe, and the worst kind. Same wire type, so old readers will silently show phone numbers as email addresses.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": [\n      0,\n      1,\n      3\n    ],\n    \"workedExample\": \"Remember what's on the wire: a key, then the value. No names.\\n\\n```formula\\nK = (n << 3) | w\\nK = n × 8 + w\\nwhere\\nK: the field key written before each value\\nn: the field's tag number from the .proto file\\nw: the wire type, a small code for how the value is encoded: 0 for varints (integers), 2 for length-delimited data (strings, bytes, nested messages)\\n<<: shift left: move the bits of n three places left, which is the same as multiplying by 8\\n|: bitwise OR: combine the bits; here it just puts w into the three low bits, the same as adding it\\nIn words: only the tag number and the wire type identify a field on the wire, so a change is safe if both still mean the same thing to old readers.\\n```\\n\\n| Change | Tag | Wire type | Safe? |\\n|---|---|---|---|\\n| Add tag 9 | new | - | Yes |\\n| Rename | same | same | Yes |\\n| int32→string | same | 0→2 | No |\\n| Delete + reserve | gone | - | Yes |\\n| Reuse 3 | reused | same | No |\\n\\nThe reuse case is nastier than the type change: with matching wire types nothing fails at all, and the data is simply wrong.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"Protocol Buffers: Language guide (proto3)\",\n      \"url\": \"https://protobuf.dev/programming-guides/proto3/\"\n    }\n  }\n]\n"}
- {"file":"api-grpc-streaming-selection.questions.json","reason":"Resolve dictionary words to their intended local meaning","before":"[\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q5\",\n    \"level\": \"advanced\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"You migrate internal service calls from REST/JSON to gRPC. Behind an L4 (TCP) load balancer with 10 backends, you notice one backend at 90% CPU and the others nearly idle. What's the most likely cause?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Protobuf is slower to parse than JSON\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Parsing performance depends on workload and implementation. It would not by itself explain why an L4 balancer pins a high-volume connection to one backend.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"gRPC multiplexes many calls over one long-lived HTTP/2 connection, and an L4 balancer only balances connections, so all of a client's requests stick to one backend\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct under the stated connection-level balancer. Each TCP connection is assigned to a backend; a long-lived gRPC connection can carry many calls to that backend. HTTP/1.1 also supports persistent pooled connections, so it does not guarantee an even distribution. L7 or client-side balancing can distribute calls.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"gRPC deadlines are concentrating retries on one server\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Deadlines cause calls to fail fast when time runs out. They don't steer traffic to a particular backend.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"The backend is running a different protobuf version\",\n        \"explanation\": \"A version mismatch could cause decoding issues, but it wouldn't attract a disproportionate share of traffic to one host.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 1,\n    \"workedExample\": \"1. L4 balancing decides at **TCP connect** time.\\n2. With HTTP/1.1, clients typically open a pool of connections and recycle them, so load spreads.\\n3. gRPC/HTTP/2 multiplexes: one connection carries hundreds of concurrent streams and lives for hours.\\n4. If a few large callers each hold one connection, those connections land on one or two backends, and stay there.\\n\\nFixes:\\n- L7 proxy (Envoy, Linkerd, gRPC-aware cloud LBs) that balances per **request**.\\n- Client-side load balancing: the client resolves all backend addresses and round-robins calls.\\n- Set a max connection age so connections are periodically re-established.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"gRPC: Performance best practices\",\n      \"url\": \"https://grpc.io/docs/guides/performance/\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q6\",\n    \"level\": \"advanced\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"A checkout service calls Pricing (which calls Tax, which calls a Currency service). The user-facing SLA is 500 ms. Pricing sometimes hangs for 5 s, and Tax keeps working on requests long after the user has given up. Which gRPC feature most directly addresses this wasted work?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Bidirectional streaming\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Streaming changes the shape of the conversation (many messages each way), not whether downstream services know the caller has stopped waiting.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Server reflection\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Reflection lets tools like `grpcurl` discover services at runtime. It has nothing to do with timeouts.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Deadline propagation: Checkout sets a 500 ms deadline that flows to Pricing, Tax and Currency, each of which can cancel when the remaining time hits zero\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct when deadline propagation is configured. Downstream handlers can observe cancellation, but must cooperate by stopping their own work and canceling supported database/child operations; gRPC does not forcibly interrupt arbitrary application code.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Protobuf field tags\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Field tags are about wire encoding and schema evolution, not about time budgets.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 2,\n    \"workedExample\": \"Work the time budget:\\n\\n```\\nCheckout  deadline = now + 500ms\\n  -> Pricing   (spent 20ms, 480 left)\\n     -> Tax    (spent 50ms, 430 left)\\n        -> FX  (430ms left)\\n```\\n\\nEach hop passes on whatever time is left:\\n\\n```formula\\nT_left = D − T_used\\nT_left = 500 ms − 20 ms = 480 ms\\nT_left = 480 ms − 50 ms = 430 ms\\nwhere\\nT_left: the time remaining before the deadline when the next call starts\\nD: the time that was left when this hop received the call (500 ms at Checkout)\\nT_used: the time this hop has already spent\\nms: milliseconds, thousandths of a second\\nIn words: the deadline is fixed, so every hop gets only what its callers have not used up.\\n```\\n\\nIf Pricing stalls and 500 ms elapses, the deadline expires everywhere: Checkout returns `DEADLINE_EXCEEDED`, and Tax and FX can observe cancellation and must stop their work cooperatively. Without propagation, each hop would have its own independent (often longer) timeout and keep working for nobody.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"gRPC: Core concepts\",\n      \"url\": \"https://grpc.io/docs/what-is-grpc/core-concepts/\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q7\",\n    \"level\": \"advanced\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"A ride-hailing team explicitly wants (a) resource-oriented HTTP endpoints callable with ordinary tools for partners, (b) UI-specific response composition, and (c) generated protobuf RPC stubs and streaming internally. Which combination most directly fits these chosen constraints?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"gRPC for all three, for consistency\",\n        \"explanation\": \"gRPC is poor for (a): partners would need protobuf tooling and couldn't use plain HTTP clients. It's also awkward for the mobile web parts of (b) without gRPC-Web proxies.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"(a) REST, (b) GraphQL or a BFF, (c) gRPC\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. Partners want something any language can call with documented, cacheable endpoints (REST + OpenAPI). The app benefits from fetching exactly the shape each screen needs (GraphQL or a BFF). High-volume internal calls benefit from protobuf's small payloads, generated stubs, deadlines and HTTP/2 multiplexing (gRPC).\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"(a) GraphQL, (b) REST, (c) REST\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Public GraphQL and internal REST are valid designs, but this option does not provide the explicitly requested internal protobuf RPC stubs and gRPC streaming contract. No universal CPU or latency comparison follows from a request rate alone.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"REST for all three, because it's the most widely understood\",\n        \"explanation\": \"REST can use compound responses, streaming HTTP and application deadlines. This option still does not meet the chosen requirement for generated protobuf RPC stubs and gRPC streaming internally.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 1,\n    \"workedExample\": \"Match each consumer to the style's strengths:\\n\\n| Consumer | Needs | Fit |\\n|---|---|---|\\n| Partners | Easy, stable, any language | REST |\\n| Rider app | Few round trips, varied shapes | GraphQL/BFF |\\n| Internal | Low latency, typed, high QPS | gRPC |\\n\\nThe combination follows the stated interface requirements. Actual payload savings, CPU cost and latency need workload-specific measurement; the earlier 60% byte saving is not universal.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"gRPC: Core concepts\",\n      \"url\": \"https://grpc.io/docs/what-is-grpc/core-concepts/\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q11\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"Your internal `Inventory` service speaks gRPC. The web team now wants to call it directly from a React app in the browser. What do they need?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Nothing: modern browsers use HTTP/2, so they can call gRPC endpoints directly\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Browsers do use HTTP/2, but JavaScript can't control HTTP/2 framing or read the trailers gRPC uses for its status, so raw gRPC isn't callable from page code.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Convert the service to GraphQL first\",\n        \"explanation\": \"That's a much bigger change than needed. GraphQL might suit a UI-facing gateway, but it isn't required just to reach a gRPC service from a browser.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Open a WebSocket to each gRPC backend\",\n        \"explanation\": \"WebSockets are a different protocol. gRPC servers don't accept WebSocket connections, and you'd lose generated stubs and gRPC semantics.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"gRPC-Web (or a browser-compatible protocol such as Connect), typically with a proxy such as Envoy translating to native gRPC\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. gRPC-Web carries gRPC semantics in a form browsers can send and read, and a proxy translates it to normal gRPC for the backend. You keep generated, typed clients.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 3,\n    \"workedExample\": \"1. gRPC depends on HTTP/2 details: framing, and **trailers** carrying `grpc-status`.\\n2. Browser `fetch` gives page code no control over framing and no reliable access to trailers.\\n3. gRPC-Web moves the status into the response body and works over HTTP/1.1 or 2.\\n4. A proxy (commonly Envoy) translates gRPC-Web to native gRPC:\\n\\n```\\nBrowser --gRPC-Web--> Envoy\\nEnvoy --gRPC (HTTP/2)--> Inventory\\n```\\n\\nNote that gRPC-Web supports unary and server streaming, but not client or bidirectional streaming.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"gRPC: Core concepts\",\n      \"url\": \"https://grpc.io/docs/what-is-grpc/core-concepts/\"\n    }\n  }\n]\n","after":"[\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q5\",\n    \"level\": \"advanced\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"You migrate internal service calls from REST/JSON to gRPC. Behind an L4 (TCP) load balancer with 10 backends, you notice one backend at 90% CPU and the others nearly idle. What's the most likely cause?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Protobuf is slower to parse than JSON\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Parsing performance depends on workload and implementation. It would not by itself explain why an L4 balancer pins a high-volume connection to one backend.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"gRPC multiplexes many calls over one long-lived HTTP/2 connection, and an L4 balancer only balances connections, so all of a client's requests stick to one backend\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct under the stated connection-level balancer. Each TCP connection is assigned to a backend; a long-lived gRPC connection can carry many calls to that backend. HTTP/1.1 also supports persistent pooled connections, so it does not guarantee an even distribution. L7 or client-side balancing can distribute calls.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"gRPC deadlines are concentrating retries on one server\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Deadlines cause calls to fail fast when time runs out. They don't steer traffic to a particular backend.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"The backend is running a different protobuf version\",\n        \"explanation\": \"A version mismatch could cause decoding issues, but it wouldn't attract a disproportionate share of traffic to one host.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 1,\n    \"workedExample\": \"1. L4 balancing decides at **TCP connect** time.\\n2. With HTTP/1.1, clients typically open a pool of connections and recycle them, so load spreads.\\n3. gRPC/HTTP/2 multiplexes: one connection carries hundreds of concurrent streams and lives for hours.\\n4. If a few large callers each hold one connection, those connections land on one or two backends, and stay there.\\n\\nFixes:\\n- L7 proxy (Envoy, Linkerd, gRPC-aware cloud LBs) that balances per **request**.\\n- Client-side load balancing: the client resolves all backend addresses and round-robins calls.\\n- Set a max connection age so connections are periodically re-established.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"gRPC: Performance best practices\",\n      \"url\": \"https://grpc.io/docs/guides/performance/\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q6\",\n    \"level\": \"advanced\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"A checkout service calls Pricing (which calls Tax, which calls a Currency service). The user-facing SLA is 500 ms. Pricing sometimes hangs for 5 s, and Tax keeps working on requests long after the user has given up. Which gRPC feature most directly addresses this wasted work?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Bidirectional streaming\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Streaming changes the shape of the conversation (many messages each way), not whether downstream services know the caller has stopped waiting.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Server reflection\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Reflection lets tools like `grpcurl` discover services at runtime. It has nothing to do with timeouts.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Deadline propagation: Checkout sets a 500 ms deadline that flows to Pricing, Tax and Currency, each of which can cancel when the remaining time hits zero\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct when deadline propagation is configured. Downstream handlers can observe cancellation, but must cooperate by stopping their own work and canceling supported database/child operations; gRPC does not forcibly interrupt arbitrary application code.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Protobuf field tags\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Field tags are about wire encoding and protobuf schema evolution, not about time budgets.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 2,\n    \"workedExample\": \"Work the time budget:\\n\\n```\\nCheckout  deadline = now + 500ms\\n  -> Pricing   (spent 20ms, 480 left)\\n     -> Tax    (spent 50ms, 430 left)\\n        -> FX  (430ms left)\\n```\\n\\nEach hop passes on whatever time is left:\\n\\n```formula\\nT_left = D − T_used\\nT_left = 500 ms − 20 ms = 480 ms\\nT_left = 480 ms − 50 ms = 430 ms\\nwhere\\nT_left: the time remaining before the deadline when the next call starts\\nD: the time that was left when this hop received the call (500 ms at Checkout)\\nT_used: the time this hop has already spent\\nms: milliseconds, thousandths of a second\\nIn words: the deadline is fixed, so every hop gets only what its callers have not used up.\\n```\\n\\nIf Pricing stalls and 500 ms elapses, the deadline expires everywhere: Checkout returns `DEADLINE_EXCEEDED`, and Tax and FX can observe cancellation and must stop their work cooperatively. Without propagation, each hop would have its own independent (often longer) timeout and keep working for nobody.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"gRPC: Core concepts\",\n      \"url\": \"https://grpc.io/docs/what-is-grpc/core-concepts/\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q7\",\n    \"level\": \"advanced\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"A ride-hailing team explicitly wants (a) resource-oriented HTTP endpoints callable with ordinary tools for partners, (b) UI-specific response composition, and (c) generated protobuf RPC stubs and streaming internally. Which combination most directly fits these chosen constraints?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"gRPC for all three, for consistency\",\n        \"explanation\": \"gRPC is poor for (a): partners would need protobuf tooling and couldn't use plain HTTP clients. It's also awkward for the mobile web parts of (b) without gRPC-Web proxies.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"(a) REST, (b) GraphQL or a BFF, (c) gRPC\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. Partners want something any language can call with documented, cacheable endpoints (REST + OpenAPI). The app benefits from fetching exactly the shape each screen needs (GraphQL or a BFF). High-volume internal calls benefit from protobuf's small payloads, generated stubs, deadlines and HTTP/2 multiplexing (gRPC).\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"(a) GraphQL, (b) REST, (c) REST\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Public GraphQL and internal REST are valid designs, but this option does not provide the explicitly requested internal protobuf RPC stubs and gRPC streaming contract. No universal CPU or latency comparison follows from a request rate alone.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"REST for all three, because it's the most widely understood\",\n        \"explanation\": \"REST can use compound responses, streaming HTTP and application deadlines. This option still does not meet the chosen requirement for generated protobuf RPC stubs and gRPC streaming internally.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 1,\n    \"workedExample\": \"Match each consumer to the style's strengths:\\n\\n| Consumer | Needs | Fit |\\n|---|---|---|\\n| Partners | Easy, stable, any language | REST |\\n| Rider app | Few round trips, varied shapes | GraphQL/BFF |\\n| Internal | Low latency, typed, high QPS | gRPC |\\n\\nThe combination follows the stated interface requirements. Actual payload savings, CPU cost and latency need workload-specific measurement; the earlier 60% byte saving is not universal.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"gRPC: Core concepts\",\n      \"url\": \"https://grpc.io/docs/what-is-grpc/core-concepts/\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q11\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"Your internal `Inventory` service speaks gRPC. The web team now wants to call it directly from a React app in the browser. What do they need?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Nothing: modern browsers use HTTP/2, so they can call gRPC endpoints directly\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Browsers do use HTTP/2, but JavaScript can't control HTTP/2 framing or read the trailers gRPC uses for its status, so raw gRPC isn't callable from page code.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Convert the service to GraphQL first\",\n        \"explanation\": \"That's a much bigger change than needed. GraphQL might suit a UI-facing gateway, but it isn't required just to reach a gRPC service from a browser.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Open a WebSocket to each gRPC backend\",\n        \"explanation\": \"WebSockets are a different protocol. gRPC servers don't accept WebSocket connections, and you'd lose generated stubs and gRPC semantics.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"gRPC-Web (or a browser-compatible protocol such as Connect), typically with a proxy such as Envoy translating to native gRPC\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. gRPC-Web carries gRPC semantics in a form browsers can send and read, and a proxy translates it to normal gRPC for the backend. You keep generated, typed clients.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 3,\n    \"workedExample\": \"1. gRPC depends on HTTP/2 details: framing, and **trailers** carrying `grpc-status`.\\n2. Browser `fetch` gives page code no control over framing and no reliable access to trailers.\\n3. gRPC-Web moves the status into the response body and works over HTTP/1.1 or 2.\\n4. A proxy (commonly Envoy) translates gRPC-Web to native gRPC:\\n\\n```\\nBrowser --gRPC-Web--> Envoy\\nEnvoy --gRPC (HTTP/2)--> Inventory\\n```\\n\\nNote that gRPC-Web supports unary and server streaming, but not client or bidirectional streaming.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"gRPC: Core concepts\",\n      \"url\": \"https://grpc.io/docs/what-is-grpc/core-concepts/\"\n    }\n  }\n]\n"}
- {"file":"api-session-token-revocation.md","reason":"Resolve dictionary words to their intended local meaning","before":"---\nid: api-session-token-revocation\ntitle: Sessions, tokens and revocation\nlevel: advanced\nminutes: 3\nsummary: Separate identity from permission and compare authoritative session lookups with self-contained tokens.\nsourceLesson: api-auth\n---\n\nTwo questions sit in front of every API call:\n\n- **Authentication (authn)**: *who* is calling?\n- **Authorisation (authz)**: *are they allowed* to do this?\n\nThey're separate concerns and often separate systems. A valid identity tells you nothing about permissions. Mixing them up is behind a large share of real-world API breaches; \"broken object level authorisation\" (checking that the user is logged in, but not that the invoice is *theirs*) tops the OWASP API Security Top 10.\n\n## Sessions vs tokens\n\n## Server-side sessions\n\nThe classic web approach. After login, the server creates a session record and gives the browser an opaque, random session id in a cookie.\n\n```http\nSet-Cookie: sid=9f8c...; HttpOnly;\n  Secure; SameSite=Lax; Path=/\n```\n\nOn each request the server looks up `sid` in a shared store (Redis, a database) to find the user.\n\n- **Session revocation applies at validation**: delete that session record and subsequent validated requests using that session are rejected. Other device sessions require separate revocation, and cached sessions can delay enforcement.\n- **The cookie reveals nothing**: it's just a random pointer.\n- **Cost**: a store lookup per request, and the store must be shared across servers (to stay stateless at the app tier).\n\n## Self-contained tokens\n\nThe token *itself* carries the claims, signed so the server can verify it without a lookup. JWT is the common format.\n\n- **No lookup**: with asymmetric signing, a service with the trusted public key can verify its signature and then validate its required claims. Great for microservices.\n- **Hard to revoke**: without additional revocation enforcement, a stolen token remains usable until expiry.\n\n| | Session id | JWT |\n|---|---|---|\n| Verify | Store lookup | Signature check |\n| Revoke | On next authoritative session validation; caches affect delay | Expiry or added revocation/status mechanism |\n| Size | Chosen random identifier plus encoding overhead | Depends on claims, signature algorithm and encoding |\n| Cross-service | Shared authoritative state | Trusted verification key and profile checks |\n\nNeither is universally better. Many systems use **both**: an opaque session cookie at the edge for browsers, translated by the gateway into a short-lived internal JWT for service-to-service calls.\n\nA short-lived access token plus a refresh token checked against current account state narrows the period after an account is disabled. It does not give instant revocation: a token issued immediately before disabling can remain valid for its remaining lifetime unless the API enforces a revocation mechanism.\n\n## Key takeaways\n- Authentication establishes identity; authorisation still checks the requested object.\n- An opaque session identifier points to server-side state; authoritative validation enforces revocation.\n- A self-contained token verifies locally but remains valid until expiry unless revocation is enforced.\n- Short access tokens plus checked refreshes reduce stale-access exposure.\n\n## Further reading\n- [OWASP: Session Management Cheat Sheet](https://cheatsheetseries.owasp.org/cheatsheets/Session_Management_Cheat_Sheet.html)\n- [RFC 8725: JWT Best Current Practices](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8725)\n","after":"---\nid: api-session-token-revocation\ntitle: Sessions, tokens and credential revocation\nlevel: advanced\nminutes: 3\nsummary: Separate identity from permission and compare authoritative session lookups with self-contained tokens.\nsourceLesson: api-auth\n---\n\nTwo questions sit in front of every API call:\n\n- **Authentication (authn)**: *who* is calling?\n- **Authorisation (authz)**: *are they allowed* to do this?\n\nThey're separate concerns and often separate systems. A valid identity tells you nothing about permissions. Mixing them up is behind a large share of real-world API breaches; \"broken object level authorisation\" (checking that the user is logged in, but not that the invoice is *theirs*) tops the OWASP API Security Top 10.\n\n## Sessions vs tokens\n\n## Server-side sessions\n\nThe classic web approach. After login, the server creates a session record and gives the browser an opaque, random session id in a cookie.\n\n```http\nSet-Cookie: sid=9f8c...; HttpOnly;\n  Secure; SameSite=Lax; Path=/\n```\n\nOn each request the server looks up `sid` in a shared store (Redis, a database) to find the user.\n\n- **Session credential revocation applies at validation**: delete that session record and subsequent validated requests using that session are rejected. Other device sessions require separate credential revocation, and cached sessions can delay enforcement.\n- **The cookie reveals nothing**: it's just a random pointer.\n- **Cost**: a store lookup per request, and the store must be shared across servers (to stay stateless at the app tier).\n\n## Self-contained tokens\n\nThe token *itself* carries the claims, signed so the server can verify it without a lookup. JWT is the common format.\n\n- **No lookup**: with asymmetric signing, a service with the trusted public key can verify its signature and then validate its required claims. Great for microservices.\n- **Hard to revoke**: without additional credential revocation enforcement, a stolen token remains usable until expiry.\n\n| | Session id | JWT |\n|---|---|---|\n| Verify | Store lookup | Signature check |\n| Revoke | On next authoritative session validation; caches affect delay | Expiry or added credential revocation/status mechanism |\n| Size | Chosen random identifier plus encoding overhead | Depends on claims, signature algorithm and encoding |\n| Cross-service | Shared authoritative state | Trusted verification key and profile checks |\n\nNeither is universally better. Many systems use **both**: an opaque session cookie at the edge for browsers, translated by the gateway into a short-lived internal JWT for service-to-service calls.\n\nA short-lived access token plus a refresh token checked against current account state narrows the period after an account is disabled. It does not give instant credential revocation: a token issued immediately before disabling can remain valid for its remaining lifetime unless the API enforces a credential revocation mechanism.\n\n## Key takeaways\n- Authentication establishes identity; authorisation still checks the requested object.\n- An opaque session identifier points to server-side state; authoritative validation enforces credential revocation.\n- A self-contained token verifies locally but remains valid until expiry unless credential revocation is enforced.\n- Short access tokens plus checked refreshes reduce stale-access exposure.\n\n## Further reading\n- [OWASP: Session Management Cheat Sheet](https://cheatsheetseries.owasp.org/cheatsheets/Session_Management_Cheat_Sheet.html)\n- [RFC 8725: JWT Best Current Practices](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8725)\n"}
- {"file":"api-session-token-revocation.questions.json","reason":"Resolve dictionary words to their intended local meaning","before":"[\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-auth-q6\",\n    \"level\": \"advanced\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"An account is disabled at 09:00. Its stateless JWT access token was issued at 08:30 and expires eight hours later. The API validates signature and claims, including exp, but does no revocation/account lookup. What exposure remains, and what reduces it without a per-request database lookup?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"No exposure; disabling the account invalidates the JWT\",\n        \"explanation\": \"A stateless JWT is verified purely by its signature and claims. The API never consults the account table, so disabling the account changes nothing until expiry.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Until 16:30. Shorten access tokens to ~10 minutes and use refresh tokens that are checked against the account on each refresh\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct under the stated validation policy. Short access-token lifetimes reduce exposure to at most about ten minutes if refreshes check the current account state. A jti deny-list can enforce earlier token revocation, subject to propagation and cache delays.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Until 16:30. Switch from RS256 to HS256\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Changing the signing algorithm doesn't affect lifetime or revocation. It would also make every verifier capable of forging tokens.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Until 16:30. Rotate the signing key\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Issuing a new signing key alone does not invalidate tokens signed by a still-trusted old key. Removing the old verification key from every verifier can invalidate all tokens using it, with propagation/cache delays and broad disruption.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 1,\n    \"workedExample\": \"```formula\\nT_exp = T_iss + L\\nT_exp = 08:30 + 8 h = 16:30\\nE = T_exp − T_dis\\nE = 16:30 − 09:00 = 7.5 h\\nwhere\\nT_exp: the token's expiry time, its exp claim\\nT_iss: the time the token was issued, 08:30\\nL: the token's lifetime, 8 hours\\nE: the exposure window: how long the token still works after the account is disabled\\nT_dis: the time the account was disabled, 09:00\\nh: hours\\nIn words: the token outlives the account by **7.5 hours**, because nothing checks the account until **16:30**.\\n```\\n\\nWith 10-minute access tokens, the worst case is a token issued just before 09:00, so the exposure is at most the lifetime: ≤ **10 minutes**. At 09:10 the client tries to refresh; the auth server sees the account is disabled and refuses.\\n\\nCost: one auth-server check every 10 minutes per client, instead of one DB lookup per API request. If you need sub-minute revocation (a compromised admin), push the `jti` to a deny-list cached on every gateway.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"RFC 8725: JWT Best Current Practices\",\n      \"url\": \"https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8725\"\n    }\n  }\n]\n","after":"[\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-auth-q6\",\n    \"level\": \"advanced\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"An account is disabled at 09:00. Its stateless JWT access token was issued at 08:30 and expires eight hours later. The API validates signature and claims, including exp, but does no credential revocation/account lookup. What exposure remains, and what reduces it without a per-request database lookup?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"No exposure; disabling the account invalidates the JWT\",\n        \"explanation\": \"A stateless JWT is verified purely by its signature and claims. The API never consults the account table, so disabling the account changes nothing until expiry.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Until 16:30. Shorten access tokens to ~10 minutes and use refresh tokens that are checked against the account on each refresh\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct under the stated validation policy. Short access-token lifetimes reduce exposure to at most about ten minutes if refreshes check the current account state. A jti deny-list can enforce earlier token credential revocation, subject to propagation and cache delays.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Until 16:30. Switch from RS256 to HS256\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Changing the signing algorithm doesn't affect lifetime or credential revocation. It would also make every verifier capable of forging tokens.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Until 16:30. Rotate the signing key\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Issuing a new signing key alone does not invalidate tokens signed by a still-trusted old key. Removing the old verification key from every verifier can invalidate all tokens using it, with propagation/cache delays and broad disruption.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 1,\n    \"workedExample\": \"```formula\\nT_exp = T_iss + L\\nT_exp = 08:30 + 8 h = 16:30\\nE = T_exp − T_dis\\nE = 16:30 − 09:00 = 7.5 h\\nwhere\\nT_exp: the token's expiry time, its exp claim\\nT_iss: the time the token was issued, 08:30\\nL: the token's lifetime, 8 hours\\nE: the exposure window: how long the token still works after the account is disabled\\nT_dis: the time the account was disabled, 09:00\\nh: hours\\nIn words: the token outlives the account by **7.5 hours**, because nothing checks the account until **16:30**.\\n```\\n\\nWith 10-minute access tokens, the worst case is a token issued just before 09:00, so the exposure is at most the lifetime: ≤ **10 minutes**. At 09:10 the client tries to refresh; the auth server sees the account is disabled and refuses.\\n\\nCost: one auth-server check every 10 minutes per client, instead of one DB lookup per API request. If you need sub-minute credential revocation (a compromised admin), push the `jti` to a deny-list cached on every gateway.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"RFC 8725: JWT Best Current Practices\",\n      \"url\": \"https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8725\"\n    }\n  }\n]\n"}
- {"file":"api-jwt-validation-pitfalls.md","reason":"Resolve dictionary words to their intended local meaning","before":"---\nid: api-jwt-validation-pitfalls\ntitle: JWT validation and storage pitfalls\nlevel: advanced\nminutes: 3\nsummary: Treat signed JWT claims as readable and pin algorithms, trusted keys and required claims.\nsourceLesson: api-auth\n---\n\n## JWT in detail\n\nA signed JWT in JWS compact form uses three base64url parts joined by dots. JWTs can also use encrypted JWE forms; this section covers the common signed form:\n\n```\nheader.payload.signature\n\n{\"alg\":\"RS256\",\"kid\":\"k1\"}\n.\n{\"sub\":\"user_42\",\n \"iss\":\"https://auth.example\",\n \"aud\":\"orders-api\",\n \"exp\":1767225600,\n \"scope\":\"orders:read\"}\n.\n<RSA signature over the first two>\n```\n\n`exp` is the expiry time as a Unix timestamp, a count of seconds since 1 January 1970 UTC: 1767225600 is midnight UTC on 1 January 2026.\n\nThe payload is **encoded, not encrypted**. Anyone holding the token can read it. Never put secrets or sensitive personal data in it.\n\nSigning choices:\n\n- **HS256** (HMAC): one shared secret signs and verifies. Every verifier can also *forge* tokens.\n- **RS256 / ES256** (asymmetric): the auth server signs with a private key; services verify with the public key, published at a JWKS endpoint. Verifiers can't mint tokens. Prefer this across services.\n\n## JWT pitfalls\n\nRFC 8725 (JWT Best Current Practices) exists because these mistakes keep happening:\n\n1. **`alg: none`.** Early libraries accepted unsigned tokens if the header said `\"alg\": \"none\"`. Always pin the expected algorithm on the verifier; never trust the header.\n1. **Algorithm confusion.** A server expects RS256 but the library honours the header's `HS256`, then verifies the HMAC using the RSA *public* key as the secret. The attacker, who knows the public key, can forge tokens.\n1. **Not validating claims.** Validate the claims required by your token profile, including expiry, issuer and audience, and enforce `nbf` when present. A token issued for `billing-api` must not be accepted by `admin-api`.\n1. **Trusting key hints in the header.** `kid`, `jku` and `x5u` tell the verifier which key to use, or where to fetch it. If the verifier fetches keys from whatever URL the token names, the attacker just points it at their own key. Only look `kid` up in your own allow-listed JWKS.\n1. **Long lifetimes.** Since you can't easily revoke, keep access tokens short (typically 5–15 minutes) and use refresh tokens.\n1. **Storage in the browser.** `localStorage` is readable by any script on the page, so one XSS bug leaks every token. An `HttpOnly` cookie can't be read by JavaScript (though you then need CSRF protection).\n1. **Using a JWT as a session.** Stuffing roles into a 24-hour JWT means a demoted admin stays an admin for a day.\n\n> [!tip] Revocation strategies\n> Short access-token lifetime plus a refresh token that *is* checked against a database on each refresh. For urgent revocation, keep a small deny-list of `jti` (token id) values until they expire. That's a lookup, but on a tiny, cacheable set.\n\n## Key takeaways\n- JWS compact tokens have three encoded parts; signatures provide integrity rather than secrecy.\n- Pin accepted algorithms and bind trusted keys to them; never trust arbitrary key URLs.\n- Validate the token profile’s issuer, audience, expiry and any not-before claim.\n- Short lifetimes, refresh checks and appropriate browser storage reduce theft and stale roles.\n\n## Further reading\n- [RFC 7519: JSON Web Token](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7519)\n- [RFC 8725: JWT Best Current Practices](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8725)\n","after":"---\nid: api-jwt-validation-pitfalls\ntitle: JWT validation and storage pitfalls\nlevel: advanced\nminutes: 3\nsummary: Treat signed JWT claims as readable and pin algorithms, trusted keys and required claims.\nsourceLesson: api-auth\n---\n\n## JWT in detail\n\nA signed JWT in JWS compact form uses three base64url parts joined by dots. JWTs can also use encrypted JWE forms; this section covers the common signed form:\n\n```\nheader.payload.signature\n\n{\"alg\":\"RS256\",\"kid\":\"k1\"}\n.\n{\"sub\":\"user_42\",\n \"iss\":\"https://auth.example\",\n \"aud\":\"orders-api\",\n \"exp\":1767225600,\n \"scope\":\"orders:read\"}\n.\n<RSA signature over the first two>\n```\n\n`exp` is the expiry time as a Unix timestamp, a count of seconds since 1 January 1970 UTC: 1767225600 is midnight UTC on 1 January 2026.\n\nThe payload is **encoded, not encrypted**. Anyone holding the token can read it. Never put secrets or sensitive personal data in it.\n\nSigning choices:\n\n- **HS256** (HMAC): one shared secret signs and verifies. Every verifier can also *forge* tokens.\n- **RS256 / ES256** (asymmetric): the auth server signs with a private key; services verify with the public key, published at a JWKS endpoint. Verifiers can't mint tokens. Prefer this across services.\n\n## JWT pitfalls\n\nRFC 8725 (JWT Best Current Practices) exists because these mistakes keep happening:\n\n1. **`alg: none`.** Early libraries accepted unsigned tokens if the header said `\"alg\": \"none\"`. Always pin the expected algorithm on the verifier; never trust the header.\n1. **Algorithm confusion.** A server expects RS256 but the library honours the header's `HS256`, then verifies the HMAC using the RSA *public* key as the secret. The attacker, who knows the public key, can forge tokens.\n1. **Not validating claims.** Validate the claims required by your token profile, including expiry, issuer and audience, and enforce `nbf` when present. A token issued for `billing-api` must not be accepted by `admin-api`.\n1. **Trusting key hints in the header.** `kid`, `jku` and `x5u` tell the verifier which key to use, or where to fetch it. If the verifier fetches keys from whatever URL the token names, the attacker just points it at their own key. Only look `kid` up in your own allow-listed JWKS.\n1. **Long lifetimes.** Since you can't easily revoke, keep access tokens short (typically 5–15 minutes) and use refresh tokens.\n1. **Storage in the browser.** `localStorage` is readable by any script on the page, so one XSS bug leaks every token. An `HttpOnly` cookie can't be read by JavaScript (though you then need CSRF protection).\n1. **Using a JWT as a session.** Stuffing roles into a 24-hour JWT means a demoted admin stays an admin for a day.\n\n> [!tip] credential revocation strategies\n> Short access-token lifetime plus a refresh token that *is* checked against a database on each refresh. For urgent credential revocation, keep a small deny-list of `jti` (token id) values until they expire. That's a lookup, but on a tiny, cacheable set.\n\n## Key takeaways\n- JWS compact tokens have three encoded parts; signatures provide integrity rather than secrecy.\n- Pin accepted algorithms and bind trusted keys to them; never trust arbitrary key URLs.\n- Validate the token profile’s issuer, audience, expiry and any not-before claim.\n- Short lifetimes, refresh checks and appropriate browser storage reduce theft and stale roles.\n\n## Further reading\n- [RFC 7519: JSON Web Token](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7519)\n- [RFC 8725: JWT Best Current Practices](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8725)\n"}
- {"file":"api-oauth-grants-refresh-policy.md","reason":"Resolve dictionary words to their intended local meaning","before":"---\nid: api-oauth-grants-refresh-policy\ntitle: OAuth grants and refresh-token safety\nlevel: advanced\nminutes: 3\nsummary: Choose machine and device grants, detect refresh-token reuse and apply current PKCE requirements.\nsourceLesson: api-auth\n---\n\n## Other grants\n\n- **Client credentials**: machine-to-machine, no user. A backend service exchanges its client id and secret for a token.\n- **Device code**: TVs and CLIs with no browser. The device shows a code; you approve on your phone.\n- **Refresh token**: exchange a long-lived refresh token for a new access token. Use **rotation**: each refresh issues a new refresh token and invalidates the old one, so reuse of a stolen one is detectable.\n\n> [!warning] Deprecated grants\n> The **implicit** grant (access token returned directly in the URL fragment, where it is exposed to browser history and page scripts; fragments are not sent in ordinary HTTP Referer headers) and the **password** grant (client collects the user's password) are out. The OAuth 2.0 Security Best Current Practice, published as RFC 9700 in January 2025, says the implicit grant should not be used and the password grant must not be. OAuth 2.1 (still an IETF draft, consolidating these rules) drops both grants entirely. Use authorisation code + PKCE instead, including for single-page apps.\n\n## PKCE for every client\n\nPKCE was first designed for *public* clients (mobile and single-page apps) that can't keep a client secret. It turned out to protect confidential clients too, against code injection, which a client secret doesn't prevent.\n\n- **RFC 9700**: PKCE is required for public clients and recommended for confidential ones.\n- **OAuth 2.1**: PKCE is required for every client using the authorisation code flow, with only a narrow exception for confidential OpenID Connect clients that use the `nonce` parameter correctly. It also bans the `plain` method and requires exact redirect-URI matching, except for the allowed variable port in native-app loopback redirects.\n\nIn practice: use PKCE with `S256` everywhere, and configure your authorisation server to reject code requests without it.\n\nWhen an invalidated rotated refresh token is presented again, the authorisation server detects possible theft. It cannot tell which participant is legitimate, so revoke the active refresh-token lineage and require a new login. Already issued access tokens need separate revocation or expire according to their lifetime. RFC 9700 requires public-client refresh tokens to use rotation or sender constraints.\n\n## Key takeaways\n- Client credentials suit machines without a user; device code supports limited-input clients.\n- Rotation detects refresh-token reuse; reject the old token and revoke its active refresh lineage.\n- RFC 9700 rules and the OAuth 2.1 draft differ in PKCE requirements; use S256 for code flows.\n- Implicit and password grants are unsuitable for new deployments under the current security guidance.\n\n## Further reading\n- [OAuth 2.0 overview (oauth.net)](https://oauth.net/2/)\n- [RFC 9700: OAuth 2.0 Security Best Current Practice](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9700)\n- [oauth.net: OAuth 2.1](https://oauth.net/2.1/)\n- [IETF: OAuth 2.1 draft](https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-ietf-oauth-v2-1/)\n","after":"---\nid: api-oauth-grants-refresh-policy\ntitle: OAuth grants and refresh-token safety\nlevel: advanced\nminutes: 3\nsummary: Choose machine and device grants, detect refresh-token reuse and apply current PKCE requirements.\nsourceLesson: api-auth\n---\n\n## Other grants\n\n- **Client credentials**: machine-to-machine, no user. A backend service exchanges its client id and secret for a token.\n- **Device code**: TVs and CLIs with no browser. The device shows a code; you approve on your phone.\n- **Refresh token**: exchange a long-lived refresh token for a new access token. Use **rotation**: each refresh issues a new refresh token and invalidates the old one, so reuse of a stolen one is detectable.\n\n> [!warning] Deprecated grants\n> The **implicit** grant (access token returned directly in the URL fragment, where it is exposed to browser history and page scripts; fragments are not sent in ordinary HTTP Referer headers) and the **password** grant (client collects the user's password) are out. The OAuth 2.0 Security Best Current Practice, published as RFC 9700 in January 2025, says the implicit grant should not be used and the password grant must not be. OAuth 2.1 (still an IETF draft, consolidating these rules) drops both grants entirely. Use authorisation code + PKCE instead, including for single-page apps.\n\n## PKCE for every client\n\nPKCE was first designed for *public* clients (mobile and single-page apps) that can't keep a client secret. It turned out to protect confidential clients too, against code injection, which a client secret doesn't prevent.\n\n- **RFC 9700**: PKCE is required for public clients and recommended for confidential ones.\n- **OAuth 2.1**: PKCE is required for every client using the authorisation code flow, with only a narrow exception for confidential OpenID Connect clients that use the `nonce` parameter correctly. It also bans the `plain` method and requires exact redirect-URI matching, except for the allowed variable port in native-app loopback redirects.\n\nIn practice: use PKCE with `S256` everywhere, and configure your authorisation server to reject code requests without it.\n\nWhen an invalidated rotated refresh token is presented again, the authorisation server detects possible theft. It cannot tell which participant is legitimate, so revoke the active refresh-token lineage and require a new login. Already issued access tokens need separate credential revocation or expire according to their lifetime. RFC 9700 requires public-client refresh tokens to use rotation or sender constraints.\n\n## Key takeaways\n- Client credentials suit machines without a user; device code supports limited-input clients.\n- Rotation detects refresh-token reuse; reject the old token and revoke its active refresh lineage.\n- RFC 9700 rules and the OAuth 2.1 draft differ in PKCE requirements; use S256 for code flows.\n- Implicit and password grants are unsuitable for new deployments under the current security guidance.\n\n## Further reading\n- [OAuth 2.0 overview (oauth.net)](https://oauth.net/2/)\n- [RFC 9700: OAuth 2.0 Security Best Current Practice](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9700)\n- [oauth.net: OAuth 2.1](https://oauth.net/2.1/)\n- [IETF: OAuth 2.1 draft](https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-ietf-oauth-v2-1/)\n"}
- {"file":"api-oauth-grants-refresh-policy.questions.json","reason":"Resolve dictionary words to their intended local meaning","before":"[\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-auth-q4\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"A nightly batch job in your billing service needs to call your internal reporting API. No user is involved. Which OAuth 2.0 grant fits?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Authorisation code with PKCE\",\n        \"explanation\": \"That flow needs a user to log in and consent via a browser. There's no user in a nightly batch job.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Implicit grant\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Implicit is deprecated and was designed for browser apps with a user. It's wrong on both counts here.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Resource owner password grant\",\n        \"explanation\": \"This grant collects a user's password, which is deprecated and irrelevant when no user exists.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Client credentials\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. The service authenticates as itself with its client id and secret (or a private-key JWT or mTLS) and receives an access token scoped to what the service, not a user, may do.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 3,\n    \"workedExample\": \"Decision tree:\\n\\n```\\nIs a user involved?\\n  yes -> has a browser?\\n           yes -> auth code + PKCE\\n           no  -> device code\\n  no  -> client credentials\\n```\\n\\nRequest:\\n\\n```\\nPOST /token\\ngrant_type=client_credentials\\n&client_id=billing\\n&client_secret=...\\n&scope=reports:read\\n```\\n\\nThe token authorizes the billing client under the server's contract; OAuth does not require every access token to be a JWT or contain a sub claim.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"oauth.net: Client credentials\",\n      \"url\": \"https://oauth.net/2/grant-types/client-credentials/\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-auth-q11\",\n    \"level\": \"advanced\",\n    \"type\": \"multi\",\n    \"prompt\": \"A traditional server-rendered web app is a **confidential** OAuth client with a client secret. You're aligning it with RFC 9700 and OAuth 2.1. Which statements are correct? Select all that apply.\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"It should use PKCE too: OAuth 2.1 requires it for confidential clients, apart from a narrow OIDC `nonce` exception\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. PKCE is no longer just for public clients. Even where the exception applies, OAuth 2.1 still recommends using it.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Its client secret already stops a stolen authorisation code being used, so PKCE adds nothing\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Wrong. In a code injection attack, the attacker plants a stolen code into the *legitimate* client's callback, and that client redeems it with its own secret. PKCE stops this, because the code is bound to a verifier that client never issued.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Use `code_challenge_method=S256`, not `plain`\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. With `plain` the challenge *is* the verifier, so anyone who sees the authorisation request learns it. OAuth 2.1 prohibits `plain`.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Because it's confidential, the implicit grant is acceptable for it\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Wrong. Implicit is deprecated for every client type. Its problem is tokens in the URL, not whether the client has a secret.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"For this web client, the authorization server should match redirect URIs exactly against registered values\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. Pattern or prefix matching has repeatedly let attackers redirect codes to URLs they control. RFC 9700 and OAuth 2.1 require exact string matching.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": [\n      0,\n      2,\n      4\n    ],\n    \"workedExample\": \"**Code injection**, step by step, against a client that has a secret but no PKCE:\\n\\n1. The attacker steals a code issued to the *victim* (e.g. from a leaky log).\\n2. The attacker starts a login on the real client in their own browser, then swaps the stolen code into the callback.\\n3. The client redeems it with its own valid secret. The attacker's session is now logged in as the victim.\\n\\nWith PKCE, the code was bound to the victim's `code_challenge`. The attacker's session holds a different verifier, so redemption fails.\\n\\nThe other rules: `S256` keeps the verifier secret, exact redirect matching keeps codes from going astray, and implicit is out for everyone.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"oauth.net: OAuth 2.1\",\n      \"url\": \"https://oauth.net/2.1/\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-auth-q12\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"Your auth server rotates refresh tokens. A user's refresh token R1 is stolen. The legitimate app refreshes first, receiving R2 (and R1 is invalidated). An hour later the attacker presents R1. What should the server do?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Reject R1 and do nothing else, since it has already been used\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Rejecting is right, but it wastes the signal. Reuse of a rotated token signals possible compromise (or a duplicate legitimate attempt), and the server can't tell which, so R2 should be distrusted too.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Accept R1, because refresh tokens are long-lived\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Accepting an already-rotated token defeats the entire point of rotation, which is to make each refresh token single-use.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Issue a new access token but no new refresh token\",\n        \"explanation\": \"That still hands the attacker an access token. A rotated refresh token should never produce anything.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Treat it as theft: reject R1 and revoke the whole token family, including R2, so the user must log in again\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct for the stated theft scenario: reject reuse and revoke the active refresh-token lineage. Reuse is a compromise signal, though duplicate legitimate refresh attempts can also cause it. Already issued self-contained access tokens need separate revocation enforcement or expire naturally.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 3,\n    \"workedExample\": \"Timeline:\\n\\n```\\n09:00  issue R1\\n09:05  attacker copies R1\\n09:30  app uses R1 -> gets R2\\n       R1 marked used\\n10:30  attacker uses R1\\n       -> REUSE DETECTED\\n       revoke the refresh lineage\\n       access tokens: revoke separately\\n       or wait for their short expiry\\n```\\n\\nIf the *attacker* had refreshed first, the legitimate app's later use of R1 would trigger the same detection. Either way, the reuse is caught and the stolen chain dies. RFC 9700 requires refresh tokens for public clients to be either rotated like this or sender-constrained (e.g. with DPoP or mTLS).\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"RFC 9700: OAuth 2.0 Security Best Current Practice\",\n      \"url\": \"https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9700\"\n    }\n  }\n]\n","after":"[\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-auth-q4\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"A nightly batch job in your billing service needs to call your internal reporting API. No user is involved. Which OAuth 2.0 grant fits?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Authorisation code with PKCE\",\n        \"explanation\": \"That flow needs a user to log in and consent via a browser. There's no user in a nightly batch job.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Implicit grant\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Implicit is deprecated and was designed for browser apps with a user. It's wrong on both counts here.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Resource owner password grant\",\n        \"explanation\": \"This grant collects a user's password, which is deprecated and irrelevant when no user exists.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Client credentials\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. The service authenticates as itself with its client id and secret (or a private-key JWT or mTLS) and receives an access token scoped to what the service, not a user, may do.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 3,\n    \"workedExample\": \"Decision tree:\\n\\n```\\nIs a user involved?\\n  yes -> has a browser?\\n           yes -> auth code + PKCE\\n           no  -> device code\\n  no  -> client credentials\\n```\\n\\nRequest:\\n\\n```\\nPOST /token\\ngrant_type=client_credentials\\n&client_id=billing\\n&client_secret=...\\n&scope=reports:read\\n```\\n\\nThe token authorizes the billing client under the server's contract; OAuth does not require every access token to be a JWT or contain a sub claim.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"oauth.net: Client credentials\",\n      \"url\": \"https://oauth.net/2/grant-types/client-credentials/\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-auth-q11\",\n    \"level\": \"advanced\",\n    \"type\": \"multi\",\n    \"prompt\": \"A traditional server-rendered web app is a **confidential** OAuth client with a client secret. You're aligning it with RFC 9700 and OAuth 2.1. Which statements are correct? Select all that apply.\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"It should use PKCE too: OAuth 2.1 requires it for confidential clients, apart from a narrow OIDC `nonce` exception\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. PKCE is no longer just for public clients. Even where the exception applies, OAuth 2.1 still recommends using it.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Its client secret already stops a stolen authorisation code being used, so PKCE adds nothing\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Wrong. In a code injection attack, the attacker plants a stolen code into the *legitimate* client's callback, and that client redeems it with its own secret. PKCE stops this, because the code is bound to a verifier that client never issued.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Use `code_challenge_method=S256`, not `plain`\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. With `plain` the challenge *is* the verifier, so anyone who sees the authorisation request learns it. OAuth 2.1 prohibits `plain`.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Because it's confidential, the implicit grant is acceptable for it\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Wrong. Implicit is deprecated for every client type. Its problem is tokens in the URL, not whether the client has a secret.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"For this web client, the authorization server should match redirect URIs exactly against registered values\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. Pattern or prefix matching has repeatedly let attackers redirect codes to URLs they control. RFC 9700 and OAuth 2.1 require exact string matching.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": [\n      0,\n      2,\n      4\n    ],\n    \"workedExample\": \"**Code injection**, step by step, against a client that has a secret but no PKCE:\\n\\n1. The attacker steals a code issued to the *victim* (e.g. from a leaky log).\\n2. The attacker starts a login on the real client in their own browser, then swaps the stolen code into the callback.\\n3. The client redeems it with its own valid secret. The attacker's session is now logged in as the victim.\\n\\nWith PKCE, the code was bound to the victim's `code_challenge`. The attacker's session holds a different verifier, so redemption fails.\\n\\nThe other rules: `S256` keeps the verifier secret, exact redirect matching keeps codes from going astray, and implicit is out for everyone.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"oauth.net: OAuth 2.1\",\n      \"url\": \"https://oauth.net/2.1/\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-auth-q12\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"Your auth server rotates refresh tokens. A user's refresh token R1 is stolen. The legitimate app refreshes first, receiving R2 (and R1 is invalidated). An hour later the attacker presents R1. What should the server do?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Reject R1 and do nothing else, since it has already been used\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Rejecting is right, but it wastes the signal. Reuse of a rotated token signals possible compromise (or a duplicate legitimate attempt), and the server can't tell which, so R2 should be distrusted too.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Accept R1, because refresh tokens are long-lived\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Accepting an already-rotated token defeats the entire point of rotation, which is to make each refresh token single-use.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Issue a new access token but no new refresh token\",\n        \"explanation\": \"That still hands the attacker an access token. A rotated refresh token should never produce anything.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Treat it as theft: reject R1 and revoke the whole token family, including R2, so the user must log in again\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct for the stated theft scenario: reject reuse and revoke the active refresh-token lineage. Reuse is a compromise signal, though duplicate legitimate refresh attempts can also cause it. Already issued self-contained access tokens need separate credential revocation enforcement or expire naturally.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 3,\n    \"workedExample\": \"Timeline:\\n\\n```\\n09:00  issue R1\\n09:05  attacker copies R1\\n09:30  app uses R1 -> gets R2\\n       R1 marked used\\n10:30  attacker uses R1\\n       -> REUSE DETECTED\\n       revoke the refresh lineage\\n       access tokens: revoke separately\\n       or wait for their short expiry\\n```\\n\\nIf the *attacker* had refreshed first, the legitimate app's later use of R1 would trigger the same detection. Either way, the reuse is caught and the stolen chain dies. RFC 9700 requires refresh tokens for public clients to be either rotated like this or sender-constrained (e.g. with DPoP or mTLS).\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"RFC 9700: OAuth 2.0 Security Best Current Practice\",\n      \"url\": \"https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9700\"\n    }\n  }\n]\n"}
- {"file":"api-sse-reconnect-resume.md","reason":"Resolve dictionary words to their intended local meaning","before":"---\nid: api-sse-reconnect-resume\ntitle: Server-Sent Events and replay\nlevel: advanced\nminutes: 2\nsummary: Stream one-way text events with automatic reconnection and a server-backed Last-Event-ID replay path.\nsourceLesson: api-real-time\n---\n\n## Server-Sent Events (SSE)\n\nSSE is a standard for the server to **stream** events over one long-lived HTTP response. The browser's `EventSource` API handles it natively.\n\n```\nGET /scores/stream HTTP/1.1\nAccept: text/event-stream\n```\n\n```\nHTTP/1.1 200 OK\nContent-Type: text/event-stream\nCache-Control: no-cache\n\nid: 101\nevent: goal\ndata: {\"team\":\"ARS\",\"min\":63}\n\nid: 102\nevent: goal\ndata: {\"team\":\"CHE\",\"min\":71}\n```\n\nEach event is a few text lines followed by a blank line. Features that make SSE pleasant:\n\n- **Automatic reconnection.** If the connection drops, the browser reconnects by itself.\n- **Resumption.** On reconnect the browser sends `Last-Event-ID: 102`, so the server can replay anything missed. That only works if the server keeps a short replay buffer (or can query events after id 102); the header just tells it where to start.\n- **`retry:` field** lets the server set the reconnect delay.\n- It uses HTTP, including HTTP/2 multiplexing. Configure intermediary buffering and timeouts. Native EventSource supports cookies but does not expose arbitrary request headers such as Authorization.\n\nLimitations:\n\n- **One-way**: server to client only. The client sends data with ordinary `POST` requests.\n- **Text only** (UTF-8). Binary needs a text encoding such as base64.\n- Over HTTP/1.1, browsers allow only ~6 connections per domain, so many tabs with SSE can exhaust them. HTTP/2 multiplexes streams, subject to negotiated stream limits.\n\nSSE is underused. Live feeds, notifications, progress bars and streaming LLM responses (OpenAI's and Anthropic's APIs both stream tokens over SSE) are a perfect fit.\n\n## Key takeaways\n- EventSource reads UTF-8 events from a long-lived HTTP response.\n- Automatic reconnect does not recover events unless the server retains or can query them.\n- Configure buffering, timeouts and connection limits; native EventSource does not expose arbitrary request headers.\n\n## Further reading\n- [MDN: Using server-sent events](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Server-sent_events/Using_server-sent_events)\n","after":"---\nid: api-sse-reconnect-resume\ntitle: Server-Sent Events and replay\nlevel: advanced\nminutes: 2\nsummary: Stream one-way text events with automatic reconnection and a server-backed Last-Event-ID replay path.\nsourceLesson: api-real-time\n---\n\n## Server-Sent Events (SSE)\n\nSSE is a standard for the server to **stream** events over one long-lived HTTP response. The browser's `EventSource` API handles it natively.\n\n```\nGET /scores/stream HTTP/1.1\nAccept: text/event-stream\n```\n\n```\nHTTP/1.1 200 OK\nContent-Type: text/event-stream\nCache-Control: no-cache\n\nid: 101\nevent: goal\ndata: {\"team\":\"ARS\",\"min\":63}\n\nid: 102\nevent: goal\ndata: {\"team\":\"CHE\",\"min\":71}\n```\n\nEach event is a few text lines followed by a blank line. Features that make SSE pleasant:\n\n- **Automatic reconnection.** If the connection drops, the browser reconnects by itself.\n- **replay of missed events.** On reconnect the browser sends `Last-Event-ID: 102`, so the server can replay anything missed. That only works if the server keeps a short replay buffer (or can query events after id 102); the header just tells it where to start.\n- **`retry:` field** lets the server set the reconnect delay.\n- It uses HTTP, including HTTP/2 multiplexing. Configure intermediary buffering and timeouts. Native EventSource supports cookies but does not expose arbitrary request headers such as Authorization.\n\nLimitations:\n\n- **One-way**: server to client only. The client sends data with ordinary `POST` requests.\n- **Text only** (UTF-8). Binary needs a text encoding such as base64.\n- Over HTTP/1.1, browsers allow only ~6 connections per domain, so many tabs with SSE can exhaust them. HTTP/2 multiplexes streams, subject to negotiated stream limits.\n\nSSE is underused. Live feeds, notifications, progress bars and streaming LLM responses (OpenAI's and Anthropic's APIs both stream tokens over SSE) are a perfect fit.\n\n## Key takeaways\n- EventSource reads UTF-8 events from a long-lived HTTP response.\n- Automatic reconnect does not recover events unless the server retains or can query them.\n- Configure buffering, timeouts and connection limits; native EventSource does not expose arbitrary request headers.\n\n## Further reading\n- [MDN: Using server-sent events](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Server-sent_events/Using_server-sent_events)\n"}

Updates flow only from server to browser, a few per minute, to millions of viewers. You want automatic reconnection and resumption after mobile network drops with minimal custom code. Which is the best fit?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"WebSockets\",\n        \"explanation\": \"WebSockets would work, but they're full-duplex (you don't need client→server streaming), the browser API has no automatic reconnect, and you'd have to build resume logic yourself.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Short polling every second\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Millions of viewers polling every second means millions of requests per second, nearly all returning no change. Wasteful and still up to 1 s late.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Server-Sent Events\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. SSE is one-way server push over plain HTTP. `EventSource` reconnects automatically and sends `Last-Event-ID`, so the server can replay missed goals. Configure HTTP intermediaries for streaming; native EventSource supports cookies, but not custom Authorization headers.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"gRPC bidirectional streaming\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Browsers can't use raw gRPC without gRPC-Web and a proxy, and bidirectional streaming is unnecessary for one-way updates.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 2,\n    \"workedExample\": \"Requirements checklist:\\n\\n| Need | SSE | WebSocket |\\n|---|---|---|\\n| Server→client | ✔ | ✔ |\\n| Client→server stream | not needed | ✔ |\\n| Auto-reconnect | built in | DIY |\\n| Resume missed events | `Last-Event-ID` | DIY |\\n| Plain HTTP infra | ✔ | upgrade needed |\\n\\nSSE meets every requirement with the least custom code.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"MDN: Using server-sent events\",\n      \"url\": \"https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Server-sent_events/Using_server-sent_events\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-real-time-q8\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"Your SSE notifications stream sends `id:` on every event. A phone drops off the network for 20 seconds during which events 340–352 are sent, then `EventSource` reconnects with `Last-Event-ID: 339`. Users report missing notifications after reconnecting. What's the most likely server-side gap?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"The server ignores `Last-Event-ID` or has nowhere to read past events from, so it only sends new events from the moment of reconnection\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. The browser does its half automatically: reconnect and report the last id. The server has to keep a replay buffer (or query a log) and send everything after 339 before resuming live events.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"`EventSource` doesn't reconnect automatically, so the client must do it\",\n        \"explanation\": \"It does reconnect automatically; that's one of SSE's selling points. The client did reconnect here and sent the header.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"SSE can't carry ids larger than 255\",\n        \"explanation\": \"The `id` field is an arbitrary string. There's no numeric limit.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"The events were binary, which SSE drops\",\n        \"explanation\": \"SSE is text-only, but that would fail on every event, not just during the outage. Binary data is sent base64-encoded.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 0,\n    \"workedExample\": \"Split the resume work into its two halves:\\n\\n| Step | Who does it |\\n|---|---|\\n| Detect drop, reconnect | Browser |\\n| Send `Last-Event-ID: 339` | Browser |\\n| Find events 340+ | **Server** |\\n| Send them, then go live | **Server** |\\n\\nImplementation sketch: keep each user's recent events in a capped Redis list or stream (say, the last 5 minutes). Avoid a replay-to-live race: consume a durable stream continuously after the last ID, or establish and buffer a live subscription before replaying to a captured high-water mark, then deduplicate the overlap. If the requested id is older than the buffer, tell the client to do a full refresh.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"MDN: Using server-sent events\",\n      \"url\": \"https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Server-sent_events/Using_server-sent_events\"\n    }\n  }\n]\n","after":"[\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-real-time-q3\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"You're building a live football scores page. Wasteful and still up to 1 s late.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Server-Sent Events\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. SSE is one-way server push over plain HTTP. `EventSource` reconnects automatically and sends `Last-Event-ID`, so the server can replay missed goals. Configure HTTP intermediaries for streaming; native EventSource supports cookies, but not custom Authorization headers.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"gRPC bidirectional streaming\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Browsers can't use raw gRPC without gRPC-Web and a proxy, and bidirectional streaming is unnecessary for one-way updates.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 2,\n    \"workedExample\": \"Requirements checklist:\\n\\n| Need | SSE | WebSocket |\\n|---|---|---|\\n| Server→client | ✔ | ✔ |\\n| Client→server stream | not needed | ✔ |\\n| Auto-reconnect | built in | DIY |\\n| Resume missed events | `Last-Event-ID` | DIY |\\n| Plain HTTP infra | ✔ | upgrade needed |\\n\\nSSE meets every requirement with the least custom code.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"MDN: Using server-sent events\",\n      \"url\": \"https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Server-sent_events/Using_server-sent_events\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-real-time-q8\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"Your SSE notifications stream sends `id:` on every event. A phone drops off the network for 20 seconds during which events 340–352 are sent, then `EventSource` reconnects with `Last-Event-ID: 339`. Users report missing notifications after reconnecting. What's the most likely server-side gap?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"The server ignores `Last-Event-ID` or has nowhere to read past events from, so it only sends new events from the moment of reconnection\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. The server has to keep a replay buffer (or query a log) and send everything after 339 before resuming live events.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"`EventSource` doesn't reconnect automatically, so the client must do it\",\n        \"explanation\": \"It does reconnect automatically; that's one of SSE's selling points. There's no numeric limit.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"The events were binary, which SSE drops\",\n        \"explanation\": \"SSE is text-only, but that would fail on every event, not just during the outage.
