- {"file":"api-gateway-bff-boundaries.md","reason":"Resolve dictionary words to their intended local meaning","before":"---\nid: api-gateway-bff-boundaries\ntitle: API gateways and backends for frontends\nlevel: advanced\nminutes: 4\nsummary: Separate edge policy from client-specific response shaping and keep business rules in core services.\nsourceLesson: api-webhooks-gateways\n---\n\n## API gateways\n\nAs systems grow into dozens of services, you don't want every service to re-implement auth, rate limiting and TLS. An **API gateway** is a reverse proxy that handles these cross-cutting concerns at the edge.\n\n```\n   clients\n      |\n+------------+\n| API gateway|  TLS, auth,\n|            |  rate limit,\n+------------+  routing, logs\n  |    |    |\n svc  svc  svc\n```\n\nTypical responsibilities:\n\n- **Routing**: `/orders/*` to the orders service, `/v2/*` to new deployments, canary splits.\n- **Authentication**: validate JWTs or API keys, then pass a verified identity downstream.\n- **Rate limiting and quotas** per API key or plan.\n- **TLS termination**, request size limits, WAF rules.\n- **Observability**: request ids, access logs, metrics, tracing headers.\n- **Transformation**: header injection, protocol translation (REST to gRPC).\n\nExamples: Kong, AWS API Gateway, Apigee, Azure API Management, Envoy-based gateways. Load balancing itself is covered in the networking module; a gateway is an L7 proxy with API-aware features layered on top.\n\n> [!warning] Don't turn the gateway into a monolith\n> Keep business logic out. Once the gateway starts aggregating responses, applying domain rules and knowing about order states, every team's change has to go through it, and it becomes the bottleneck you split your monolith to escape.\n\nAlso remember the gateway is on every request's path: it must be horizontally scaled, highly available and fast. A gateway that adds 30 ms to every call becomes a big chunk of your latency budget.\n\n## Backend-for-frontend (BFF)\n\nDifferent clients need different things. The iOS app wants small payloads tuned for a phone screen. The web app shows richer pages. A partner integration wants stable, generic resources. One general-purpose API ends up serving all of them badly.\n\nThe **backend-for-frontend** pattern, popularised by Sam Newman and based on work at SoundCloud, can give each distinct client experience its **own** thin backend, owned by the team that builds that frontend.\n\n```\n iOS     Android    Web\n  |        |         |\n+----+  +----+   +-----+\n|BFF |  |BFF |   | BFF |\n|mob |  |mob |   | web |\n+----+  +----+   +-----+\n   \\       |       /\n  +----------------+\n  |  core services |\n  +----------------+\n```\n\nWhat a BFF does:\n\n- **Aggregates** several downstream calls into one response shaped for a specific screen, cutting mobile round trips.\n- **Trims** payloads to what that client needs.\n- **Handles client-specific concerns**: for browsers, it can hold OAuth tokens server-side and give the browser only an `HttpOnly` session cookie (the \"token handler\" pattern), keeping tokens out of JavaScript.\n- **Evolves with its frontend**, so the mobile team can ship a new screen without waiting for a core-services change.\n\nTrade-offs:\n\n- **Duplication** across BFFs (two mobile BFFs often converge). Share libraries, or merge them if the clients are near-identical.\n- **More services to run.**\n- Like the gateway, keep **domain logic in core services**. A BFF should orchestrate and shape, not decide business rules.\n\nGraphQL can be an alternative or a complement: clients select response fields through a shared schema. Client-specific authentication, aggregation policy and ownership may still justify BFFs.\n\n## Gateway vs BFF\n\n| | API gateway | BFF |\n|---|---|---|\n| Count | One (or few) | One per client type |\n| Owner | Platform team | Frontend team |\n| Focus | Cross-cutting | Client-specific shape |\n| Logic | Policy only | Aggregation |\n\nThey usually coexist: the gateway handles TLS, auth and rate limits at the edge, then routes to the right BFF.\n\n## Key takeaways\n- Gateways centralise routing, identity checks, limits, TLS and observability.\n- A BFF is owned with its frontend and aggregates or trims responses for that experience.\n- Gateways and BFFs both need operational capacity and clear domain boundaries.\n- GraphQL and BFFs can complement each other; neither removes client-specific ownership decisions.\n\n## Further reading\n- [Sam Newman: Backends for frontends](https://samnewman.io/patterns/architectural/bff/)\n- [Microsoft Azure Architecture Center: Backends for Frontends pattern](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/architecture/patterns/backends-for-frontends)\n- [Microsoft Azure Architecture Center: API gateways](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/architecture/microservices/design/gateway)\n","after":"---\nid: api-gateway-bff-boundaries\ntitle: API gateways and backends for frontends\nlevel: advanced\nminutes: 4\nsummary: Separate edge policy from client-specific response shaping and keep business rules in core services.\nsourceLesson: api-webhooks-gateways\n---\n\n## API gateways\n\nAs systems grow into dozens of services, you don't want every service to re-implement auth, rate limiting and TLS. An **API gateway** is a reverse proxy that handles these cross-cutting concerns at the edge.\n\n```\n   clients\n      |\n+------------+\n| API gateway|  TLS, auth,\n|            |  rate limit,\n+------------+  routing, logs\n  |    |    |\n svc  svc  svc\n```\n\nTypical responsibilities:\n\n- **Routing**: `/orders/*` to the orders service, `/v2/*` to new deployments, canary splits.\n- **Authentication**: validate JWTs or API keys, then pass a verified identity downstream.\n- **Rate limiting and quotas** per API key or plan.\n- **TLS termination**, request size limits, WAF rules.\n- **Observability**: request ids, access logs, metrics, tracing headers.\n- **Transformation**: header injection, protocol translation (REST to gRPC).\n\nExamples: Kong, AWS API Gateway, Apigee, Azure API Management, Envoy-based gateways. Load balancing itself is covered in the networking module; a gateway is an L7 proxy with API-aware features layered on top.\n\n> [!warning] Don't turn the gateway into a monolith\n> Keep business logic out. Once the gateway starts aggregating responses, applying domain rules and knowing about order states, every team's change has to go through it, and it becomes the bottleneck you split your monolith to escape.\n\nAlso remember the gateway is on every request's path: it must be horizontally scaled, highly available and fast. A gateway that adds 30 ms to every call becomes a big chunk of your latency budget.\n\n## Backend-for-frontend (BFF)\n\nDifferent clients need different things. The iOS app wants small payloads tuned for a phone screen. The web app shows richer pages. A partner integration wants stable, generic resources. One general-purpose API ends up serving all of them badly.\n\nThe **backend-for-frontend** pattern, popularised by Sam Newman and based on work at SoundCloud, can give each distinct client experience its **own** thin backend, owned by the team that builds that frontend.\n\n```\n iOS     Android    Web\n  |        |         |\n+----+  +----+   +-----+\n|BFF |  |BFF |   | BFF |\n|mob |  |mob |   | web |\n+----+  +----+   +-----+\n   \\       |       /\n  +----------------+\n  |  core services |\n  +----------------+\n```\n\nWhat a BFF does:\n\n- **Aggregates** several downstream calls into one response shaped for a specific screen, cutting mobile round trips.\n- **Trims** payloads to what that client needs.\n- **Handles client-specific concerns**: for browsers, it can hold OAuth tokens server-side and give the browser only an `HttpOnly` session cookie (the \"token handler\" pattern), keeping tokens out of JavaScript.\n- **Evolves with its frontend**, so the mobile team can ship a new screen without waiting for a core-services change.\n\nTrade-offs:\n\n- **Duplication** across BFFs (two mobile BFFs often converge). Share libraries, or merge them if the clients are near-identical.\n- **More services to run.**\n- Like the gateway, keep **domain logic in core services**. A BFF should orchestrate and shape, not decide business rules.\n\nGraphQL can be an alternative or a complement: clients select response fields through a shared GraphQL schema. Client-specific authentication, aggregation policy and ownership may still justify BFFs.\n\n## Gateway vs BFF\n\n| | API gateway | BFF |\n|---|---|---|\n| Count | One (or few) | One per client type |\n| Owner | Platform team | Frontend team |\n| Focus | Cross-cutting | Client-specific shape |\n| Logic | Policy only | Aggregation |\n\nThey usually coexist: the gateway handles TLS, auth and rate limits at the edge, then routes to the right BFF.\n\n## Key takeaways\n- Gateways centralise routing, identity checks, limits, TLS and observability.\n- A BFF is owned with its frontend and aggregates or trims responses for that experience.\n- Gateways and BFFs both need operational capacity and clear domain boundaries.\n- GraphQL and BFFs can complement each other; neither removes client-specific ownership decisions.\n\n## Further reading\n- [Sam Newman: Backends for frontends](https://samnewman.io/patterns/architectural/bff/)\n- [Microsoft Azure Architecture Center: Backends for Frontends pattern](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/architecture/patterns/backends-for-frontends)\n- [Microsoft Azure Architecture Center: API gateways](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/architecture/microservices/design/gateway)\n"}

HTTP, OAuth and WebSocket specifications are published as RFCs.","example":"RFC 9110 defines HTTP methods and status codes; RFC 6749 defines OAuth 2.0.","source":"https://www.rfc-editor.org/about/"},"reason":"Correct or substantiate glossary semantics against independently fetched primary source"}
- {"file":"glossary.json","term":"rpc-deadline","before":{"id":"rpc-deadline","term":"RPC deadline","aliases":["deadline propagation","DEADLINE_EXCEEDED","gRPC deadline","gRPC deadlines","RPC deadlines"],"definition":"In gRPC, the absolute time by which a call must finish. With deadline propagation, downstream calls inherit the remaining time, and when it runs out the call fails with DEADLINE_EXCEEDED so every hop can stop work.","example":"A 500 ms deadline set at checkout leaves 430 ms for a call made two hops later.","source":"https://grpc.io/docs/guides/deadlines/"},"after":{"id":"rpc-deadline","term":"RPC deadline","aliases":["deadline propagation","DEADLINE_EXCEEDED","gRPC deadline","gRPC deadlines","RPC deadlines"],"definition":"In gRPC, the time by which a call must finish. Configured deadline propagation passes the remaining budget downstream; expiry fails the call and signals cancellation, but handlers must stop their own work cooperatively.","example":"A 500 ms deadline set at checkout leaves 430 ms for a call made two hops later.","source":"https://grpc.io/docs/guides/deadlines/"},"reason":"Correct or substantiate glossary semantics against independently fetched primary source"}
- {"file":"glossary.json","term":"ratelimit-headers","before":{"id":"ratelimit-headers","term":"RateLimit headers","aliases":["RateLimit-Policy","RateLimit header","X-RateLimit-Limit","X-RateLimit-Remaining","X-RateLimit-Reset","RateLimit-Limit","RateLimit-Remaining","RateLimit-Reset"],"definition":"HTTP response headers that tell a client its quota, how much is left and when it resets. An IETF draft defines RateLimit-Policy and RateLimit; many APIs use older X-RateLimit-* headers.","example":"RateLimit-Policy: \"min\";q=100;w=60","source":"https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-ietf-httpapi-ratelimit-headers/"},"after":{"id":"ratelimit-headers","term":"RateLimit headers","aliases":["RateLimit-Policy","RateLimit header","X-RateLimit-Limit","X-RateLimit-Remaining","X-RateLimit-Reset","RateLimit-Limit","RateLimit-Remaining","RateLimit-Reset"],"definition":"HTTP response fields that advertise a quota policy and remaining allowance. The IETF RateLimit draft uses an effective time window, which is not a universal promise of a full reset; provider-specific X-RateLimit headers can use different reset units.","example":"RateLimit-Policy: \"min\";q=100;w=60","source":"https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-ietf-httpapi-ratelimit-headers/"},"reason":"Correct or substantiate glossary semantics against independently fetched primary source"}
- {"file":"glossary.json","term":"revocation","before":{"id":"revocation","term":"Revocation","aliases":["revoke","revoked","revoking","revocable"],"definition":"Cancelling a credential before it would naturally expire, so it stops working.","example":"Deleting a session record revokes that session at once; a self-contained JWT keeps working until it expires unless you add a deny-list.","source":"https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7009"},"after":{"id":"revocation","term":"Revocation","aliases":["revoke","revoked","revoking","revocable","credential revocation","token revocation","session revocation"],"definition":"Cancelling a credential before it would naturally expire, so it stops working.","example":"Deleting a session record rejects that session at subsequent authoritative validation; caches can delay enforcement. Do not carry API v1's saved-failure or retention assumptions into API v2 without checking its current documentation.\n\nAn expired IETF Internet-Draft","reason":"Prevent replaying saved 500 behavior being applied to v2"}

- {"file":"api-idempotency-key-contracts.questions.json","question":"api-idempotency-q4","before":{"id":"api-idempotency-q4","level":"intermediate","type":"single","prompt":"A client retries `POST /payments` with `Idempotency-Key: K` but, due to a bug, changes `amount` from 5000 to 7000 on the retry. The first request with K succeeded. How does Stripe's idempotency layer respond?","options":[{"text":"It returns an idempotency error, because the parameters don't match the original request made with that key","explanation":"Correct. Stripe compares the incoming parameters with the original. Silently returning the old 5000 result would hide the client bug; charging 7000 would break the key's promise. Erroring makes the bug visible."},{"text":"It charges 7000, because the parameters are new","explanation":"That would defeat the purpose of the key: the client would get a second charge for what it claims is the same operation."},{"text":"It silently returns the original 5000 result","explanation":"That would hide a real bug: the client believes it charged 7000. Stripe deliberately errors instead."},{"text":"It refunds the first charge and charges 7000","explanation":"Idempotency layers never take compensating actions on their own. They either replay or reject."}],"answer":0,"workedExample":"The server stores, per key: `(request hash, status, response)`.\n\n1. Retry arrives with K.\n2. Lookup finds K → stored hash = `hash(amount=5000, ...)`.\n3. Incoming hash = `hash(amount=7000, ...)` → **mismatch**.\n4. Return an error (Stripe uses an `idempotency_error`; the IETF draft suggests `422`).\n\nThis is why implementations store a hash of the request body, not just the key.","link":{"title":"Stripe API: Idempotent requests","url":"https://docs.stripe.com/api/idempotent_requests"}},"after":{"id":"api-idempotency-q4","level":"intermediate","type":"single","prompt":"A client retries `POST /payments` with `Idempotency-Key: K` but, due to a bug, changes `amount` from 5000 to 7000 on the retry. The first request with K succeeded. Under Stripe API v1's documented contract, how does its idempotency layer respond?","options":[{"text":"It returns an idempotency error, because the parameters don't match the original request made with that key","explanation":"Correct. Stripe compares the incoming parameters with the original. Silently returning the old 5000 result would hide the client bug; charging 7000 would break the key's promise. Erroring makes the bug visible."},{"text":"It charges 7000, because the parameters are new","explanation":"That would defeat the purpose of the key: the client would get a second charge for what it claims is the same operation."},{"text":"It silently returns the original 5000 result","explanation":"That would hide a real bug: the client believes it charged 7000. Stripe deliberately errors instead."},{"text":"It refunds the first charge and charges 7000","explanation":"Idempotency layers never take compensating actions on their own. They either replay or reject."}],"answer":0,"workedExample":"The server stores, per key: `(request hash, status, response)`.\n\n1. Retry arrives with K.\n2. Lookup finds K → stored hash = `hash(amount=5000, ...)`.\n3. Incoming hash = `hash(amount=7000, ...)` → **mismatch**.\n4. Return an error (Stripe uses an `idempotency_error`; the IETF draft suggests `422`).\n\nThis is why implementations store a hash of the request body, not just the key.","link":{"title":"Stripe API: Idempotent requests","url":"https://docs.stripe.com/api/idempotent_requests"}},"reason":"Scope provider question to checked v1 contract"}

- {"file":"api-backoff-jitter-budgets.questions.json","question":"api-idempotency-q11","before":{"id":"api-idempotency-q11","level":"advanced","type":"single","prompt":"A database blips and 10,000 clients get an error at the same instant. Each waits before retrying: policy A uses plain exponential backoff, so every client waits exactly 2 s; policy B uses full jitter, so each waits a uniformly random time between 0 and 2 s. What does the database see?","options":[{"text":"Both deliver 10,000 retries, so the load is the same","explanation":"The *total* is the same, but the *peak* isn't. A database copes with a steady rate far better than with everything arriving in the same instant."},{"text":"A: about 5,000/s for two seconds; B: all 10,000 exactly 2 s later","explanation":"This is the right pair of outcomes attached to the wrong policies. The fixed wait is what synchronises clients."},{"text":"B is worse, because some clients retry almost immediately","explanation":"A few early retries are cheap. Spreading arrivals across the window is exactly what keeps the peak low."},{"text":"A: all 10,000 retries arrive together 2 s after the blip; B: roughly 5,000/s spread across the 2 s window","explanation":"Correct. Without jitter the clients stay in lockstep, a thundering herd on every retry round. Full jitter spreads 10,000 retries over 2 s, about 5 per millisecond."}],"answer":3,"workedExample":"**Policy A** (no jitter): every client computes the same wait.\n\n```\nt=0.0  10,000 fail\nt=2.0  10,000 retry at once\nt=6.0  10,000 retry at once\n```\n\nThe herd stays synchronised for every round.\n\n**Policy B** (full jitter): waits are uniform on [0, 2 s].\n\n```formula\nR = N / W\nR = 10,000 / 2 s = 5,000/s\nR = 5,000/s ÷ 1,000 = 5/ms\nwhere\nR: the average retry arrival rate at the database\nN: the number of clients retrying, 10,000\nW: the width of the jitter window, 2 s\ns: seconds; /s means per second\nms: milliseconds; /ms means per millisecond (a second has 1,000)\nIn words: 10,000 retries spread evenly over 2 seconds arrive at about **5,000 per second**, roughly **5 per millisecond**.\n```\n\n- Next round, the window doubles to 4 s, so remaining failures spread out even further.\n\nAWS's analysis found full jitter cuts both total work and completion time compared with no jitter.","link":{"title":"AWS Architecture Blog: Exponential backoff and jitter","url":"https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/exponential-backoff-and-jitter/"}},"after":{"id":"api-idempotency-q11","level":"advanced","type":"single","prompt":"A database blips and 10,000 clients get an error at the same instant. Each waits before retrying: policy A uses plain exponential backoff, so every client waits exactly 2 s; policy B uses full jitter, so each waits a uniformly random time between 0 and 2 s. What does the database see?","options":[{"text":"Both deliver 10,000 retries, so the load is the same","explanation":"The *total* is the same, but the *peak* isn't. A database copes with a steady rate far better than with everything arriving in the same instant."},{"text":"A: about 5,000/s for two seconds; B: all 10,000 exactly 2 s later","explanation":"This is the right pair of outcomes attached to the wrong policies. The fixed wait is what synchronises clients."},{"text":"B is worse, because some clients retry almost immediately","explanation":"A few early retries are cheap. Spreading arrivals across the window is exactly what keeps the peak low."},{"text":"A: all 10,000 retries arrive together 2 s after the blip; B: roughly 5,000/s spread across the 2 s window","explanation":"Correct. Without jitter the clients stay in lockstep, a thundering herd on every retry round. Full jitter spreads 10,000 retries over 2 s, about 5 per millisecond."}],"answer":3,"workedExample":"**Policy A** (no jitter): every client computes the same wait.\n\n```\n0.0 s  10,000 fail\n2.0 s  10,000 retry at once\n6.0 s  10,000 retry at once\n```\n\nThe herd stays synchronised for every round.\n\n**Policy B** (full jitter): waits are uniform on [0, 2 s].\n\n```formula\nR = N / W\nR = 10,000 / 2 s = 5,000/s\nR = 5,000/s ÷ 1,000 = 5/ms\nwhere\nR: the average retry arrival rate at the database\nN: the number of clients retrying, 10,000\nW: the width of the jitter window, 2 s\ns: seconds; /s means per second\nms: milliseconds; /ms means per millisecond (a second has 1,000)\nIn words: 10,000 retries spread evenly over 2 seconds arrive at about **5,000 per second**, roughly **5 per millisecond**.\n```\n\n- Next round, the window doubles to 4 s, so remaining failures spread out even further.\n\nAWS's analysis found full jitter cuts both total work and completion time compared with no jitter.","link":{"title":"AWS Architecture Blog: Exponential backoff and jitter","url":"https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/exponential-backoff-and-jitter/"}},"reason":"Timeline labels are not equations"}
- {"file":"api-grpc-streaming-selection.questions.json","question":"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q6","before":{"id":"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q6","level":"advanced","type":"single","prompt":"A checkout service calls Pricing (which calls Tax, which calls a Currency service). The user-facing SLA is 500 ms. Pricing sometimes hangs for 5 s, and Tax keeps working on requests long after the user has given up. Which gRPC feature most directly addresses this wasted work?","options":[{"text":"Bidirectional streaming","explanation":"Streaming changes the shape of the conversation (many messages each way), not whether downstream services know the caller has stopped waiting."},{"text":"Server reflection","explanation":"Reflection lets tools like `grpcurl` discover services at runtime. It has nothing to do with timeouts."},{"text":"Deadline propagation: Checkout sets a 500 ms deadline that flows to Pricing, Tax and Currency, each of which can cancel when the remaining time hits zero","explanation":"Correct when deadline propagation is configured. Downstream handlers can observe cancellation, but must cooperate by stopping their own work and canceling supported database/child operations; gRPC does not forcibly interrupt arbitrary application code."},{"text":"Protobuf field tags","explanation":"Field tags are about wire encoding and protobuf schema evolution, not about time budgets."}],"answer":2,"workedExample":"Work the time budget:\n\n```\nCheckout  deadline = now + 500ms\n  -> Pricing   (spent 20ms, 480 left)\n     -> Tax    (spent 50ms, 430 left)\n        -> FX  (430ms left)\n```\n\nEach hop passes on whatever time is left:\n\n```formula\nT_left = D − T_used\nT_left = 500 ms − 20 ms = 480 ms\nT_left = 480 ms − 50 ms = 430 ms\nwhere\nT_left: the time remaining before the deadline when the next call starts\nD: the time that was left when this hop received the call (500 ms at Checkout)\nT_used: the time this hop has already spent\nms: milliseconds, thousandths of a second\nIn words: the deadline is fixed, so every hop gets only what its callers have not used up.\n```\n\nIf Pricing stalls and 500 ms elapses, the deadline expires everywhere: Checkout returns `DEADLINE_EXCEEDED`, and Tax and FX can observe cancellation and must stop their work cooperatively. Without propagation, each hop would have its own independent (often longer) timeout and keep working for nobody.","link":{"title":"gRPC: Core concepts","url":"https://grpc.io/docs/what-is-grpc/core-concepts/"}},"after":{"id":"api-rest-graphql-grpc-q6","level":"advanced","type":"single","prompt":"A checkout service calls Pricing (which calls Tax, which calls a Currency service). The user-facing SLA is 500 ms. Pricing sometimes hangs for 5 s, and Tax keeps working on requests long after the user has given up. Which gRPC feature most directly addresses this wasted work?","options":[{"text":"Bidirectional streaming","explanation":"Streaming changes the shape of the conversation (many messages each way), not whether downstream services know the caller has stopped waiting."},{"text":"Server reflection","explanation":"Reflection lets tools like `grpcurl` discover services at runtime. It has nothing to do with timeouts."},{"text":"Deadline propagation: Checkout sets a 500 ms deadline that flows to Pricing, Tax and Currency, each of which can cancel when the remaining time hits zero","explanation":"Correct when deadline propagation is configured. Downstream handlers can observe cancellation, but must cooperate by stopping their own work and canceling supported database/child operations; gRPC does not forcibly interrupt arbitrary application code."},{"text":"Protobuf field tags","explanation":"Field tags are about wire encoding and protobuf schema evolution, not about time budgets."}],"answer":2,"workedExample":"Work the time budget:\n\n```\nCheckout: 500 ms budget from now\n  -> Pricing   (spent 20ms, 480 left)\n     -> Tax    (spent 50ms, 430 left)\n        -> FX  (430ms left)\n```\n\nEach hop passes on whatever time is left:\n\n```formula\nT_left = D − T_used\nT_left = 500 ms − 20 ms = 480 ms\nT_left = 480 ms − 50 ms = 430 ms\nwhere\nT_left: the time remaining before the deadline when the next call starts\nD: the time that was left when this hop received the call (500 ms at Checkout)\nT_used: the time this hop has already spent\nms: milliseconds, thousandths of a second\nIn words: the deadline is fixed, so every hop gets only what its callers have not used up.\n```\n\nIf Pricing stalls and 500 ms elapses, the deadline expires everywhere: Checkout returns `DEADLINE_EXCEEDED`, and Tax and FX can observe cancellation and must stop their work cooperatively. Without propagation, each hop would have its own independent (often longer) timeout and keep working for nobody.","link":{"title":"gRPC: Core concepts","url":"https://grpc.io/docs/what-is-grpc/core-concepts/"}},"reason":"Budget timeline need not use undefined pseudo-symbols"}
- {"file":"api-token-bucket-refill.questions.json","question":"api-rate-limiting-q9","before":{"id":"api-rate-limiting-q9","level":"intermediate","type":"single","prompt":"A token bucket should sustain 60 requests/minute and permit an instantaneous burst of 20. Which parameters fit, and how many unit requests can be accepted from t=0 through t=60 seconds inclusive, starting full with demand always available?","options":[{"text":"Refill 60 tokens/s, capacity 20; up to 3,620","explanation":"60 per *second* is 3,600 per minute, sixty times the spec. The refill rate has to be the long-run average expressed per second: 60 / 60 = 1."},{"text":"Refill 1 token/s, capacity 60; up to 120","explanation":"This swaps the roles. Capacity sets the burst size, so it should be 20, not 60. With capacity 60 a client could fire 60 at once."},{"text":"Refill 1 token/s, capacity 20; up to 80","explanation":"Correct. Refill rate = average (60/min = 1/s); capacity = burst (20). From full: 20 immediately, plus 60 refilled over the minute, so 80, settling to 60/min after that."},{"text":"Refill 1 token/s, capacity 20; exactly 60","explanation":"The parameters are right, but this forgets the initial full bucket. The 20 stored tokens are spent on top of the 60 that refill during the minute."}],"answer":2,"workedExample":"1. **Refill rate** = long-run average: 60 per minute ÷ 60 s = **1 token/s**.\n2. **Capacity** = biggest burst: **20**.\n3. First minute from a full bucket:\n\n```formula\nA = C + r × T\nA = 20 + 1 token/s × 60 s\nA = 20 + 60 = 80\nwhere\nA: the most requests accepted from t = 0 to t = 60 s\nC: the capacity, 20 tokens, all available at the start\nr: the refill rate, 1 token per second\nT: the length of the period, 60 s\nt: time since the start, in seconds\ntoken: one request's worth of allowance\ns: seconds\nIn words: the client spends the 20 stored tokens at once, then one new token each second for 60 seconds, so 80 in total.\n```\n\n4. Every minute after that the bucket is empty on average, so the client gets **60/min**.\n\nThat first-minute overshoot (80 vs 60) is the burst allowance doing its job. If that's unacceptable, use a smaller capacity.","link":{"title":"Wikipedia: Token bucket","url":"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Token_bucket"}},"after":{"id":"api-rate-limiting-q9","level":"intermediate","type":"single","prompt":"A token bucket should sustain 60 requests/minute and permit an instantaneous burst of 20. Which parameters fit, and how many unit requests can be accepted from t=0 through t=60 seconds inclusive, starting full with demand always available?","options":[{"text":"Refill 60 tokens/s, capacity 20; up to 3,620","explanation":"60 per *second* is 3,600 per minute, sixty times the spec. The refill rate has to be the long-run average expressed per second: 60 / 60 = 1."},{"text":"Refill 1 token/s, capacity 60; up to 120","explanation":"This swaps the roles. Capacity sets the burst size, so it should be 20, not 60. With capacity 60 a client could fire 60 at once."},{"text":"Refill 1 token/s, capacity 20; up to 80","explanation":"Correct. Sixty tokens per minute is one token per second; the capacity of 20 supplies the burst. From full, 20 can be spent immediately and 60 refill over the inclusive minute, so up to 80 are accepted, settling to 60 per minute afterwards."},{"text":"Refill 1 token/s, capacity 20; exactly 60","explanation":"The parameters are right, but this forgets the initial full bucket. The 20 stored tokens are spent on top of the 60 that refill during the minute."}],"answer":2,"workedExample":"1. **Refill rate** = long-run average: 60 per minute ÷ 60 s = **1 token/s**.\n2. **Capacity** = biggest burst: **20**.\n3. First minute from a full bucket:\n\n```formula\nA = C + r × T\nA = 20 + 1 token/s × 60 s\nA = 20 + 60 = 80\nwhere\nA: the most requests accepted from t = 0 to t = 60 s\nC: the capacity, 20 tokens, all available at the start\nr: the refill rate, 1 token per second\nT: the length of the period, 60 s\nt: time since the start, in seconds\ntoken: one request's worth of allowance\ns: seconds\nIn words: the client spends the 20 stored tokens at once, then one new token each second for 60 seconds, so 80 in total.\n```\n\n4. Every minute after that the bucket is empty on average, so the client gets **60/min**.\n\nThat first-minute overshoot (80 vs 60) is the burst allowance doing its job. If that's unacceptable, use a smaller capacity.","link":{"title":"Wikipedia: Token bucket","url":"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Token_bucket"}},"reason":"Avoid inline equations in an option explanation"}

- {"file":"api-sliding-window-accuracy.questions.json","question":"api-rate-limiting-q10","before":{"id":"api-rate-limiting-q10","level":"advanced","type":"multi","prompt":"For the sliding-window estimate below, assume 0 < t < W and all previous-window arrivals discussed remain inside the true rolling window.\n\n```formula\nestimate = prev × (1 − t / W) + curr\nwhere\nestimate: the approximate number of requests in the last W seconds\nprev: the request count for the whole previous fixed window\ncurr: the request count so far in the current window\nt: the time elapsed since the current window started\nW: the window length, 60 s for a per-minute limit\n<: less than\nIn words: count this window's requests, plus the share of last window's requests the sliding window still covers.\n```\n\nWhich statements hold? Select all that apply.","options":[{"text":"It needs only two counters per client, the current and previous windows","explanation":"True. That's its main advantage over the sliding log, which stores every timestamp."},{"text":"It assumes the previous window's requests were spread evenly across that window","explanation":"True. Uniform weighting assumes an even spread. Bursty or adversarial traffic can make the approximation substantially wrong."},{"text":"If the previous window's requests all arrived in its last few seconds, it underestimates the true count over the past W seconds","explanation":"True under the stated timing assumption: all previous arrivals still count in reality, while the approximation discounts them. The excess can be substantial."},{"text":"The weight on the previous window equals the fraction of the current window that has elapsed","explanation":"False, it's the opposite. The weight is the *remaining* overlap, 1 − t/W. It starts at 1 and falls to 0 as the current window progresses."},{"text":"It's exact, like the sliding log, just using less memory","explanation":"False. It's an approximation. Cloudflare measured the error as tiny in practice (about 0.003% of requests mis-classified), but it isn't zero."}],"answer":[0,1,2],"workedExample":"Test the even-spread assumption with numbers. W = 60 s, limit 100, t = 30 s into the current window.\n\n- Previous window: 80 requests, **all** in its last 10 s.\n- Estimate:\n\n```formula\nestimate = 80 × (1 − 30 s / 60 s) + curr\nestimate = 40 + curr\nwhere\nestimate: the limiter's approximate count over the last 60 s\ncurr: the requests so far in the current window\ns: seconds\nIn words: only half of the previous window's 80 requests are counted.\n```\n\n- Truth: the sliding window (last 60 s) contains all 80 of them, so 80 + curr.\n\nThe estimate is 40 too low, so up to 40 extra requests could slip through. This is a counterexample, not the global worst case: later times with sufficiently late previous arrivals can undercount even more. Cloudflare’s reported low misclassification rate describes its historical measured workload, not an adversarial bound.","link":{"title":"Cloudflare blog: Counting things, a lot of different things","url":"https://blog.cloudflare.com/counting-things-a-lot-of-different-things/"}},"after":{"id":"api-rate-limiting-q10","level":"advanced","type":"multi","prompt":"For the sliding-window estimate below, assume the current fixed window has begun but not ended and all previous-window arrivals discussed remain inside the true rolling window.\n\n```formula\nestimate = prev × (1 − t / W) + curr\nwhere\nestimate: the approximate number of requests in the last W seconds\nprev: the request count for the whole previous fixed window\ncurr: the request count so far in the current window\nt: the time elapsed since the current window started\nW: the window length, 60 s for a per-minute limit\n<: less than\nIn words: count this window's requests, plus the share of last window's requests the sliding window still covers.\n```\n\nWhich statements hold? Select all that apply.","options":[{"text":"It needs only two counters per client, the current and previous windows","explanation":"True. That's its main advantage over the sliding log, which stores every timestamp."},{"text":"It assumes the previous window's requests were spread evenly across that window","explanation":"True. Uniform weighting assumes an even spread. Bursty or adversarial traffic can make the approximation substantially wrong."},{"text":"If the previous window's requests all arrived in its last few seconds, it underestimates the true count over the past W seconds","explanation":"True under the stated timing assumption: all previous arrivals still count in reality, while the approximation discounts them. The excess can be substantial."},{"text":"The weight on the previous window equals the fraction of the current window that has elapsed","explanation":"False, it's the opposite. The weight is the *remaining* overlap, 1 − t/W. It starts at 1 and falls to 0 as the current window progresses."},{"text":"It's exact, like the sliding log, just using less memory","explanation":"False. It's an approximation. Cloudflare measured the error as tiny in practice (about 0.003% of requests mis-classified), but it isn't zero."}],"answer":[0,1,2],"workedExample":"Test the even-spread assumption with numbers. W = 60 s, limit 100, t = 30 s into the current window.\n\n- Previous window: 80 requests, **all** in its last 10 s.\n- Estimate:\n\n```formula\nestimate = 80 × (1 − 30 s / 60 s) + curr\nestimate = 40 + curr\nwhere\nestimate: the limiter's approximate count over the last 60 s\ncurr: the requests so far in the current window\ns: seconds\nIn words: only half of the previous window's 80 requests are counted.\n```\n\n- Truth: the sliding window (last 60 s) contains all 80 of them, so 80 + curr.\n\nThe estimate is 40 too low, so up to 40 extra requests could slip through. This is a counterexample, not the global worst case: later times with sufficiently late previous arrivals can undercount even more. Cloudflare’s reported low misclassification rate describes its historical measured workload, not an adversarial bound.","link":{"title":"Cloudflare blog: Counting things, a lot of different things","url":"https://blog.cloudflare.com/counting-things-a-lot-of-different-things/"}},"reason":"Remove inline symbolic timing relation already defined locally"}

- {"file":"glossary.json","term":"revocation","before":{"id":"revocation","term":"Revocation","aliases":["revoke","revoked","revoking","revocable","credential revocation","token revocation","session revocation"],"definition":"Cancelling a credential before it would naturally expire, so it stops working.","example":"Deleting a session record rejects that session at subsequent authoritative validation; caches can delay enforcement. A self-contained JWT needs separate credential revocation enforcement before expiry.","source":"https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7009"},"after":{"id":"revocation","term":"Credential revocation","aliases":["revoke","revoked","revoking","revocable","credential revocation","token revocation","session revocation"],"definition":"Cancelling a credential before it would naturally expire, so it stops working.","example":"Deleting a session record rejects that session at subsequent authoritative validation; caches can delay enforcement. A self-contained JWT needs separate credential revocation enforcement before expiry.","source":"https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7009"},"reason":"Compiler skips a term when an earlier alias owns its display name; precise display name prevents credential aliases being donated to certificate revocation"}
- {"file":"api-jwt-validation-pitfalls.questions.json","question":"api-auth-q10","before":"1. Your verifier holds `PUBLIC_KEY_PEM`, which is published at your JWKS endpoint.\n2. The attacker builds header `{\"alg\":\"HS256\"}` and any payload, e.g. `{\"sub\":\"admin\",\"aud\":\"orders-api\"}`.\n3. They compute the HMAC themselves, since they know the PEM:\n\n```formula\ns = HMAC(K, m)\nK = PEM\nwhere\ns: the signature placed in the forged token\nHMAC: HMAC-SHA256, a keyed hash meant to need a secret key\nK: the key the HMAC uses\nPEM: the server's RSA public key written as PEM text, which is published at the JWKS endpoint and so is not secret\nm: the signing input: the base64url header, a dot, then the base64url payload\nIn words: the attacker uses public bytes as the HMAC key, so their signature is exactly what the confused verifier computes.\n```\n\n4. A library that honours the header runs HMAC with the same 'secret' and gets a match. **Forged token accepted.**\n\nFix, per RFC 8725: the verifier decides the algorithm, never the token.\n\nThis library call is a fragment: `token` and the trusted `key` must come from the API’s validation context.\n\n```python\n# The server chooses the allowed\n# algorithm.\njwt.decode(token, key,\n  algorithms=[\"RS256\"],\n  audience=\"orders-api\")\n```","after":"1. Your verifier holds `PUBLIC_KEY_PEM`, which is published at your JWKS endpoint.\n2. The attacker builds header `{\"alg\":\"HS256\"}` and any payload, e.g. `{\"sub\":\"admin\",\"aud\":\"orders-api\"}`.\n3. They compute the HMAC themselves, since they know the PEM:\n\n```formula\ns = HMAC(K, m)\nK = PEM\nwhere\ns: the signature placed in the forged token\nHMAC: HMAC-SHA256, a keyed hash meant to need a secret key\nK: the key the HMAC uses\nPEM: the server's RSA public key written as PEM text, which is published at the JWKS endpoint and so is not secret\nm: the signing input: the base64url header, a dot, then the base64url payload\nIn words: the attacker uses public bytes as the HMAC key, so their signature is exactly what the confused verifier computes.\n```\n\n4. A library that honours the header runs HMAC with the same 'secret' and gets a match. **Forged token accepted.**\n\nFix, per RFC 8725: the verifier decides the algorithm, never the token.\n\nThis library call demonstrates algorithm and audience checks. `token` and the trusted `key` come from the API’s validation context; the complete validator must also require and validate the issuer and expiry claims for the stated token profile.\n\n```python\n# The server chooses the allowed\n# algorithm.\njwt.decode(token, key,\n  algorithms=[\"RS256\"],\n  audience=\"orders-api\")\n```","reason":"JWT decode fragment does not by itself require issuer or exp presence"}

This is a counterexample, not the global worst case: later times with sufficiently late previous arrivals can undercount even more. Cloudflare’s reported low misclassification rate describes its historical measured workload, not an adversarial bound.","after":"Test the even-spread assumption with numbers. The window length is 60 seconds, the limit is 100 requests, and 30 seconds have elapsed in the current window.\n\n- Previous window: 80 requests, **all** in its last 10 s.\n- Estimate:\n\n```formula\nestimate = 80 × (1 − 30 s / 60 s) + curr\nestimate = 40 + curr\nwhere\nestimate: the limiter's approximate count over the last 60 s\ncurr: the requests so far in the current window\ns: seconds\nIn words: only half of the previous window's 80 requests are counted.\n```\n\n- Truth: the rolling window contains all 80 previous requests, plus all requests so far in the current window.\n\nThe estimate is 40 too low, so up to 40 extra requests could slip through. This is a counterexample, not the global worst case: later times with sufficiently late previous arrivals can undercount even more. Cloudflare’s reported low misclassification rate describes its historical measured workload, not an adversarial bound.","reason":"Replace repeated prose maths with words while preserving existing formula cards"}
- {"file":"api-idempotency-atomic-recovery.md","before":"Foreign calls (to the card network) happen *between* transactions, and are themselves idempotent because the same key is forwarded.","after":"Foreign calls (to the payment downstream) happen *between* transactions. Repeating a call is safe only when that downstream enforces the forwarded key under a documented scope and retention contract.","reason":"Forwarding an arbitrary key alone does not make an external side effect idempotent"}
- {"file":"api-idempotency-atomic-recovery.questions.json","question":"api-idempotency-q9","before":"Forward K (or a key derived from it) to the card network, so re-running the charge step is itself idempotent","after":"Forward K (or a scoped derived key) to a payment downstream that enforces an applicable idempotency contract, so resuming the charge step does not repeat the effect","reason":"Make downstream deduplication contract explicit in correct option"}
