- {"file":"api-object-access-models.md","before":"---\nid: api-object-access-models\ntitle: Object access with roles, attributes and relationships\nlevel: advanced\nminutes: 2\nsummary: Choose RBAC, ABAC or ReBAC while enforcing permission on every requested object.\nsourceLesson: api-auth\n---\n\n## Authorisation models\n\nOnce you know who the caller is, decide what they can do.\n\n## RBAC: role-based\n\nUsers get **roles**; roles have **permissions**.\n\n```\nalice -> [editor]\neditor -> posts:read, posts:write\nviewer -> posts:read\n```\n\nSimple, auditable and good enough for most internal tools. It strains when permissions depend on context: \"editors can edit posts *in their own department*, *during business hours*\". Teams end up with role explosion: `editor-uk`, `editor-uk-weekend`...\n\n## ABAC: attribute-based\n\nDecisions are policies over **attributes** of the user, the resource, the action and the environment:\n\n```pseudocode\n# Check context and object attributes.\nallow if\n  user.dept == resource.dept\n  and action == \"edit\"\n  and resource.status != \"locked\"\n```\n\nMore expressive, but harder to audit (\"who can see this document?\" requires evaluating policies). Tools such as Open Policy Agent (Rego) and AWS IAM policies are ABAC-style.\n\n## ReBAC: relationship-based\n\nPermissions follow relationships in a graph: \"you can view this doc because you're in a group that has access to the folder that contains it\". Google's **Zanzibar** (behind Drive, YouTube, Calendar) is the canonical example; OpenFGA and SpiceDB are open-source implementations.\n\n| Model | Best for |\n|---|---|\n| RBAC | Simple apps, admin tools |\n| ABAC | Context-dependent rules |\n| ReBAC | Sharing, hierarchies |\n\nWhatever the model, **enforce authorisation on the server for every object**, not just at login, and not just by hiding buttons in the UI.\n\n## Key takeaways\n- RBAC groups permissions into roles but can multiply roles for contextual rules.\n- ABAC evaluates attributes and environment; ReBAC follows sharing and hierarchy relationships.\n- Check object access on the server on every request; a valid token and hidden buttons are insufficient.\n\n## Further reading\n- [OWASP API Security Top 10: Broken object level authorization](https://owasp.org/API-Security/editions/2023/en/0xa1-broken-object-level-authorization/)\n- [Google Research: Zanzibar](https://research.google/pubs/zanzibar-googles-consistent-global-authorization-system/)\n- [NIST: Attribute based access control](https://csrc.nist.gov/projects/attribute-based-access-control)\n","after":"---\nid: api-object-access-models\ntitle: Object access with roles, attributes and relationships\nlevel: advanced\nminutes: 2\nsummary: Choose RBAC, ABAC or ReBAC while enforcing permission on every requested object.\nsourceLesson: api-auth\n---\n\n## Authorisation models\n\nOnce you know who the caller is, decide what they can do.\n\n## RBAC: role-based\n\nUsers get **access-control roles**; each role has **API permissions**.\n\n```\nalice -> [editor]\neditor -> posts:read, posts:write\nviewer -> posts:read\n```\n\nSimple, auditable and good enough for most internal tools. It strains when permissions depend on context: \"editors can edit posts *in their own department*, *during business hours*\". Teams end up with role explosion: `editor-uk`, `editor-uk-weekend`...\n\n## ABAC: attribute-based\n\nDecisions are policies over **access-control attributes** of the user, the resource, the action and the environment:\n\n```pseudocode\n# Check context and object attributes.\nallow if\n  user.dept == resource.dept\n  and action == \"edit\"\n  and resource.status != \"locked\"\n```\n\nMore expressive, but harder to audit (\"who can see this document?\" requires evaluating policies). Tools such as Open Policy Agent (Rego) and AWS IAM policies are ABAC-style.\n\n## ReBAC: relationship-based\n\nPermissions follow relationships in a graph: \"you can view this doc because you're in a group that has access to the folder that contains it\". Google's **Zanzibar** (behind Drive, YouTube, Calendar) is the canonical example; OpenFGA and SpiceDB are open-source implementations.\n\n| Model | Best for |\n|---|---|\n| RBAC | Simple apps, admin tools |\n| ABAC | Context-dependent rules |\n| ReBAC | Sharing, hierarchies |\n\nWhatever the model, **enforce authorisation on the server for every object**, not just at login, and not just by hiding buttons in the UI.\n\n## Key takeaways\n- RBAC groups permissions into roles but can multiply roles for contextual rules.\n- ABAC evaluates attributes and environment; ReBAC follows sharing and hierarchy relationships.\n- Check object access on the server on every request; a valid token and hidden buttons are insufficient.\n\n## Further reading\n- [OWASP API Security Top 10: Broken object level authorization](https://owasp.org/API-Security/editions/2023/en/0xa1-broken-object-level-authorization/)\n- [Google Research: Zanzibar](https://research.google/pubs/zanzibar-googles-consistent-global-authorization-system/)\n- [NIST: Attribute based access control](https://csrc.nist.gov/projects/attribute-based-access-control)\n","reason":"Bold technical introductions need precise glossary targets"}
- {"file":"api-token-bucket-refill.md","before":"---\nid: api-token-bucket-refill\ntitle: Token buckets, bursts and refill waits\nlevel: advanced\nminutes: 4\nsummary: Compute lazy refill, immediate burst allowance and the wait for the next request token.\nsourceLesson: api-rate-limiting\n---\n\n## The algorithms\n\n## Token bucket\n\nThe most widely used algorithm. Each client has a bucket that holds up to **capacity** tokens. Tokens are added at a fixed **refill rate**. Each request takes one token; if the bucket is empty, the request is rejected.\n\n```\n  refill r tokens/s\n        |\n        v\n   +---------+\n   | o o o o |  capacity C\n   | o o o   |\n   +----+----+\n        |\n   1 token per request\n   empty -> 429\n```\n\nTwo parameters, two meanings:\n\n- **Refill rate** = long-run average throughput.\n- **Capacity** = largest burst allowed.\n\nYou don't need a timer to add tokens. Store the token count and the time of the last top-up, and when a request arrives, add the tokens that would have dripped in since then:\n\n```formula\nb_new = min(C, b_old + r × Δt)\nwhere\nb_new: the number of tokens in the bucket after the top-up\nb_old: the tokens stored at the last top-up\nC: the capacity, the most tokens the bucket can hold\nr: the refill rate, in tokens per second\nΔt: delta t, the time since the last top-up, in seconds\nmin: the smaller of the two values, so the bucket never overflows its capacity\nIn words: add tokens for the time that has passed, but never more than the bucket holds.\n```\n\nIf the bucket then holds less than one whole token, the request is rejected, and the client can be told how long until one token is ready:\n\n```formula\nW = (1 − b_new) / r\nwhere\nW: the wait until the bucket holds one whole token, in seconds; this is the natural Retry-After value\nb_new: the tokens in the bucket now, less than 1\nr: the refill rate, in tokens per second\nIn words: the missing fraction of a token divided by how fast tokens arrive. This only applies while b_new is below 1 (otherwise the request is allowed); Retry-After takes whole seconds, so send W rounded up.\n```\n\nThe same logic as pseudocode, with policy branches rather than executable allow/reject functions (here `C`, `r`, `tokens` and `last_refill` are the capacity, refill rate, b and the stored time):\n\n```pseudocode\n# Refill only when a request arrives.\nnow = time()\nelapsed = now - last_refill\ntokens = min(C, tokens + elapsed * r)\nlast_refill = now\n# Spend only complete request allowances.\nif tokens >= 1:\n    tokens -= 1; allow\nelse:\n    # Time to earn the missing token\n    # fraction.\n    reject; retry_after =\n      (1 - tokens) / r\n```\n\n> [!example] Token bucket maths\n> Capacity 10, refill 2 tokens/s, bucket full. A client sends 15 requests instantly: the first 10 succeed, 5 get 429. One second later the bucket has 2 tokens, so 2 more can go through. Sustained, the client gets 2 requests/s, or 120 per minute.\n\nThe refill one second after the burst, and the long-run rate:\n\n```formula\nb_new = min(10, 0 + 2 tokens/s × 1 s) = 2\nR = 2 tokens/s × 60 s = 120 tokens\nwhere\nb_new: the tokens in the bucket one second after the burst emptied it\nR: the most requests the client can make per minute once its burst is spent, one per token\ntokens: the bucket's units; each request spends one\ns: seconds\nmin: the smaller of the two values\nIn words: an empty bucket earns 2 tokens each second, so after the first burst the client is held to 2 requests per second, 120 per minute.\n```\n\nAmazon API Gateway and AWS service APIs use token buckets (they call the parameters \"rate\" and \"burst\"). Stripe uses token buckets in Redis.\n\n## Key takeaways\n- Capacity bounds the stored burst; refill rate sets the sustained average.\n- Refill lazily from elapsed time, capped at capacity, then spend a token.\n- Round the missing-token wait upwards for a whole-second Retry-After.\n\n## Further reading\n- [Stripe blog: Scaling your API with rate limiters](https://stripe.com/blog/rate-limiters)\n- [Wikipedia: Token bucket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Token_bucket)\n","after":"---\nid: api-token-bucket-refill\ntitle: Token buckets, bursts and refill waits\nlevel: advanced\nminutes: 4\nsummary: Compute lazy refill, immediate burst allowance and the wait for the next request token.\nsourceLesson: api-rate-limiting\n---\n\n## The algorithms\n\n## Token bucket\n\nThe most widely used algorithm. Each client has a bucket that holds up to **capacity** tokens. Tokens are added at a fixed **refill rate**. Each request takes one token; if the bucket is empty, the request is rejected.\n\n```\n  refill r tokens/s\n        |\n        v\n   +---------+\n   | o o o o |  capacity C\n   | o o o   |\n   +----+----+\n        |\n   1 token per request\n   empty -> 429\n```\n\nTwo parameters, two meanings:\n\n- **Refill rate** = long-run average throughput.\n- **Capacity** = largest burst allowed.\n\nYou don't need a timer to add tokens. Store the token count and the time of the last top-up, and when a request arrives, add the tokens that would have dripped in since then:\n\n```formula\nb_new = min(C, b_old + r × Δt)\nwhere\nb_new: the number of tokens in the bucket after the top-up\nb_old: the tokens stored at the last top-up\nC: the capacity, the most tokens the bucket can hold\nr: the refill rate, in tokens per second\nΔt: delta t, the time since the last top-up, in seconds\nmin: the smaller of the two values, so the bucket never overflows its capacity\nIn words: add tokens for the time that has passed, but never more than the bucket holds.\n```\n\nIf the bucket then holds less than one whole token, the request is rejected, and the client can be told how long until one token is ready:\n\n```formula\nW = (1 − b_new) / r\nwhere\nW: the wait until the bucket holds one whole token, in seconds; this is the natural Retry-After value\nb_new: the tokens in the bucket now, less than 1\nr: the refill rate, in tokens per second\nIn words: the missing fraction of a token divided by how fast tokens arrive. This only applies while b_new is below 1 (otherwise the request is allowed); Retry-After takes whole seconds, so send W rounded up.\n```\n\nThis executable Python function receives the current token count, its stored timestamp, a trusted current timestamp in seconds, the capacity and the refill rate. Assume finite inputs: capacity at least one, positive refill rate, and a token count between zero and capacity. Use one consistent monotonic clock; never accept arbitrary client timestamps. The small clamp prevents a regressing timestamp from minting tokens, while large forward clock jumps still need an explicit policy.\n\n```python\ndef take_token(tokens, last_refill, now,\n               capacity, rate):\n    # Never move stored time backwards.\n    now = max(now, last_refill)\n    elapsed = now - last_refill\n    # Refill only when a request arrives.\n    tokens = min(capacity,\n                 tokens + elapsed * rate)\n    if tokens >= 1:\n        # Allowed: spend a complete token.\n        return True, tokens - 1, now, 0.0\n    # Rejected: keep fractional allowance.\n    wait = (1 - tokens) / rate\n    return False, tokens, now, wait\n```\n\nThe returned tuple contains the decision, remaining tokens, new stored timestamp and wait in seconds. The wait is zero for an allowed request; otherwise round it upwards for a whole-second `Retry-After`. This local function does not coordinate concurrent servers; the later atomic-limiter unit handles shared state.\n\n> [!example] Token bucket maths\n> Capacity 10, refill 2 tokens/s, bucket full. A client sends 15 requests instantly: the first 10 succeed, 5 get 429. One second later the bucket has 2 tokens, so 2 more can go through. Sustained, the client gets 2 requests/s, or 120 per minute.\n\nThe refill one second after the burst, and the long-run rate:\n\n```formula\nb_new = min(10, 0 + 2 tokens/s × 1 s) = 2\nR = 2 tokens/s × 60 s = 120 tokens\nwhere\nb_new: the tokens in the bucket one second after the burst emptied it\nR: the most requests the client can make per minute once its burst is spent, one per token\ntokens: the bucket's units; each request spends one\ns: seconds\nmin: the smaller of the two values\nIn words: an empty bucket earns 2 tokens each second, so after the first burst the client is held to 2 requests per second, 120 per minute.\n```\n\nAmazon API Gateway and AWS service APIs use token buckets (they call the parameters \"rate\" and \"burst\"). Stripe uses token buckets in Redis.\n\n## Key takeaways\n- Capacity bounds the stored burst; refill rate sets the sustained average.\n- Refill lazily from elapsed time, capped at capacity, then spend a token.\n- Round the missing-token wait upwards for a whole-second Retry-After.\n\n## Further reading\n- [Stripe blog: Scaling your API with rate limiters](https://stripe.com/blog/rate-limiters)\n- [Wikipedia: Token bucket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Token_bucket)\n","reason":"Convert inherited policy pseudocode to actual executed Python/Lua with explicit fixture context and preserved teaching contracts"}
- {"file":"api-leaky-fixed-window.md","before":"---\nid: api-leaky-fixed-window\ntitle: Leaky buckets and fixed-window boundaries\nlevel: advanced\nminutes: 3\nsummary: Trade queueing delay for smooth output and recognise fixed-window boundary bursts.\nsourceLesson: api-rate-limiting\n---\n\n## Leaky bucket\n\nRequests enter a **queue** of fixed size, and are processed (they \"leak out\") at a constant rate. If the queue is full, new requests are dropped.\n\n```\n requests in (bursty)\n     | | |||  |\n     v v vvv  v\n   +---------+\n   | queue   |  size B\n   +---------+\n       |\n       v  constant rate r\n   to backend\n```\n\nThe worst-case extra delay is a full queue draining at the leak rate:\n\n```formula\nW_max = B / r\nW_max = 100 / 50 = 2 s\nwhere\nW_max: the longest a request can wait in the queue, in seconds (s)\nB: the queue size, in requests; here 100\nr: the leak rate, in requests per second; here 50\ns: seconds\nIn words: a request that joins the back of a full queue waits for every request ahead of it to leak out, so a 100-request queue at 50 per second adds up to 2 s.\n```\n\nThe output is perfectly smooth, which is great for protecting a fragile downstream (say, a legacy system that can only handle 50 req/s). The cost is latency: bursty requests wait in the queue instead of being served immediately. NGINX's `limit_req` is a leaky bucket; its `burst` parameter is the queue size. Add `nodelay` and queued requests are served immediately instead, while still using up queue slots that drain at the set rate, so the behaviour becomes much closer to a token bucket.\n\n| | Token bucket | Leaky bucket |\n|---|---|---|\n| Allows bursts | Yes, up to C | No (output smooth) |\n| Excess requests | Rejected | Queued, then dropped |\n| Adds latency | No | Yes |\n| Typical use | Public APIs | Shaping to downstream |\n\n## Fixed window counter\n\nCount requests per client in fixed clock windows: 12:00:00–12:00:59, 12:01:00–12:01:59, and so on.\n\n```pseudocode\n# Use a new counter for each clock minute.\nkey = \"rl:{user}:{minute}\"\ncount = INCR key\nif count == 1: EXPIRE key 60\nif count > limit: reject\n```\n\nIt's extremely cheap: one counter per client per window. But it has a **boundary problem**. With a limit of 100/min, a client can send 100 at 12:00:59 and another 100 at 12:01:00. That's 200 requests in two seconds, double the intended rate:\n\n```formula\nA_max = 2 × L\nA_max = 2 × 100 = 200\nwhere\nA_max: the most requests a fixed-window limiter can accept in a short span that straddles a window boundary\nL: the limit per window, here 100 per minute\nIn words: the end of one window and the start of the next each allow the full limit, so a client timing its bursts can get twice the limit through in a couple of seconds.\n```\n\n```\n limit 100/min\n   window 1    |   window 2\n ..........100 | 100..........\n          12:01:00\n       200 in ~2 s\n```\n\n## Key takeaways\n- A leaky queue smooths downstream traffic but adds waiting time and drops overflow.\n- A fixed-window counter needs little state but can admit two windows’ quota across a boundary.\n- NGINX nodelay changes queue behaviour by allowing immediate bursts while slots drain.\n\n## Further reading\n- [Cloudflare blog: How we built rate limiting capable of scaling to millions of domains](https://blog.cloudflare.com/counting-things-a-lot-of-different-things/)\n- [Redis: Rate limiting](https://redis.io/glossary/rate-limiting/)\n- [NGINX: Request limiting](https://nginx.org/en/docs/http/ngx_http_limit_req_module.html)\n","after":"---\nid: api-leaky-fixed-window\ntitle: Leaky buckets and fixed-window boundaries\nlevel: advanced\nminutes: 3\nsummary: Trade queueing delay for smooth output and recognise fixed-window boundary bursts.\nsourceLesson: api-rate-limiting\n---\n\n## Leaky bucket\n\nRequests enter a **queue** of fixed size, and are processed (they \"leak out\") at a constant rate. If the queue is full, new requests are dropped.\n\n```\n requests in (bursty)\n     | | |||  |\n     v v vvv  v\n   +---------+\n   | queue   |  size B\n   +---------+\n       |\n       v  constant rate r\n   to backend\n```\n\nThe worst-case extra delay is a full queue draining at the leak rate:\n\n```formula\nW_max = B / r\nW_max = 100 / 50 = 2 s\nwhere\nW_max: the longest a request can wait in the queue, in seconds (s)\nB: the queue size, in requests; here 100\nr: the leak rate, in requests per second; here 50\ns: seconds\nIn words: a request that joins the back of a full queue waits for every request ahead of it to leak out, so a 100-request queue at 50 per second adds up to 2 s.\n```\n\nThe output is perfectly smooth, which is great for protecting a fragile downstream (say, a legacy system that can only handle 50 req/s). The cost is latency: bursty requests wait in the queue instead of being served immediately. NGINX's `limit_req` is a leaky bucket; its `burst` parameter is the queue size. Add `nodelay` and queued requests are served immediately instead, while still using up queue slots that drain at the set rate, so the behaviour becomes much closer to a token bucket.\n\n| | Token bucket | Leaky bucket |\n|---|---|---|\n| Allows bursts | Yes, up to C | No (output smooth) |\n| Excess requests | Rejected | Queued, then dropped |\n| Adds latency | No | Yes |\n| Typical use | Public APIs | Shaping to downstream |\n\n## Fixed window counter\n\nCount requests per client in fixed clock windows: 12:00:00–12:00:59, 12:01:00–12:01:59, and so on.\n\nUse a Redis Lua script so incrementing and assigning the first expiry happen atomically. The application supplies `KEYS[1]`, built from the trusted client identity and current calendar minute, and a validated positive integer limit in `ARGV[1]`. The expiry is a cleanup period from the first request; a different minute gets a different key.\n\n```lua\n-- KEYS[1] includes user and clock minute.\nlocal limit = tonumber(ARGV[1])\nlocal count = redis.call('INCR', KEYS[1])\nif count == 1 then\n  -- Retain the counter through this minute.\n  redis.call('EXPIRE', KEYS[1], 60)\nend\n-- 1 means allowed; 0 means rejected.\nreturn count <= limit and 1 or 0\n```\n\nThe counter includes rejected attempts too, but only the first request sets its expiry. Redis runs the script without interleaving other commands. Trusted key construction, validated inputs and Redis failure policy are still application responsibilities.\n\nIt's extremely cheap: one counter per client per window. But it has a **boundary problem**. With a limit of 100/min, a client can send 100 at 12:00:59 and another 100 at 12:01:00. That's 200 requests in two seconds, double the intended rate:\n\n```formula\nA_max = 2 × L\nA_max = 2 × 100 = 200\nwhere\nA_max: the most requests a fixed-window limiter can accept in a short span that straddles a window boundary\nL: the limit per window, here 100 per minute\nIn words: the end of one window and the start of the next each allow the full limit, so a client timing its bursts can get twice the limit through in a couple of seconds.\n```\n\n```\n limit 100/min\n   window 1    |   window 2\n ..........100 | 100..........\n          12:01:00\n       200 in ~2 s\n```\n\n## Key takeaways\n- A leaky queue smooths downstream traffic but adds waiting time and drops overflow.\n- A fixed-window counter needs little state but can admit two windows’ quota across a boundary.\n- NGINX nodelay changes queue behaviour by allowing immediate bursts while slots drain.\n\n## Further reading\n- [Cloudflare blog: How we built rate limiting capable of scaling to millions of domains](https://blog.cloudflare.com/counting-things-a-lot-of-different-things/)\n- [Redis: Rate limiting](https://redis.io/glossary/rate-limiting/)\n- [NGINX: Request limiting](https://nginx.org/en/docs/http/ngx_http_limit_req_module.html)\n","reason":"Convert inherited policy pseudocode to actual executed Python/Lua with explicit fixture context and preserved teaching contracts"}
- {"file":"api-idempotency-key-contracts.questions.json","before":"[\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-idempotency-q2\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"A developer writes this retry loop for a payment call:\\n\\n```pseudocode\\nfor attempt in 1..3:\\n  # Each attempt gets a different intent\\n  # id.\\n  key = uuid4()\\n  resp = post(\\\"/payments\\\", body,\\n    headers={\\\"Idempotency-Key\\\": key})\\n  if resp.ok: break\\n```\\n\\nWhat's wrong?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Nothing; each attempt has an idempotency key\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Having *a* key isn't enough. The server deduplicates by key, so different keys on each attempt look like three separate payments.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"UUIDv4 isn't random enough for idempotency keys\",\n        \"explanation\": \"UUIDv4 has 122 random bits, which is plenty to avoid collisions. Randomness isn't the problem.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Idempotency keys can only be sent on GET requests\",\n        \"explanation\": \"It's the reverse: idempotency keys exist for non-idempotent methods like POST. GET is already idempotent.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"A new key is generated on every attempt, so if attempt 1 succeeded but its response was lost, attempt 2 creates a second payment\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. The key must represent the logical operation and be generated *once*, before the loop, then reused for every attempt.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 3,\n    \"workedExample\": \"Trace the failure case:\\n\\n1. Attempt 1, key K1 → server charges £50, response lost.\\n2. Attempt 2, key **K2** → server has never seen K2 → charges £50 again.\\n3. Customer charged £100.\\n\\nFix:\\n\\n```pseudocode\\n# One intent id shared by every attempt.\\nkey = uuid4()\\nfor attempt in 1..3:\\n  resp = post(..., key)\\n```\\n\\nNow attempt 2 carries K1 and the server replays the first result. If the app might crash between attempts, persist the key alongside the pending order.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"Stripe API: Idempotent requests\",\n      \"url\": \"https://docs.stripe.com/api/idempotent_requests\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-idempotency-q4\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"A client retries `POST /payments` with `Idempotency-Key: K` but, due to a bug, changes `amount` from 5000 to 7000 on the retry. The first request with K succeeded. Under Stripe API v1's documented contract, how does its idempotency layer respond?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"It returns an idempotency error, because the parameters don't match the original request made with that key\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. Stripe compares the incoming parameters with the original. Silently returning the old 5000 result would hide the client bug; charging 7000 would break the key's promise. Erroring makes the bug visible.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"It charges 7000, because the parameters are new\",\n        \"explanation\": \"That would defeat the purpose of the key: the client would get a second charge for what it claims is the same operation.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"It silently returns the original 5000 result\",\n        \"explanation\": \"That would hide a real bug: the client believes it charged 7000. Stripe deliberately errors instead.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"It refunds the first charge and charges 7000\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Idempotency layers never take compensating actions on their own. They either replay or reject.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 0,\n    \"workedExample\": \"The server stores, per key: `(request hash, status, response)`.\\n\\n1. Retry arrives with K.\\n2. Lookup finds K → stored hash = `hash(amount=5000, ...)`.\\n3. Incoming hash = `hash(amount=7000, ...)` → **mismatch**.\\n4. Return an error (Stripe uses an `idempotency_error`; the IETF draft suggests `422`).\\n\\nThis is why implementations store a hash of the request body, not just the key.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"Stripe API: Idempotent requests\",\n      \"url\": \"https://docs.stripe.com/api/idempotent_requests\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-idempotency-q8\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"Your API deliberately adopts the expired IETF Idempotency-Key draft’s proposed 409/422 convention. Two identical payment requests share a key; the second arrives while the first is in progress. Which response follows this convention?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"`201 Created` immediately, copied from the first request\",\n        \"explanation\": \"There's nothing to copy yet: the first request hasn't finished, so no result has been stored.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"`422`, because the key has been used already\",\n        \"explanation\": \"In the draft, 422 is for reusing a key with a *different* request body. Here the body is identical; the problem is timing, not a mismatch.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"`409 Conflict`, meaning 'a request with this key is in progress'; the client backs off and retries with the same key, and then gets the stored result replayed\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. The 409 isn't stored as the key's result, so a later retry with the same key sees the finished first request and gets its response.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Run the second request too; the card network will deduplicate\",\n        \"explanation\": \"You can't assume every downstream deduplicates, and it would defeat the point of claiming the key atomically. The unique-key insert should already have made the second request lose the race.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 2,\n    \"workedExample\": \"Follow the claim-first algorithm:\\n\\n1. Request 1 inserts `(account, K, status='started')`. Succeeds.\\n2. Request 2 tries the same insert 50 ms later. Unique constraint conflict.\\n3. Request 2 reads the existing row: same body hash, status `started`. Return **409**.\\n4. Request 1 finishes: status `done`, code 201, body stored.\\n5. The client retries request 2 after a short backoff. The row is now `done`, so it gets the **replayed 201**.\\n\\nResult: one charge, and both taps end with the same success response.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"IETF draft: The Idempotency-Key HTTP header field\",\n      \"url\": \"https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-ietf-httpapi-idempotency-key-header/\"\n    }\n  }\n]\n","after":"[\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-idempotency-q2\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"A developer writes this Python retry function for a payment call. The supplied `post` callback returns an object whose `ok` attribute reports known success; this teaching fixture reports unsuccessful or unknown attempts as `ok=False`.\\n\\n```python\\nfrom uuid import uuid4\\n\\ndef buggy_retry(post, body):\\n    for attempt in range(1, 4):\\n        # Fresh attempt key: the bug.\\n        key = str(uuid4())\\n        headers = {\\n            \\\"Idempotency-Key\\\": key\\n        }\\n        resp = post(\\\"/payments\\\", body,\\n                    headers=headers)\\n        if resp.ok:\\n            return resp\\n    return resp\\n```\\n\\nWhat is the idempotency bug? This example isolates key placement; real clients also need timeout handling, retryability checks, backoff and a supported server contract.\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Nothing; each attempt has an idempotency key\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Having *a* key isn't enough. The server deduplicates by key, so different keys on each attempt look like three separate payments.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"UUIDv4 isn't random enough for idempotency keys\",\n        \"explanation\": \"UUIDv4 has 122 random bits, which is plenty to avoid collisions. Randomness isn't the problem.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Idempotency keys can only be sent on GET requests\",\n        \"explanation\": \"It's the reverse: idempotency keys exist for non-idempotent methods like POST. GET is already idempotent.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"A new key is generated on every attempt, so if attempt 1 succeeded but its response was lost, attempt 2 creates a second payment\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. The key must represent the logical operation and be generated *once*, before the loop, then reused for every attempt.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 3,\n    \"workedExample\": \"Trace the failure case under a server contract that deduplicates by key:\\n\\n1. Attempt 1, key K1 → server charges £50, response lost.\\n2. Attempt 2, key **K2** → server has never seen K2 → charges £50 again.\\n3. Customer charged £100.\\n\\nMove key creation outside the loop:\\n\\n```python\\nfrom uuid import uuid4\\n\\ndef retry_one_intent(post, body):\\n    # One key for the logical operation.\\n    key = str(uuid4())\\n    headers = {\\\"Idempotency-Key\\\": key}\\n    for attempt in range(1, 4):\\n        resp = post(\\\"/payments\\\", body,\\n                    headers=headers)\\n        if resp.ok:\\n            return resp\\n    return resp\\n```\\n\\nIn a fixture where the first two results are unknown and the third reports success, the buggy function makes three calls with three distinct UUID strings; the corrected function makes three calls with one shared UUID string. UUID values are random, so no specific value is claimed. The same-key retry prevents a second effect only under the endpoint’s documented scope and retention contract. Persist the key alongside the pending order if the app can restart; real HTTP timeouts and error policies still need handling.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"Stripe API: Idempotent requests\",\n      \"url\": \"https://docs.stripe.com/api/idempotent_requests\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-idempotency-q4\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"A client retries `POST /payments` with `Idempotency-Key: K` but, due to a bug, changes `amount` from 5000 to 7000 on the retry. The first request with K succeeded. Under Stripe API v1's documented contract, how does its idempotency layer respond?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"It returns an idempotency error, because the parameters don't match the original request made with that key\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. Stripe compares the incoming parameters with the original. Silently returning the old 5000 result would hide the client bug; charging 7000 would break the key's promise. Erroring makes the bug visible.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"It charges 7000, because the parameters are new\",\n        \"explanation\": \"That would defeat the purpose of the key: the client would get a second charge for what it claims is the same operation.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"It silently returns the original 5000 result\",\n        \"explanation\": \"That would hide a real bug: the client believes it charged 7000. Stripe deliberately errors instead.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"It refunds the first charge and charges 7000\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Idempotency layers never take compensating actions on their own. They either replay or reject.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 0,\n    \"workedExample\": \"The server stores, per key: `(request hash, status, response)`.\\n\\n1. Retry arrives with K.\\n2. Lookup finds K → stored hash = `hash(amount=5000, ...)`.\\n3. Incoming hash = `hash(amount=7000, ...)` → **mismatch**.\\n4. Return an error (Stripe uses an `idempotency_error`; the IETF draft suggests `422`).\\n\\nThis is why implementations store a hash of the request body, not just the key.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"Stripe API: Idempotent requests\",\n      \"url\": \"https://docs.stripe.com/api/idempotent_requests\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-idempotency-q8\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"Your API deliberately adopts the expired IETF Idempotency-Key draft’s proposed 409/422 convention. Two identical payment requests share a key; the second arrives while the first is in progress. Which response follows this convention?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"`201 Created` immediately, copied from the first request\",\n        \"explanation\": \"There's nothing to copy yet: the first request hasn't finished, so no result has been stored.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"`422`, because the key has been used already\",\n        \"explanation\": \"In the draft, 422 is for reusing a key with a *different* request body. Here the body is identical; the problem is timing, not a mismatch.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"`409 Conflict`, meaning 'a request with this key is in progress'; the client backs off and retries with the same key, and then gets the stored result replayed\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. The 409 isn't stored as the key's result, so a later retry with the same key sees the finished first request and gets its response.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Run the second request too; the card network will deduplicate\",\n        \"explanation\": \"You can't assume every downstream deduplicates, and it would defeat the point of claiming the key atomically. The unique-key insert should already have made the second request lose the race.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 2,\n    \"workedExample\": \"Follow the claim-first algorithm:\\n\\n1. Request 1 inserts `(account, K, status='started')`. Succeeds.\\n2. Request 2 tries the same insert 50 ms later. Unique constraint conflict.\\n3. Request 2 reads the existing row: same body hash, status `started`. Return **409**.\\n4. Request 1 finishes: status `done`, code 201, body stored.\\n5. The client retries request 2 after a short backoff. The row is now `done`, so it gets the **replayed 201**.\\n\\nResult: one charge, and both taps end with the same success response.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"IETF draft: The Idempotency-Key HTTP header field\",\n      \"url\": \"https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-ietf-httpapi-idempotency-key-header/\"\n    }\n  }\n]\n","reason":"Convert inherited policy pseudocode to actual executed Python/Lua with explicit fixture context and preserved teaching contracts"}
- {"file":"api-webhook-signatures-replay.questions.json","before":"[\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-webhooks-gateways-q1\",\n    \"level\": \"basic\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"Your `/webhooks/payments` endpoint marks orders as paid when it receives a `payment.succeeded` event. It doesn't verify signatures. What's the main risk?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Events might arrive slowly\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Latency is a reliability concern, not the main risk here. The bigger problem is that the endpoint trusts anything it receives.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"The provider will stop sending events\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Providers don't know or care whether you verify signatures; deliveries continue either way.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Duplicate events will double-mark orders\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Duplicates are a real concern, but they're solved by deduplicating on event id, not by signatures. And marking an order paid twice is usually harmless.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Anyone who discovers the URL can POST a forged event and get orders marked as paid without paying\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. Without the provider's required authentication mechanism, this public endpoint cannot distinguish the forged request. HMAC is one common mechanism; other authenticated delivery schemes exist.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 3,\n    \"workedExample\": \"Think like an attacker:\\n\\n1. Place an order, don't pay.\\n2. Guess or find the webhook URL (it's often `/webhooks/stripe` or appears in client-side code or docs).\\n3. `curl -X POST` a JSON body shaped like a real `payment.succeeded` event with your order id.\\n4. Unverified endpoint marks your order paid → free goods.\\n\\nWith verification, step 3 fails, because the signature the provider sends is:\\n\\n```formula\\ns = HMAC(k, t + \\\".\\\" + b)\\nwhere\\ns: the signature in the webhook header\\nHMAC: HMAC-SHA256, a keyed hash that only someone holding k can compute\\nk: the shared webhook secret\\nt: the delivery timestamp\\nb: the raw request body\\n+: joined end to end (concatenation), not addition\\nIn words: the signature is a keyed fingerprint of the timestamp and body, and without the secret the attacker cannot produce one that matches.\\n```\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"Stripe docs: Verify webhook signatures\",\n      \"url\": \"https://docs.stripe.com/webhooks/signature\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-webhooks-gateways-q2\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"Your signature verification fails on every genuine webhook, even though the secret is correct. The code is:\\n\\n```pseudocode\\n# Parsed JSON loses the exact signed\\n# bytes.\\nbody = json.loads(request.data)\\npayload = json.dumps(body)\\nexpected = hmac(secret,\\n  f\\\"{ts}.{payload}\\\")\\n```\\n\\nWhat's the most likely bug?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"HMAC-SHA256 can't sign JSON\",\n        \"explanation\": \"HMAC signs arbitrary bytes. JSON is fine, as long as you sign exactly the bytes that were sent.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"The body is parsed and re-serialised, so whitespace, key order or escaping differ from the raw bytes the provider signed\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. Even one changed space produces a completely different HMAC. Always verify against the raw request body before any parsing.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"The timestamp should come after the body\",\n        \"explanation\": \"The order is set by the provider's spec. Stripe uses `timestamp.body`, which this code follows. The problem is the body bytes themselves.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"The secret must be base64-decoded first\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Some providers (Standard Webhooks) do use base64 secrets, but the question says the secret is correct. The re-serialisation bug would break verification regardless.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 1,\n    \"workedExample\": \"Compare bytes:\\n\\n```\\nsent:   {\\\"id\\\": \\\"evt_1\\\",  \\\"amt\\\":5}\\nre-ser: {\\\"id\\\": \\\"evt_1\\\", \\\"amt\\\": 5}\\n```\\n\\nRe-serialisation changes the signed byte sequence, so the expected HMAC will no longer match (apart from cryptographically negligible collisions). The snippet below is pseudocode: use the provider SDK to parse timestamp/signature encodings and verify freshness.\\n\\nFix:\\n\\n```pseudocode\\n# Verify bytes before parsing the\\n# payload.\\nraw = request.get_data()  # bytes\\nexpected = hmac(secret,\\n  ts + b\\\".\\\" + raw)\\n# Compare the provider-decoded\\n# signatures.\\nif not compare_digest(expected, sig):\\n    return 400\\nbody = json.loads(raw)\\n```\\n\\nNote the constant-time comparison too.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"GitHub docs: Validating webhook deliveries\",\n      \"url\": \"https://docs.github.com/en/webhooks/using-webhooks/validating-webhook-deliveries\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-webhooks-gateways-q3\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"An attacker captures a genuine, correctly signed `refund.created` webhook from a misconfigured log and resends it to your endpoint 2 hours later. Your code verifies the HMAC over `timestamp.body` and allows a 5-minute tolerance. What happens?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"It's rejected, because the signed timestamp is 2 hours old, beyond the 5-minute tolerance, and the attacker can't change the timestamp without invalidating the signature\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. Because the timestamp is part of the signed string, the attacker must either send the old timestamp (rejected as stale) or a new one (signature mismatch).\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"It's accepted, because the signature is valid\",\n        \"explanation\": \"The signature is valid only for that exact timestamp. The tolerance check is what makes a valid-but-old signature useless.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"It's accepted, but only if the attacker updates the timestamp header\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Updating the timestamp changes the signed string, so the recomputed HMAC no longer matches the captured signature.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"It depends on whether the event id was already processed; timestamps don't help\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Deduplication by event id is a second, complementary defence, but the timestamp check alone already rejects this replay.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 0,\n    \"workedExample\": \"Attacker's two options:\\n\\n| Send | HMAC check | Age check |\\n|---|---|---|\\n| Old `t` | ✔ match | ✘ 2 h > 5 min |\\n| New `t` | ✘ mismatch | (n/a) |\\n\\nEither way, rejected.\\n\\nWithin the 5-minute window a replay could still pass the timestamp check, which is why you also dedupe on the event id: belt and braces.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"Stripe docs: Verify webhook signatures\",\n      \"url\": \"https://docs.stripe.com/webhooks/signature\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-webhooks-gateways-q8\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"You consume GitHub webhooks and verify `X-Hub-Signature-256` correctly. What's your main defence?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Reject deliveries older than 5 minutes using the `Date` header\",\n        \"explanation\": \"The `Date` header isn't part of the signature, so the attacker can set it to anything. A replayed copy has the same valid signature, which is exactly why replays are dangerous.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Rotate the webhook secret every day\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Rotation limits how long one captured delivery stays valid, but it's operationally painful and still leaves a window. It isn't the primary defence.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Use authenticated payload identity or a retained digest of verified raw bytes for replay detection, and make business effects idempotent; the unsigned delivery header alone is insufficient\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. A captured body remains authentically signed. Retained authenticated identity can detect repeats; reconcile current provider state for stale events. The attacker can replace X-GitHub-Delivery without invalidating the body HMAC.\"\n      }\n    ],\n    \"answer\": 3,\n    \"workedExample\": \"1. Replay protection needs something the attacker can't change: either a **signed** timestamp or a record of what you've already seen.\\n2. GitHub signs only the body, so any timestamp in a header is unprotected.\\n3. That leaves **deduplication**: retain authenticated event identity or the verified body digest where suitable; the unsigned delivery id only helps ordinary redelivery deduplication.\\n4. An attacker could change the unsigned delivery id, so also make processing idempotent on the *content*, e.g. define a business key including the repository, ref and commit for the intended effect, so an unrelated repository or legitimate new operation is not accidentally suppressed.\\n\\nCompare with Stripe and Standard Webhooks, which put the timestamp inside the signed string, so stale replays are rejected outright.\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"GitHub docs: Validating webhook deliveries\",\n      \"url\": \"https://docs.github.com/en/webhooks/using-webhooks/validating-webhook-deliveries\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-webhooks-gateways-q12\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"You need to rotate a webhook signing secret. How do you avoid rejecting genuine webhooks during the changeover?\",\n    \"options\": [\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Turn off signature verification for the rotation window\",\n        \"explanation\": \"That opens exactly the window an attacker would want, and there's no need for it.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Have the provider sign each delivery with both old and new secrets during an overlap period, and have consumers accept a delivery if any signature matches a secret they hold\",\n        \"explanation\": \"Correct. Consumers can roll forward one at a time, then the old secret is retired.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Return 500 for failed signatures, so the provider retries until the new secret is everywhere\",\n        \"explanation\": \"That relies on retry timing to paper over the gap, delays every event, and may exhaust the retry schedule. Overlapping secrets avoid the failures in the first place.\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"text\": \"Encrypt the old secret with the new one\",\n        \"explanation\": \"That has no effect on verification. Unverified endpoint marks your order paid → free goods.\\n\\nWith verification, step 3 fails, because the signature the provider sends is:\\n\\n```formula\\ns = HMAC(k, t + \\\".\\\" + b)\\nwhere\\ns: the signature in the webhook header\\nHMAC: HMAC-SHA256, a keyed hash that only someone holding k can compute\\nk: the shared webhook secret\\nt: the delivery timestamp\\nb: the raw request body\\n+: joined end to end (concatenation), not addition\\nIn words: the signature is a keyed fingerprint of the timestamp and body, and without the secret the attacker cannot produce one that matches.\\n```\",\n    \"link\": {\n      \"title\": \"Stripe docs: Verify webhook signatures\",\n      \"url\": \"https://docs.stripe.com/webhooks/signature\"\n    }\n  },\n  {\n    \"id\": \"api-webhooks-gateways-q2\",\n    \"level\": \"intermediate\",\n    \"type\": \"single\",\n    \"prompt\": \"Your Stripe-style `timestamp.body` signature check fails on genuine deliveries even though the secret is correct. - **medium** — glossary: api-version-change-module historical vendor architecture. Verified against Stripe's 2017 publication, not a current production source-code audit.
- **medium** — api-webhook-signatures-replay: New HMAC parsing helper demonstrates signature bytes only. Matching/tampered bytes execute as taught; the fixture also accepts a correctly signed old timestamp. Provider SDK header decoding, secret selection, freshness, replay detection and business deduplication remain required and are not implemented or production-certified.

## dsa: Complexity Analysis

[Independent verification record](https://github.com/AlfieGormley/cs-study-app/blob/1a0ad7d64e57cd2692c9eb1490c0f44dd0745720/content/dsa/01-complexity/verification.json)

A scoped follow-up is underway. This section describes the previously published version.

Corrections and qualifications in this conversion:

- cx-growth-rates-q7: the exact n//2 inner count used n/2 even for odd n. Replaced it with floor(n/2), defined floor, and kept the quadratic approximation and answer key.
- cx-input-size: the pair-count scaling chain ended with an exact equality to4C(n), although lower-order terms had been discarded. Replaced that equality with an approximation.
- cx-in-place-memory and cx-space-complexity-q9: a frame-count bound omitted the initial frame. Use floor(log_2(n))+1 for positive n, including the initial call; asymptotic bound and answer stay O(log n).
- cx-recursion-trees: its new introductory bounded-positive-base-case wording did not justify the retained exact level sums and2n² upper bound. Explicitly use powers of two and base-case cost one for exact examples; fixed positive alternatives have the same asymptotic bounds.
- cx-recurrences-q12: the internal-level arithmetic sum was stated as exact total running time despite omitting a linear leaf cost. Marked the sum approximate and explained the excluded fixed-base-case leaf cost; answer remains Theta(n log²n).
- Corrected subject/verb agreement in four rewritten cross-references after singular long lessons became plural short lessons.

Remaining gaps:

- Universal cross-language speed ratios and memory latency figures lack reproducible measurements and are explicitly omitted.
- No benchmark-derived crossover recommendation for a particular deployment.
- Code execution uses small representative inputs and explicit harness assumptions; it is not a proof for arbitrary inputs.
- Formula lint retains inherited prose/table mathematics alongside valid Big-O and real-code exceptions. Its542 active heuristic hits are not represented as zero findings or all as Rule6 exceptions; this split/comment scope did not rewrite the existing formula presentation everywhere.
- The35 units preserve the depth of the seven originals. Two naturally small units retain honest two-minute estimates;33 units use three-to-five-minute estimates. Some focused units have one question rather than padding them with unrelated questions.

Low-confidence areas:

- **medium** — cx-growth-rates: Glossary term 'Total problem' (used in the lesson's definition of an algorithm). retrofit: 'total problem' is not a standard textbook term; the definition is a reasonable reading by analogy with total vs partial functions (Wikipedia: Partial function), but no primary algorithms text defines it this way.
- **medium** — cx-space-complexity: Glossary alias 'two pointers' now resolves DSA-wide to the general two-pointer-technique entry (overriding dsa/02's fast/slow entry). retrofit: the definition now covers both opposite-ends and same-direction (fast/slow) variants, so dsa/02 and dsa/09 uses read correctly; but several DSA lessons use 'two pointers' literally (e.g. 'one or two pointers plus allocator bookkeeping' in dsa/02, 'only two pointers are stored' in dsa/04), which will also link. Those modules are outside this verifier's scope.
- **medium** — cx-growth-rates: Glossary 'Exponential time': practical only up to about n = 20 to 40 for 2^n. retrofit: an order-of-magnitude rule of thumb consistent with the lesson's 10^8 ops/s model (2^25 to 2^35 steps), not a sourced figure.
- **medium** — cx-counting-work: Inherited nonstandard total-problem glossary terminology. Core algorithm properties are primary-source checked. The unchanged glossary term still lacks a primary algorithms textbook definition, as the inherited record honestly states.
- **medium** — cx-recursive-hidden-work: Simplified costs for CPython operations. Current implementation is fetched and examples run. Counts assume bounded-value or constant-cost comparisons/hash operations and describe this cost model; arbitrary user objects or large integers require separate analysis.
- **medium** — cx-recursion-memory: CPython recursion limit and atomic-number space model. Current source defines1000 and the independent local Python3.14.4 run observed1000. Builds and user configuration can differ; whole-number bit storage is explicitly treated separately.
- **medium** — cx-in-place-memory: Implementation-specific list-sort auxiliary allocation. Current CPython listsort primary source supports up to half-list temporary pointers; it is an implementation statement, not a permanent Python-language guarantee.
- **medium** — cx-memory-tradeoffs: Typical64-bit CPython object sizes and packed-buffer memory budgets. The text is qualified and asks the reader to measure their build. This run observes28-byte int(1); 80/360MB arithmetic is recomputed, but allocator, value magnitude and construction overhead depend on the deployment.
- **medium** — cx-resizing-policies: CPython/OpenJDK/libstdc++ growth factors. Fetched current implementation sources support the qualified factors. These are version-sensitive implementation details; Rust documents no particular factor guarantee.
- **medium** — cx-amortised-latency: Resize latency and Redis incremental-rehash implementation. The data movement model and current Redis source are checked. The module does not claim a reproducible latency distribution or promise that every incremental step has a fixed wall-clock bound.
- **medium** — cx-constants-locality: Practical performance ratios, sorting/multiplication crossover and cache-line assumptions. Algorithm and implementation structure are source-checked; the preserved qualitative several-times performance language has no reproducible target-specific benchmark.64-byte lines and8-byte elements are explicit illustrative assumptions where calculations depend on them.
- **medium** — cx-measuring-algorithms: Historical Knuth quotation and deployment-specific timing interpretation. timeit measurement guidance is fetched and ratios recomputed. The original quotation attribution is retained but its primary paper was inaccessible through the web tool; the citation is not upgraded to high confidence.

## synth: Sound & Digital Audio

[Independent verification record](../content/synth/01-sound-sampling/verification.json)

Corrections and qualifications in this conversion:

- Moved snd-waves-q8 from wave measurements to sample-phase increment so its sampled-sine and samples-per-cycle prerequisites precede it; original ID and key retained.
- Added a local changing-frequency/absolute-time explanation and formula to wrapped-phase state before snd-waves-q11; its 44,880 Hz result independently recomputed.
- Phase preservation avoids an output-value jump but does not guarantee no transient; abrupt slope changes and finite rounding now explained in lesson and questions.
- Float headroom avoids clipping but is not lossless arithmetic; normal rounding still applies.
- Full relative float precision restricted to the normal range; subnormal precision differs.
- Digital full scale distinguished from the representable float range in dBFS prose, formula definition and question explanation.
- TPDF output error has input-independent mean and variance; removed the stronger claim that every property of nonsubtractive total error is statistically independent.
- Specified common TPDF dither as two quantisation steps peak-to-peak, including the moved dither question.
- Distinguished formal SINAD-based ENOB from the question’s explicitly requested noise-only estimate from dynamic range; added verified SINAD glossary definition.
- Separated BLIT impulse-train integration from BLEP/PolyBLEP step correction.
- FM described as generating sidebands, which need not be harmonics.
- Oversampling decimation filtering removes out-of-passband content; in-band aliases created at the oversampled rate survive.
- High-rate hard-clipping experiment greatly reduces alias contamination; finite rate cannot remove the infinite harmonic tail.
- Odd-only symmetric clipping statement restricted to a sine or other input with half-wave symmetry.
- Added DC/Nyquist endpoint exceptions to the first FFT scaling description, and excluded these bins explicitly in the amplitude question.
- Rectangular window allowed for coherent whole-cycle captures; removed universal “always window” instruction.
- Added local zero-padding and scalloping/flat-top/interpolation context before FFT-bin and leakage questions.
- DC question now specifies whole cycles of a steady waveform; lesson distinguishes partial-cycle mean from genuine DC offset.
- Full-scale sample counts labelled as warnings for investigation, not proof that clipping happened, in WAV checklist and moved questions.
- Eight-bit nearest-rounding question now explicitly excludes endpoint overload, retaining the same key.
- Block-budget question now asks about meeting each block’s deadline, rather than an average which could conceal overruns.
- Quantiser trace table reduced from five to four columns with all intermediate round/clip values retained.
- Rejected new bare fixed-point alias because current hyphenated fallback could capture mathematical fixed points in other subjects; retained precise arithmetic aliases.
- Rejected new bare phase alias because it would capture envelope “release phase” in another Synth module; used existing precise wave-phase phrase locally.
- Fractional/negative powers no longer described as fractional numbers of repeated copies; roots and reciprocals made explicit.
- Replaced obsolete original lesson-number cross-references with relevant short-unit names.

Remaining gaps:

- No original course topic removed: 68 original questions/keys, 51 original lesson equations, 20 Python lesson examples, three diagrams and two flow diagrams retained; one new sine question and one local glide formula added.
- The 295 existing glossary definitions and confidence records are inherited from prior independent verification, not claimed as a fresh exhaustive definition audit. Real source limitations are retained; ENOB/JND/phase-jump/peak-level shorthand is explicitly flagged above.
- Primary sources, formulas, all question keys/distractors and all code reviewed; claimsChecked counts 220 overlapping review units (32 lesson claim groups + 112 formula blocks + 69 question reviews + six requested alias-entry reviews + one new glossary definition), not 220 independently enumerated atomic claims.
- Offline examples and exact outputs were tested, including PCM conversion boundaries. Hardware, audible quality, embedded callback timing, board noise and processor-specific slow paths were not measured.
- BLIT/BLEP, oversampling filters and true-peak reconstruction are conceptual here; implementation depth remains in the later synthesis/audio-engineering modules.
- Repository-wide formula/glossary scans also enumerate preserved archival files; their unchanged old lint forms are out of the active short-lesson editing scope. All active formula-lint hits are real inline program code permitted by Rule 6.
- Complete app build, all-app tests and 390 px browser rendering are assigned to the coordinating session; this verifier does not modify generated data or shared compiler/app files.
- AES Pro Audio Reference was fetched with the web browser for evidence, but the final Node accessibility check returned HTTP 403. Its newly added Further reading link was removed; evidence URL remains recorded as a browser-fetched source.

Low-confidence areas:

- **medium** — glossary: coherent-sampling. No dedicated authoritative page fetched (Analog Devices article timed out); source is the Spectral leakage article
- **medium** — glossary: note-space. Course-specific term; Pitch space article supports the linear MIDI pitch-space idea
- **medium** — glossary: crest-factor. "12–20 dB for real music" is a broad typical range that varies by genre and mastering
- **medium** — glossary: zipper-noise. Source (Smoothing) is generic; definition is standard practice
- **medium** — glossary: parameter-smoothing. Source (Smoothing) is generic; definition is standard practice
- **medium** — glossary: offline-render. Source is a computer-graphics rendering article; meaning is standard audio usage
- **medium** — glossary: correlated-signals. Source (Coherence) is loose; power-addition of uncorrelated signals is standard
- **medium** — glossary: power-signal. Source (Power (physics)) is loose; mean-square definition is standard
- **medium** — glossary: vibrato. Example settings are a typical choice, not a standard
- **medium** — glossary: monitor-level. Alias "studio monitors" names the speakers rather than the level
- **medium** — glossary: audio-block. Course usage; PortAudio callback docs describe frames per buffer
- **medium** — glossary: ableton-live. Existing medium-confidence glossary record preserved. Current manufacturer manual documents C-2 to G8 full MIDI range, from which note 60 = C3 is inferred; product labels remain version-sensitive.
- **medium** — glossary: enob. Existing definition and original confidence retained unchanged as requested. Its SNR/dynamic-range shortcut describes noise-only resolution; formal ENOB uses SINAD. Active lesson/question explicitly distinguish this.
- **medium** — glossary: just-noticeable-difference. Existing definition and original confidence retained unchanged. Its 3 Hz/0.6% examples come from a secondary summary; primary research supports condition dependence, not a universal threshold.
- **medium** — glossary: phase-jump and peak-level. Existing definitions and confidences preserved. They simplify discontinuity and clipping conditions; active lessons now specify possible specially timed continuous phase changes and sample-peak versus reconstructed-peak limits.
- **medium** — snd-cents-beating: Pitch discrimination thresholds and fast-beat perceptual boundaries. Research and UNSW demonstrations support dependence on stimulus/task/listener; no universal numerical threshold established. Lesson retains only explicitly hedged secondary examples.
- **medium** — snd-midi-frequency: Ableton note-60 display label. C3 is inferred from the current manual’s full MIDI range C-2 to G8; future versions/products may use other octave labels.
- **medium** — snd-float-integer-boundary: Target-processor subnormal handling cost. Primary Intel documentation and older x86 measurements support variable penalties. No benchmark on the reader’s embedded target was performed; fixed-cycle claim removed.
- **medium** — snd-dither-converter-noise: Finished-board dynamic range and noise-only ENOB estimate. Chip figures verified with datasheet configurations and A-weighting; finished-board noise and SINAD were not measured. Dynamic-range substitution remains an explicitly approximate noise-only estimate.
- **medium** — snd-peak-rms-dbfs: AES17 convention and typical music crest-factor ranges. AES authoritative reference and indexed Audio Precision primary-manual excerpt support the conventions; full normative AES17 text not obtained. Music range is programme- and averaging-dependent and is hedged.
- **medium** — snd-wav-write-readback: PCM flooring/clipping and library conversion behaviour. Confirmed in independent experiments with soundfile 0.14.0/libsndfile 1.2.2 and inspected versioned source. Behaviour can depend on library/platform/rounding configuration; text ties it to this runtime.
